#5821. The Model Middle Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW YEO is a Senior Fellow and SK-Korea Foundation Chair at the Brookings Institution and Professor of Politics at the Catholic University of America. This essay is adapted from his forthcoming book South Korea’s Grand Strategy: Narratives and the Rise of a Global Nation.

What South Korea Gets Right About Geopolitics

In the ten months since Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney gave his widely discussed speech at the World Economic Forum in Davos, the question of whether, and how, the world’s middle powers can help rebuild a broken international order has become increasingly urgent. In his remarks, Carney stressed that although the old U.S.-led alliance system was collapsing, middle powers—states that do not have the ability to reshape global politics but that still possess substantial might—were well positioned to pick up the slack. But to do so, Carney argued, they would need to build up their “strength at home” and “act together” to harness their collective power, even in the absence of backing from the world’s hegemon.

Although the Canadian prime minister’s bold vision was widely applauded, some experts thought his agenda was overly ambitious. They noted, correctly, that it would be very difficult for middle powers to build a grand alliance composed of countries as diverse as Brazil, Canada, South Africa, and Turkey. “The path to unity among middle powers is fraught because this group includes rich and poor countries, as well as small and large ones,” wrote the economist Edwar Prasad in February. Carney, however, is right that middle powers have an opportunity to thrive going forward. Even when aligning with great powers, these countries can still exercise greater autonomy by developing national capacity and diversifying external partnerships.

South Korea offers a prime example of this flexible approach. For decades, Seoul has pursued a middle-power strategy centered on building domestic strength and forging partnerships with democracies everywhere while deepening its alliance with Washington. South Korea has been adaptable, recalibrating its security alignments, economic strategies, and diplomatic roles as regional and global conditions require. This has enabled a country smaller in size than most of the world’s states to punch far above its weight, exercising substantial influence in Asia and beyond.

Not every government can follow Seoul’s path, and South Korea’s strategy has its limits. As Seoul itself acknowledges, middle powers cannot escape great-power competition. But the country has begun to show what middle powers can accomplish when they leverage their partnerships and their own strength to protect their interests and deter larger threats. And by maintaining and even building its ties to the United States while asserting greater autonomy, South Korea has proven that middle powers can strengthen themselves without building whole new networks—contrary to Carney’s suggestion.

According to classic international relations theory, grand strategy is the exclusive provenance of great powers. The United States’s effort to contain the Soviet Union during the Cold War is a paradigmatic example; China’s pursuit of global economic primacy is another. Middle powers, by contrast, have little say in such strategies. Instead, these countries are seen as primarily reactive in their foreign policies, navigating around the decisions of the great powers rather than setting the agenda themselves.

But recent history suggests that the division between great and middle powers is less clear-cut than has been assumed. In practice, middle powers very much have their own affirmative visions that they pursue in the world. They have the leeway to decide which threats matter most to them, which partnerships to prioritize, and where to invest scarce resources. Japan, concerned with the threat posed by China, has been quietly exercising leadership in the Indo-Pacific by improving its ties with neighbors, increasing investment and aid to the region, and strengthening its own defense capabilities—all while remaining closely allied with the United States. India, worried about China, Pakistan, and growing economic headwinds, has successfully bolstered partnerships with the United States and other democracies but maintained its relationship with Russia.

Much like Japan and India, South Korea is worried about its neighbors. But for Seoul, this is nothing new. South Korean leaders have been concerned with the very thing Carney fretted about in his Davos speech—becoming collateral damage in conflicts between great powers— since at least the late nineteenth century, when it was caught between China, Japan, and Russia. The country has a well-known proverb, “When whales fight, the shrimp's back is broken,” that captures the sentiment that great-power feuds can harm countries caught in the middle.

To safeguard against this threat, Seoul has long sought to increase its self-reliance through industrial policy. The country transited from light to heavy export industries in the 1970s, turning it into a major player in the chemical, steel, and shipbuilding sectors. State investments helped it become a major global producer of batteries and semiconductors in the present era. South Korea takes great pride in what historians call “the miracle on the Han”—or the country's rapid rise from a war-torn and impoverished state to a rich one during the late twentieth century. They now use the state’s wealth to promote public goods globally, contributing to international development, global health, climate policy, and multilateral bodies.

Seoul has also built up a formidable military, one that experts consistently rank as among the strongest in the world. Today, the country is considering developing a nuclear arsenal for self-defense, even though South Korea officially remains under Washington’s nuclear umbrella. The state has simultaneously used its armed prowess to deepen relations with U.S. allies. Seoul, for example, has inked multibillion-dollar arms deals with European buyers, making it a key partner in rebuilding NATO’s industrial capacity. In 2025, it was the second largest supplier of weapons to European NATO members (after the United States). South Korea has also forged defense partnerships in the Middle East and Southeast Asia, widening Seoul's options and reducing its dependence on any single market. South Korea, in other words, has been able to convert its economic resources and bureaucratic capabilities into greater influence by expanding its partnerships, strengthening its voice in international bodies, and helping shape global rules and norms.

But even as it charts its own path, Seoul has no plans to abandon the United States, at least in the near future. In fact, the sanctity of the U.S.-South Korean relationship is a significant historical Korean narrative. Many South Koreans see the alliance as a bond inked in blood, thanks to the sacrifice both countries made during the Korean War, when they worked to stop North Korea, backed by China and the Soviet Union, from overrunning the south. Most South Koreans correctly believe that the continued U.S. military presence in the country—currently, 28,500 American troops are stationed on the peninsula—has played a major part in deterring North Korean aggression over the past seven decades.

At first glance, South Korea’s strategy may seem contradictory. On the one hand, the country wants to deepen its cooperation with the United States, on which it still depends for security. But on the other, it seeks greater autonomy.

Yet these twin goals are not actually at odds. By cultivating ties with Washington, Seoul has gained access to advanced military capabilities and technologies as well as a larger diplomatic platform to expand its regional and global interests. It is in part because of this relationship, for example, that South Korea has had such success at selling weapons to multiple NATO members, including Croatia, Estonia, Finland, Norway, Poland, and Romania. (Seoul has also built up a formidable diplomatic apparatus in its own right.) At the same time, South Korea helps make this compact worthwhile for the United States by providing it with valuable technology, including semiconductor chips. Seoul has also partnered with the Washington to help revive the American shipbuilding industry, agreeing to invest $150 billion in the U.S. sector.

Still, South Korea’s growing push for more independence in its foreign policy does result in some tensions with the United States that cannot easily be resolved. For instance, the current government’s push to give Seoul, not Washington, operational control over both states’ forces on the peninsula in the event of war has been rebuffed by the U.S. military. American commanders instead favor a conditions-based transfer after South Korea has proven it has the capabilities to lead a joint defense, rather than setting a fixed date. The two sides also have not reached a consensus on what role South Korea would play if Taiwan is attacked or blockaded by China.

Yet so far, Seoul has done an effective job at managing these differences. For example, in his second term, U.S. President Donald Trump has repeatedly accused Washington’s allies, including South Korea, of leeching off the United States and not paying their fair share. To extract more from Washington’s partners, Trump has implemented punishing tariffs. In response, South Korea could have been defiant and slapped on retaliatory restrictions, as other U.S. allies did. Instead, South Korean President Lee Jae-myung avoided rebuking the president and worked to meet American demands in ways that heightened the two states’ mutual dependence. For instance, Lee pledged to invest $350 billion in the United States to advance American economic and national security interests, including by purchasing more U.S. military equipment, and to increase South Korean economic support for locally stationed American forces.

Seoul’s strategy has not been a uniform success. The South Korean economy remains exposed to American and Chinese pressure despite its growth and diversification. North Korea is expanding its nuclear and missile forces and deepening military ties with Russia, creating new security headaches for which Seoul still needs U.S. assistance. The country is politically polarized, resulting in sharp policy shifts that can undermine long-term strategic planning. It also suffers from the lowest birthrate of any country in the world, threatening its tax base and the size of its armed forces. Beyond that, South Korea may not provide the ideal template for every middle power. Not every country in this category has similar economic resources, networks, or ties to the United States.

But on the whole, South Korea’s grand strategy has been effective, and it thus carries broader lessons. For one, it shows how adaptive middle powers can draw on their respective national narratives to find their strengths and to chart a path forward. Perhaps more important, it is proof positive that middle powers can exercise influence and achieve a measure of autonomy even without working together to build a neutral third pole, as Carney urged. It also illustrates that middle powers can remain firmly allied with the United States while developing their own independent capabilities. South Korea, then, demonstrates that states can reduce their vulnerability and widen their political options.

With Its Allies, Seoul Has Enough Leverage to Push Back

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5820. Mary Peltola flips Alaska's Senate seat. And, Nvidia won't reinstate Jensen Huang

Former Democratic Rep. Mary Peltola has defeated Republican Sen. Dan S. Sullivan in Alaska's U.S. Senate race, according to The Associated Press. Her victory gives Democrats 56 senators in the upcoming Congress. Alaska uses ranked-choice voting for its general election, but Peltola secured a majority of first-place votes, eliminating the need for ranked-choice calculations. Her win flips a Senate seat in a state that President Trump won by 13 percentage points in 2024. The race's final days were tumultuous due to allegations that Peltola's team tried to recruit another candidate to the race to siphon votes from the incumbent. Peltola's campaign denied any involvement.

Mary Peltola looks on during a campaign rally at the Alaska Aviation Museum on October 30, 2026 in Anchorage, Alaska. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images hide caption

Kratom and related 7-OH products are widely available at gas stations, smoke shops, liquor stores and online. As early as Friday, the federal government could make some of the products illegal. Mindy Schauer/MediaNewsGroup/Orange County Register via Getty Images hide caption

In recent years, highly concentrated products containing 7-hydroxymitragynine (7-OH) have emerged online and at smoke shops, liquor stores and gas stations. These products, available as tablets, gummies, tonics and chocolates, contain one of the major psychoactive ingredients in kratom. Unlike kratom leaf products, 7-OH acts more like an opioid in the human brain. Some people use kratom to maintain long-term recovery from opioid addiction. Others say it helps them feel energized or focused. Users of 7-OH and kratom can show signs of addiction, and unclear product labeling makes it hard to determine the quantities consumed. Over the past decade, the U.S. government has tried to restrict these products, but public opposition has kept them legal. Recently, this situation has started to change.

#5810. Democrats formally nominate Hakeem Jeffries for speaker of the House

Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., speaks during a news conference in the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 17, 2026. Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images hide caption

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries has been nominated by House Democrats to serve as Speaker of the House when the party retakes the majority in the new year.

Wednesday's vote by House Democrats kickstarts the formal selection process for Speaker, which will take place at the start of the new Congress on January 3 with a vote on the floor of the House. If successful, as is widely expected, Jeffries will make history as the first Black lawmaker to serve in the job.

"Our message to the American people who are struggling in this failed economy is a simple one. We hear you, we see you, we feel you, and House Democrats are committed to making life better for you," Jeffries said after the vote.

Jeffries centered his remarks on cost of living issues, saying Democrats would fight to lower the cost of groceries, gas, housing and health care. And in the face of what he described as "unprecedented corruption," Jeffries said Democrats would work to hold officials accountable.

"Not for partisan reasons but for patriotic ones, because the American people deserve better," he said.

The New York Democrat ran uncontested, winning a closed-door vote by a tally of 251 to 11. His victory comes just over a week after House Republicans selected Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio to be the conference's minority leader; in the Senate, Republicans tapped South Dakota's John Thune for a second term as party leader.

Jeffries, who's led House Democrats since 2023, will need just a simple majority of member votes in a Congress where Democrats are poised to control at least 257 seats, with several remaining races still to be decided.

It's a vastly different reality from what awaited House Republicans four years ago when they regained the House majority. Facing extremely narrow margins, GOP leaders struggled to unite the conference around a speaker and were forced to negotiate with a handful of far-right party holdouts in order to reach consensus. After multiple candidates failed to secure a majority, the party ultimately coalesced around the now outgoing Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La.

While Jeffries will still have to navigate intraparty divides, the size of Democrats' majority affords him room to weather some dissent.

He's faced resistance from some within the party's left-most flank, who argue Democrats should prioritize advancing a more progressive agenda next year, lobbying for votes on Medicare for All and legislation that would stop the transfer of offensive weapons to Israel.

Among those who voted against Jeffries were a number of newly-elected members — some who won with the backing of the Democratic Socialists of America. In a statement after the vote, they said they have engaged in "good-faith conversations" with Jeffries to secure commitments on their policy priorities.

"While we have seen meaningful progress, there is still work to be done to ensure that the direction our party takes this coming Congress reflects what our constituents are demanding of leadership. Those conversations are ongoing. As such, our vote in caucus today was no."

Among those who signed the statement were members-elect Darializa Avila Chevalier and Claire Valdez, both from New York; New Jersey's Adam Hamawy; Frederick Haynes of Texas; Colorado's Melat Kiros; Donavan McKinney of Michigan and Chris Rabb of Pennsylvania.

After the vote, Jeffries told reporters "the results of today's election speak for themselves."

"We're going to work hard throughout every corner of the caucus to deliver for the American people. Our job is to fight MAGA extremism — not each other — to fight MAGA extremism and to get big things done for the American people."

Given that Democrats will hold double digit margins in the chamber — an edge propelled by the success of more moderate candidates in districts President Trump carried in 2024 — it's unclear how much leverage the left wing of the party will have in the speaker's vote.

Divides inside the party may prove more pronounced in the Senate, where some Democrats are more publicly questioning whether current Minority Leader Chuck Schumer of New York is best suited to deliver on voter demands for change.

Schumer faces a challenge from Maryland's Chris Van Hollen, who announced his candidacy last week and is pitching himself as an alternative to the status quo, echoing much of the progressive populist messaging that several successful Senate candidates ran on this cycle. Like Schumer, Van Hollen has also served in Congress for decades.

Schumer, the top Democrat in the Senate for nearly a decade, contends that he still has the needed votes to be elected majority leader. However, Van Hollen's challenge comes as a handful of current and incoming members have declined to back Schumer for the job.

Both sides still have time to shore up votes, with leadership elections set for early December.

#5801. As overdose deaths fall, White House makes misleading claims about who deserves credit

Families look on as President Donald Trump speaks during a ceremony to sign the "Halt All Lethal Trafficking of Fentanyl Act" in the East Room of the White House on July 16, 2025. Evan Vucci/AP hide caption

When the White House announced big improvements in the number of people dying from fatal drug overdoses earlier this week, officials pointed to hopeful new data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention showing U.S. drug deaths have plunged to their lowest level in roughly a decade, saving nearly 50,000 lives each year.

But leading drug policy researchers say the White House statement also made misleading claims about the timing and cause of the remarkable public health success when it said that gains occurred primarily over the last 18 months due to policies enacted under President Trump's leadership.

"The historic decline in drug overdose deaths demonstrates that America is unequivocally winning the fight against illicit drugs," White House Office of National Drug Control Policy director Sara Carter said in a statement.

Carter credited heightened border security under Trump and claims of success dismantling "narco-terrorist" organizations for the recovery. "Critically, this represents a an approximately 41.5% decrease in total drug overdose deaths compared to the peak under the previous administration," the statement said.

What the White House failed to disclose is that declines in overdose fatalities began much earlier, under the Biden administration, with most of the gains occurring before Trump returned to office.

"Since the spring of 2023, we have seen a steady decline in overdose deaths," said Vanda Felbab-Brown, a drug policy expert at the Brookings Institution. "The fact that it has been sustained is very important."

According to CDC data, reductions in drug deaths began in 2023 and have continued steadily for at least 39 consecutive months — not 18 months as the ONDCP statement said. By the time Biden left office, fatal overdoses had already dropped by roughly one-third, an improvement described by addiction experts at the time as unprecedented.

Nabarun Dasgupta, a street drug researcher at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, was one of the first scientists to detect the stunning drop in overdose deaths. He said the trend toward fewer deaths began in some regions and among some demographic groups as early as 2021 and rapidly gained momentum nationwide.

"It was really the period early 2024 through spring 2025 where we were seeing the greatest rate of decline that we've ever seen," Dasgupta said.

Deaths from fentanyl smuggled into the U.S. from Mexico first began to rise in 2013 during President Obama's time in office and accelerated during Trump's first term. Fentanyl overdoses spiked sharply the first two years of Biden's presidency, during the COVID-19 pandemic. Despite progress, fentanyl remains the leading cause of drug deaths nationwide.

Dasgupta, Felbab-Brown and other and addiction experts credit the Biden administration for rolling out policy responses that included wider access to healthcare, making the opioid overdose medication naloxone more readily available, and negotiating a deal with China that disrupted the flow of industrial chemicals used to make fentanyl.

President Joe Biden met China's President President Xi Jinping in Woodside, Calif., in November 2023, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperative conference. China agreed to curtail shipments of the chemicals used to make fentanyl, the drug at the heart of the U.S. overdose epidemic. Doug Mills/Pool/The New York Times hide caption

The Biden team also scored significant drug interdiction wins against the Mexican cartels that smuggle fentanyl. "We have certainly been seeing over many years, going back to the Biden administration, important efforts to crack down against them," said Felbab-Brown.

Dasgupta and Felbab-Brown both said improvements in reducing drug mortality have continued under Trump. They said it's important to have an accurate timeline of the overdose crisis because researchers are still trying to understand why deaths involving fentanyl surged and why so many fewer people are now dying.

NPR asked the White House about progress in the fentanyl fight under Biden. In an emailed statement, ONDCP director Carter, a former podcaster, journalist and Fox News contributor, blamed Biden for failing to secure the U.S.-Mexico border and for allowing drug deaths to surge in the first place.

"While drug overdose deaths eventually began to decline following the peak, the Biden Administration doesn't get to take credit for overseeing the highest drug overdose numbers in history, then bringing them down from their own failure," Carter said.

Responding to CDC data showing drug deaths began to plummet much earlier than stated the White House announcement, Carter said "this could be misinterpreted as progress on a graph, but it was not progress."

According to Carter, the Trump administration is now well-positioned to build on overdose death reductions made over the last three years, in part by maintaining border security and by continuing to "relentlessly attack" drug gangs.

"We are dismantling and eliminating the violent cartels and drug trafficking organizations through coordinated operations," she said.

But Felbab-Brown at Brookings said she doesn't share the Trump administration's confidence that there has been significant progress "dismantling and eliminating" narco-terrorist organizations.

"I cannot think of one narco-terrorist organization that has been dismantled," she said. "They all continue operating and they are all very strong. They all continue smuggling drugs."

Military experts have also questioned the effectiveness, and legality, of Trump's ongoing strikes against civilian boats in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific, described as smuggling vessels by the Pentagon.

Dasgupta, the addiction researcher at UNC, said he's also concerned about whether progress reducing drug deaths can be sustained next year as the Trump administration implements deep cuts to Medicaid passed by the Republican Congress.

The Congressional Budget Office has estimated the cuts could mean as many as 10 million Americans losing access to health insurance coverage.

"Medicaid is the largest funder of addiction services in the United States," Dasgupta said. "Cutting Medicaid eligibility or making it harder to access forms of effective addiction treatment is guaranteed to leave people out in the cold."

#5779. Martin Solar cuts out zip ties with new mounting rail

Martin Solar‘s newly manufactured Dual-Channel Solar Rail (DCSR) was recently installed on a 70-kWDC solar project, marking its first real-world application.

The Dual-Channel Solar Rail from Martin Solar.

“Management of PV wires and AC trunk cables is one of the most important aspects of rooftop PV installation. Anyone who has ever experienced running hundreds, thousands of feet of conductors on a rooftop system knows what a hassle it is having to apply countless zip ties that are not really suitable for long-term wire management,” said Preston Nelson, director of Martin Solar and co-inventor of DCSR. “What’s worse than the initial application of zip ties is having to pull up finished solar panels to apply even more zip ties after an inspector inevitably cites an installation for having wires that droop down to the roof surface.”

DCSR is designed with a wire trough large enough to fit three-phase trunk cables, like those manufactured by Enphase, Hoymiles, and AP Systems. It can also fit up to twenty standard PV cables, without the use of zip ties. Instead of using specialized panel clamps, DCSR’s panel attachment channel is off to the side of the wire trough, where it separately accepts panel hardware.

“The dual-channel design with separately dedicated features allowed us to increase the cross-sectional area of the wire trough to a size never seen before as a rail feature. In the past, we would install a separate wire tray to manage numerous long trunk cable runs, which was even more expensive and time-consuming. Now, this present project installation involves over 600 total feet of trunk, without a single cable tie. The separate wire trough can even allow access to wire runs without lifting panels, especially convenient when a worker simply needs to track a PV string,” Nelson said.

DCSR is the first product accessory to Martin Solar’s Hidden-Fastener Solar Mount, which mounts to the roof surface without putting holes in shingles. While Martin’s proprietary solar rail and mount can be paired, both are compatible with other solar racking and mounts.

Martin Solar will show DCSR and its solar mounting system at RE+ from November 17 to 19 at booth C5071.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5772. From Callback to Confidence: How Engineering an ESS for Field Reduces the Costly Callbacks

Installer on-site callbacks are the silent margin killer in residential energy management and storage. They consume labor, fuel, new project scheduling capacity, and homeowner trust in ways that rarely show up in the original bid. Across the industry, callbacks come from three sources that often overlap: system designs that are too complex to cleanly install, hardware failures after the system is in service, and challenges that arise in the field during installations. Most contractors only feel the cumulative cost years after an install have rolled past. Conversely highly reliable system hardware allows a contractor to confidently “right-size” the project price bid, helping to bring in more business in a period of industry transition.

Preventing callbacks is not only the installer’s responsibility. How the system is engineered, built, and supported all impact callbacks. FranklinWH has focused on quality products and the simplest install, with hardware held to an independently verified reliability standard. The company provides support before, during and after the installation. All of this helps to minimize callbacks. Service data shows once system complexity and hardware failure are engineered down, the dominant driver of post-install activity that remains is the installation stage itself, the real-world variability of the field. That, too, is a problem to solve at the source, not one to pass to the installer.

This paper walks through the anatomy of a callback, the hidden cost stack it creates, the actual root causes industry-wide and in our own data, and the engineering, support, and service decisions made to reduce callback risk before the truck rolls, and to absorb the cost when it can’t be avoided.

#5763. Outer Spaces takes solar beyond the rooftop with integrated backyard energy system

Residential solar is finding a new place to call home: the backyard.

Outer Spaces, a new company founded by the entrepreneurs behind outdoor living brand Outer, is introducing a solar-powered pavilion that combines photovoltaic generation, battery storage and usable outdoor living space. The company’s first product, OS1, can be designed for multiple functions, including an outdoor kitchen, office, gym or gathering space, while generating electricity and providing backup power for the home.

Outer Spaces is planning to produce outdoor structures that integrate solar PV and energy storage. Credit: Outer Spaces

The California-based company is positioning OS1 as a new category of residential energy infrastructure that goes beyond conventional rooftop arrays and garage-mounted batteries. It builds on the growing interest in solar structures that also serve a secondary purpose.

OS1 combines a solar pavilion with a finished outdoor power deck. Solar panels integrated into the overhead structure generate electricity, while a thin battery storage system is housed beneath the deck. According to Outer Spaces, the combination allows the structure to produce energy throughout the day and provide backup power during outages.

Rather than competing exclusively with traditional rooftop solar, OS1 offers homeowners an additional way to use available property for energy production, particularly when roof aesthetics, space limitations, or homeowner preferences become obstacles to conventional installations.

Outer Spaces describes OS1 as a modular system designed for rapid on-site assembly, with minimal to no excavation required. The company says installation avoids traditional construction steps such as concrete pouring and lumber cutting. Once the structure is assembled, a licensed electrician then connects its energy system to the customer’s home.

The product’s first availability is scheduled for 2027, beginning in Southern California and select additional markets; pricing is expected to be announced in early 2027.

Solar canopies and pergolas have increasingly incorporated features such as EV charging, lighting, fans and weather protection, giving homeowners additional reasons to invest in solar outside the traditional rooftop model.

Outer Spaces represents another potential avenue for expanding residential solar adoption through design-driven products. The opportunity will ultimately depend on pricing, installation economics, permitting requirements and the system’s measurable energy performance.

Outer Spaces plans to showcase OS1 publicly for the first time at RE+ 2026, Nov. 16 to 19, in Las Vegas.

#5758. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

#5745. SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5735. AI Businesses Still Bleeding Billions

AI is fun and cool when it creates useful things for you or answers tough questions quickly and well (let’s not focus on AI slop and false claims at the moment), but that doesn’t mean AI businesses are financially sustainable or sensible. AI companies are still heavily subsidizing their broad use, with the benefits for them being growing user bases and sky-high valuations. These companies are burning billions of dollars each quarter on the hype and promise that they will rule the world one day (quite literally).

OpenAI, the company behind ChatGPT, reported this week that it had $10.9 billion in operating losses last quarter. That’s up from $10.1 billion in operating losses in the second quarter.

One might think that means not a lot changed, and certainly nothing positive. However, the company’s revenue was actually up by 50%! So, even with a huge boost in revenue, gigantic losses grew! Furthermore, the company was apparently even using a tool of “last resort” here.

OpenAI started running ads in ChatGPT, something that the company was adamantly trying to avoid. CEO Sam Altman previously called the proposal to run ads “unsettling” and claimed that the company would only go that route as a matter of “last resort.” Well, it seems the company has reached that point of last resort. And that can’t be a good thing, right? Considering that OpenAI is still losing billions of dollars a quarter, it’s even more concerning. What comes after a “last resort” effort to turn finances around?

Frankly, I wish we could spend billions of dollars a quarter to make CleanTechnica into a behemoth of a media organization! That would be amazing — imagine all of the stuff we could do! But what are the chances we’d be losing billions of dollars a quarter in doing so? And how long could we operate like that before external sources of funding stopped covering the bill?

As we know, OpenAI is one of the “leaders” of the AI industry. If it is struggling like this, who isn’t, and what does that mean for the industry overall and where it’s headed?

The one big player that has been raking in the dough in the midst of this enormous AI craze is NVIDIA, and look at what’s now happening there! Where are things going to go with that company?

“The news comes not long after controversy over how OpenAI was projecting its earnings. In October, the company reported an annualized revenue, an estimate of how much money it will earn over a year, of about $50 billion — which was $20 billion short of what it told investors only a month before,” Futurism adds. “The discrepancy was taken as an admission that the business models of AI companies weren’t as robust as their leadership was making them out to be, fueling speculation of an impending bubble.”

Why can’t the leaders of these companies just ask their chatbots how to turn a profit and implement their answers? Well, one of the answers might have been regulatory capture and blocking others from entering the market, but that dream may now be out the window. In my humble opinion, 2027 could be a very interesting year for AI companies, and that might involve a big popping noise.

He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5732. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5730. Fabric Molds Could Bring Material-Saving Concrete Beams Within Reach

MIT engineers cast a concrete beam using a sewn fabric mold with one-third the amount of concrete and half as much steel as a conventional beam.

Topology optimization is a computer design method that can help engineers design beams using reduced amounts of concrete and steel. But those intricate shapes produced from the tool are often too complex or expensive to pour into conventional rigid molds. The few that have been built have mostly relied on specialized equipment, such as concrete 3D printers or machine-carved molds, equipment that is out of reach in regions where new construction is expected to grow.

Now, MIT engineers have shown that highly efficient beams can be cast in something more accessible: sewn fabric. In a paper published in the Journal of Building Engineering, the team designed and built a 3-meter (about 10-foot) test beam using fabric molds created through an automated pattern-making process. Compared with a conventional beam designed to carry the same weight, the optimized beam used about one-third as much concrete and half as much steel reinforcement. When tested, its measured response was within about 2 percent of what the team’s computer model predicted.

Fabric formwork, in which concrete is poured into a mold made of textile, has been explored for years to create beams that vary in depth, rather than in rectangular shapes common in construction. But because fabric deforms under the weight of wet concrete, that flexibility has made the final shape hard to predict.

“The key realization was that this flexibility is not just a limitation to manage; with the right computational pattern design, it can become a way to produce optimized structural forms,” says Josephine Carstensen, the Gilbert W. Winslow (1937) Career Development Professor in Civil Engineering and senior author of the study.

Fabric molds can also be inexpensive, lightweight, and fast compared to wood or steel molds. “You could sew a formwork together off site, pack it into a box, and bring it to a job site very easily,” says Jackson Jewett PhD ’25, co-author of the study. “We joke that we are developing the H&M of the construction industry.”

Cement production accounts for roughly 8 percent of human-caused carbon emissions.

“This work addresses concrete’s climate footprint primarily by reducing material demand,” Carstensen says. “Cement production is highly carbon-intensive, so using less concrete is an important part of decarbonizing construction.”

Where new construction happens matters, too. The world’s number of buildings is expected to nearly double by 2050, with about 85 percent of that growth in developing countries, where access to advanced construction equipment is often limited. That is why, Carstensen says, the team focused on an accessible, cost-conscious fabrication approach.

Concrete is strong when compressed, but weak when pulled, so it’s reinforced with steel that can handle the pulling forces. The researchers first mapped a path for the steel reinforcement using a method called graphic statics, which can generate structural designs almost instantly. They used it to shape the steel bar so that it carries an equal share of the weight along its entire length to keep the reinforcement simple enough for builders to make. Topology optimization, which works more slowly but can place material freely, was used to determine where concrete was needed and where material could be removed from areas where it contributes little to the structural performance.

“Both methods are good at different things, so we tried to combine the strengths of both into a single methodology,” says Jewett.

The resulting beam has a top section, narrow angled legs, and a thin bottom section for the steel reinforcement. The team’s automated process translated the 3D beam design into flat fabric pieces that can be cut and sewn into a mold, accounting for how the fabric will deform once concrete is poured in. A simple timber frame supported the finished fabric mold.

For Mark Hellrich SM ’26, the study’s lead author, the main hands-on challenge was getting the concrete mix right. Woven cotton fabric lets water seep through, so a mix that is too runny loses water through the mold. It also presses harder against the fabric, pushing it away from the intended shape.

The team used a moderately wet, sand-rich mix with only small stones, so the concrete could flow through the beam’s narrow, angled legs and thin bottom section without getting stuck. They then packed it in by hand, prodding each layer and tapping and kneading the fabric from the outside.

“This is where the flexibility of the mold becomes an advantage: you can work the concrete through the interconnected passages without a vibrator, which a rigid mold of this shape would need,” says Hellrich. A concrete vibrator is a tool typically used to shake air pockets out of freshly poured concrete in rigid molds.

Once the concrete was in place, the wet fabric revealed the beam’s final shape. “All three beams were free of wrinkles and creases,” says Hellrich. The top surface stayed close to the design, while the main difference was a downward sag along the bottom, with the angled legs folding slightly inward.

To find out how much sewing precision the beam needs, the team built three molds for the same design, made from 36, 56, and 76 fabric pieces. Comparing the 3D scans of the cast beams with the target design, the researchers found that more seams gave finer control over the details, but did not necessarily make the beam’s overall shape more accurate.

“More seams improve local control because they divide the fabric into smaller, more constrained panels. But a more complex pattern also has more pieces to align and more seam length, so small cutting, sewing, and assembly inaccuracies can accumulate across the mold, explains Carstensen.

The 56-piece mold demonstrated the best balance, capturing the important design geometry without the added time, effort, and potential errors associated with the most complex pattern.

The researchers then load-tested one of the beams, gradually stacking 36 water-filled buckets on top of it, up to about 30 percent of its design load to reflect everyday service conditions. Weighted lines hung from the beam’s underside to precise measuring gauges below, tracking how far the beam bent as the load was added and removed. At the beam’s center, the measured deflection — its downward movement under the load — was within about 2 percent of the computer model’s prediction.

The team also compared the cost of a single fabric-formed beam with a conventional one and found that location makes all the difference. In the Global South, where materials tend to cost more relative to labor, all three fabric versions were cheaper, with savings of about 10 to 20 percent. In the Global North, higher labor costs for sewing reversed that advantage, making the fabric versions about 16 to 55 percent more expensive.

The findings suggest that fabric formwork is most promising for custom-shaped concrete components produced as a one-off or in small batches, such as exposed beams, roof or long-span framing, pedestrian structures, and landscape elements. For the repetitive, rectangular beams found in most buildings, conventional reusable molds remain the more economical choice where labor is expensive.

“To get started, a builder would need the digital pattern for the target component, a sewing machine and suitable fabric, and a simple timber support frame,” Carstensen says. “That is a much lower equipment threshold than robotic fabrication, concrete 3D printing, or CNC-milled molds.”

The researchers describe the 3-meter beam as a proof of concept comparable to a short building span. It is also a big step up from the 1.2-meter beam the team built in a previous project, Jewett notes.

Building bigger isn’t just a matter of enlarging the design, Carstensen notes. As a beam gets longer, its own weight and the pressure on the fabric mold change, so a larger beam would need a new design. The next step, Carstensen says, is “to test multiple beams to failure.”

“The nice thing about these optimization methods is they generate structures that look organic,” Jewett says. “Their geometries are clear expressions of how the forces move through the structural system. There is elegance to them. It makes you ask why we don’t design all our structures in this way.”

Joining Carstensen, Jewett, and Hellrich on the paper are co-authors Abhinit Sati, Edward Chien, and Emily Whiting, of Boston University’s Department of Computer Science.

The work was partially supported by the U.S. National Science Foundation.

By Stephanie Martinovich | Department of Civil and Environmental Engineering, MIT

#5723. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5720. What The US Midterm Elections Mean For Climate Progress

People across the United States voted in record numbers in last week’s incredibly consequential election, which has completely changed the makeup of Congress for its new term beginning in January 2027. The success of our electoral processes—still underway—should not be taken for granted. It’s a win for democracy when millions of citizens peacefully claim their right to a government of, by, and for the people.

Will the Trump administration acknowledge voters’ repudiation of its destructive agenda and work in cooperation with this new Congress? That remains to be seen, and I’m not holding my breath. But there’s no question that the shift in Congress has significant implications for the administration’s ability to continue to ram through its anti-science, authoritarian agenda—including on climate and clean energy issues.

Energy affordability concerns, opposition to proliferating data centers, and anger at corruption and extreme economic inequality were undoubtedly driving forces in how people voted. While not everyone explicitly named climate change as a top issue (although many did put it in their top three), its fingerprints are all over the issues a majority of voters did name in polls.

If you care about addressing the high costs of electricity and gas, then you must reckon with the fact that this current fossil energy crisis, unleashed by the Trump administration’s war against Iran, is an extreme manifestation of the risks of fossil fuel dependence. The benefits of a transition away from fossil fuels to renewable energy—renewable energy that the Trump administration has relentlessly attacked, further driving up costs for consumers—are tremendous. If you are deeply concerned about the rapid, unregulated build-out of data centers, you know that it comes with huge risks for communities related to climate, water, pollution, health, and electricity cost burdens. And it’s so clear that the Trump administration’s corruption, lies about climate science, and massive giveaways to fossil fuel polluters are leaving us all bearing the high costs of climate damages while large corporations and the president’s inner circle are raking in enormous profits.

Here’s another reality: regardless of politics, climate change is here, and science shows it’s rapidly worsening with the world’s imminent overshoot of 1.5°C of global warming. Communities are already experiencing deadly and costly climate impacts, including extreme heatwaves, catastrophic wildfires, record-breaking flooding, drought, accelerating sea level rise, intense ocean heat, irreversible loss of glaciers and ice sheets, stresses to critical ecosystems and more. These impacts are showing up in people’s daily lives—harming their health, destroying homes, livelihoods, and critical infrastructure, raising insurance premiums, hammering businesses, stressing water supplies, and threatening food security and affordability.

Climate change and clean energy are pocketbook issues. They are a lot more than that, of course. And climate change does not care if you live in a red state or a blue state. (One can ask if that is that even a relevant frame after an election that scrambled all conventional wisdom about “red” and “blue” dichotomies.)

Importantly, we will soon have a Congress where a majority accepts the scientific evidence on climate change. (Yes, I also wish we could take that for granted in 2026, but not in US politics, sadly.) That’s an important start and means we can advocate for policymakers to legislate based on the facts, instead of being stuck fighting disinformation and lies.

Here’s what this seismic “change election” could mean for action on climate change, some cautions about what likely won’t change right away, and what we’ll have to keep fighting for.

We will have to keep fighting back in courts to hold the Trump administration accountable for egregiously flouting the law. We’ve already had a major win in a lawsuit where the administration was found to be violating the Federal Advisory Committee Act in secretly setting up a ‘Climate Working Group’ stacked with climate contrarians to draft a sham science report, which the EPA then used to justify its proposal to gut the Endangerment Finding. We’ve also filed suit against the administration for repealing the Endangerment Finding, contrary to law, science, and the public interest.

On the international front, the Trump administration has shamefully exited the bedrock UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement, and that won’t change as long as it is in power. As I write from COP31 in Türkiye, the administration’s abandonment of global climate cooperation is very much on my mind; it’s crucial for the rest of the world to forge ahead regardless of the high stakes. There is a real risk that a beleaguered Trump administration will double down on its authoritarian ways and continue to find new ways to disrupt global climate progress and multilateral institutions. But Congress now has the power and responsibility to keep this reckless administration in check and ensure it is held accountable, at home and on the global stage.

“Change elections” come weighted with the hopes and dreams of many. As climate justice advocates, we know that policymakers have pushed off meaningful climate action far too long, and those failures are putting us all at profound risk. Yet, deep-pocketed fossil fuel interests, Big Tech, and other powerful entities continue to obstruct and delay action, still hold all too much sway over both parties in Congress, and we can expect their harmful tactics to ratchet up. So now it’s our responsibility to fight for what comes next, to make our shared dreams come true. Because those dreams of a safer, more just world for generations of young people to come must become reality.

Courtesy of The Equation, USC, by Rachel Cleetus.

#5713. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

“There’s an important difference between talking about building a domestic solar supply chain and actually making it happen,” said ES Foundry CEO Alex Zhu in a statement. “At ES Foundry, we’re manufacturing at commercial scale, shipping cells to customers and navigating the realities of U.S. production every day. That experience gives us a unique perspective on what it will take to build a competitive, sustainable American solar industry. Success requires more than manufacturing capacity. It requires the right economics, technology investments, customer demand and policies to support long-term growth. We’re proud to be delivering on the promise of American manufacturing today while helping shape the industry’s future.”

Visit ES Foundry at RE+ 2026: Booth C7434, November 16–19, Las Vegas, Nevada.

#5712. Rocket Fuel: The Last Frontier For Natural Gas

Fossil fuels are slowly beginning to lose their grip on the global transportation sector, with one key omission: rocket fuel. The rocket fuel conundrum has earned an uptick in attention in recent years, now that rocket launches, once rare, are becoming as commonplace as rush hour traffic. The Elon Musk venture SpaceX, for example, is building new natural gas pipelines in order to produce its own methane-based propellant, needed to sustain the launch of many thousands of rockets in the coming years.

Not all rocket fuel is methane-based, but that’s where some aerospace firms, including SpaceX, have landed in the pursuit of space domination. Methane is processed from natural gas, stripping away nitrogen and other impurities. Liquefied and mixed with liquid oxygen, it becomes a powerful propellant.

With its future volume of launch operations in mind, SpaceX aims to exercise as much control as possible over its fuel supply. The company is investing in its own natural gas pipelines and on-site fuel production facilities to help ensure that the taps don’t run dry, though it still relies on the gas industry to find the stuff and bring it up from the ground.

SpaceX has already been sucking up a copious amount of natural gas for its existing operations in Texas and Florida. “SpaceX (SPCX) is emerging as one of the largest new customers for natural gas,” reported the oil and gas analytics firm East Daley in October.

“Unlike Falcon 9, which burns rocket-grade kerosene and liquid oxygen, Starship creates a direct link between launch activity and natural gas consumption. SPCX plans to process and liquefy pipeline gas into liquid methane to fuel the rockets,” East Daley elaborates.

As described by East Daley, the SpaceX subsidiary Coastal Connect Services is planning a 32.4-mile natural gas pipeline to hook up SpaceX operations in Cape Canaveral, Florida with existing infrastructure jointly owned by Kinder Morgan and Energy Transfer.

Meanwhile, at the company’s launch site and newly incorporated (as of 2025) City of Starbase in Cameron County, Texas, plans are in motion for SpaceX affiliate Lone Star Mineral Development to connect the new 8-mile “Starpipe” natural gas pipeline with another 145-mile pipeline currently being built by the firms WhiteWater, Enbridge, and MPLX. Starpipe is expected to begin shuttling natural gas into Starbase Texas early next year.

The two pipeline projects come alongside word that SpaceX has nailed down approvals for parts of a new $100 billion project that expands the company’s US footprint into another combination city-and-launch-site under the name of Starbase, Louisiana. The new city will presumably also require a reliable supply of natural gas for on-site rocket fuel production.

For the record, Starbase, Louisiana is not part of the educational non-profit organization STARBASE Louisiana, an affiliate of the national STARBASE program of the US Department of Defense, aimed at stimulating interest in STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering, Mathematics) topics among 5th grade students. The Louisiana branch of STARBASE is located Barksdale Air Force Base in Bossier Parish, in the northwestern part of the state.

Starbase, Louisiana will take up 125,000 acres of land at Pecan Island in a marshy region along the southern coast in Vermilion Parish, where plans are being laid for 30 launches per day. More than a dozen launch towers will eventually sprout up at the site, where the operation will include producing rocket fuel.

If you’ve gotten wind of the project under the name “Osprey,” you’re not dreaming. The new city was reportedly code-named “Project Osprey” during initial discussions with public officials. As described by the Gulf State News affiliate WWNO (New Orleans Public Radio) on November 11, Project Osprey was shrouded in secrecy during the initial negotionas, with “dozens” of local officials signing non-disclosure agreements that limited what they could say about the project.

The Osprey code name also reportedly enabled Vermilion Parish officials to sidestep public participation in ostensibly public hearings on the Starbase, Louisiana project earlier this year. Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry finally let the cat out of the bag in a public announcement in August. Local residents and the nonprofit organization Vermillion Citizens coalition have already filed a lawsuit against the project, alleging that parish officials approved parts of Starbase, Louisiana in violation of the state’s Open Public Meetings Law.

If and when shovels hit the ground on Starbase, Louisiana, the Trump administration is taking steps to help ensure that natural gas producers in the US come through with the goods — not just for SpaceX, of course, although the firm has become a market-mover in the gas industry as noted by East Daley.

On November 10, for example, the US Department of Energy announced the finishing touches on a $480 million loan in support of Project Elkhorn, an endeavor under the umbrella of the oil and gas firm Arena Energy. Project Elkhorn covers the modernization of 22 existing platforms off the Louisiana coast — coincidentally or not, the same state that expects to host the new Starbase city.

Arena launched in 1999 with a focus on revitalizing mature oil and gas fields. According to the Energy Department, Project Elkhorn will ramp natural gas production up to 77 million cubic feet per day at peak production among the 22 platforms, alongside up to 38,600 barrels of oil per day. By way of contrast, the 22 platforms currently produce 30,000 barrels of oil per day. Over the lifespan of Project Elkhorn, the collective total is expected to add 25 million barrels of oil and 250 billion cubic feet of natural gas to the US fossil energy roster.

As CEO of the leading EV maker Tesla, Elon Musk has earned his share of credit for rescuing electric cars from the dustbin of automotive obscurity and vaulting them into the mainstream mobility market of today — if not so much here in the US, then certainly overseas. Musk’s other ventures, on the other hand, threaten to offset, if not override, whatever sustainability gains are to be made from EVs.

In addition to SpaceX, Musk’s xAI data centers have also contributed to spiking demand for natural gas. Last year word dropped that Musk’s Colossus data center in Memphis, Tennessee trucked in approximately three dozen industrial-sized gas generators to power the site, at least 15 of which were already in operation without an air quality permit. The Southern Environmental Law Center has been among those raising the alarm over toxic emissions from the generators, which lack the pollution controls required of permanent power plants.

Natural gas also factors into a new data center planned for the SpaceX-backed City of Starbase, Texas, which sits adjacent to Brownsville. The city of Brownsville recently imposed a temporary moratorium on new data center construction. According to ABC News affiliate KVUE and other sources, earlier this year Elon Musk asked Brownsville officials to de-annex a parcel of land at its outskirts, evidently in order to building a $24 billion data center at the site along with a new gas-fired power plant for a new data center to be located in Starbase. The request was granted, enabling the project to skirt around the Brownsville moratorium.

Starbase city officials are scheduled to conduct a public hearing on the new data center on December 9, where presumably they will decide whether or not the project can make due with gas generators until a full gas power plant with appropriate pollution controls can materialize.

Over and above the rocket fuel issue, grassroots activists, environmental organizations, and researchers have been raising the alarm over the impact of Starbase in Texas for years, tracking significant harm to local public recreation resources as well as wildlife habitats. Similar concerns have surfaced around SpaceX’s plans for Starbase, Louisiana.

Of course, nobody seriously expects the whole domestic rocket business to stop in its tracks. The issue is one of mitigating climate and biodiversity risks, developing projects that avoid pummeling vulnerable communities with additional public health risks, and investing in more sustainable options including alternative rocket fuels.

Keep an eye on the aviation industry for some hints at the future of sustainable propellants. Airline stakeholders have been focusing on SAF, generally meaning a bio-based equivalent of kerosene jet fuel. However, the going has been slow, and it’s even slower in the field of propellants. The European Space Agency, for example, has been exploring green propellants for space operations for 25 years, with little to show for its efforts.

More recently, though, aviation stakeholders have begun turning to the emerging electrofuels or “solar fuels” industry, meaning fuels synthesized from captured carbon and green hydrogen, with solar and other renewables powering the operation. The green hydrogen industry has been struggling for traction, but signs of scaleup have finally emerged, with e-kerosene jet fuel in play along with green hydrogen. Honda, for one, is envisioning a fleet of reusable rockets propelled by renewable fuels, and at least two Chinese firms are also working the reusable rocket angle.

Image: SpaceX is among the industrial-scale consumers of natural gas adding more pipes to the nation’s sprawling network of gas pipelines, which the startup needs to ensure a steady supply of fuel for its growing fleet of rockets (courtesy of US Energy Information Agency).
