#5708. Sonnen, SOLRITE, & ADAPT Energy, Integrating VPP Breakers to Turn Home Loads into Flexible Grid Assets

SOLRITE Unveils Next-Gen Virtual Power Plant Node with sonnen and ADAPT Energy, Integrating VPP Breakers to Turn Home Loads into Flexible Grid Assets

ADAPT Energy’s control of VPP breakers will add dispatchable load flexibility to the SOLRITE sonnen VPP, while paving a simpler, faster, and lower-cost path to whole-home backup

SOLRITE, a company specializing in developing, financing, and owning Virtual Power Plant Agreement (VPA) projects, together with Sonnen, a leader in Virtual Power Plant energy storage technology, announced they will now digitally integrate VPP smart breakers, managed by ADAPT Energy, into the SOLRITE  sonnen VPP. The integration marks the next evolution of sonnen and SOLRITE’s industry-leading partnership in VPP innovation, expanding beyond battery storage by transforming residential electrical loads into flexible, dispatchable grid assets for both the VPP and smart backup power applications.

Unlike traditional approaches to whole-home backup, often requiring hard-wired circuit relocation, including exclusive loads panels, the newly expanded VPP Node combines the sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to intelligently manage major household loads. This creates a more streamlined approach to whole-home backup while unlocking an entirely new source of flexibility for the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. With homeowner permission, the VPP smart breaker—controlled by ADAPT Energy—can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be flexible for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and Founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The integration addresses one of the longstanding challenges of whole-home backup: how to provide broad electrical coverage without having large household loads drain available battery capacity too quickly or cause the nano-grid to immediately fail after an off-grid transition due to excessive inrush current. With the newly expanded VPP smart breaker technology digitally integrated with the sonnen ecosystem, the system can intelligently shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns. This significantly reduces installation complexity and tedious inspections for installers, allowing crews to complete more jobs per week.

For homeowners, the system provides the convenience of a whole-home electrical configuration that intelligently manages the loads that have the greatest impact on backup duration. This work is all done in the background, meaning homeowners do not have to worry about resetting individual circuits during an outage. Instead, participating homes will see more value from their VPA program in the form of an enhanced backup power experience. Furthermore, at any time in the future, if a homeowner is interested in altering which of their VPP smart breaker-controlled loads are shed during an outage, the process to make such a change is quick and easy, requiring no electrical reconfiguration.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, President of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

The permission-based architecture aligns the interests of the homeowner, battery owner and utility. The real opportunity comes when homeowners choose to opt in to allow the breakers to participate in on-grid VPP events.

When a VPP event is dispatched, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge in concert with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads, and by using Eaton AbleEdge breakers, ADAPT achieves this right where the breaker sits, without relocation. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid. That way, the VPP can respond through both energy stored in the battery and energy that would have otherwise been consumed by the home, thereby increasing the dispatched injection into the grid, by the VPP.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, Chairman and CEO of sonnen Inc. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

#5706. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5705. Elevated demand for gas turbines could persist through 2035: Siemens Energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The U.S. midterms are not likely to slow turbine orders by data centers, company executives said, noting turbine demand is also coming from diverse industries with long-term growth trajectories.

Siemens Energy continues to book new orders at a record pace, with the company's backlog reaching 164 billion euros (about $180.4 billion) as of the end of the company's 2026 fiscal year, executives told analysts on a Nov. 11 earnings call.

New order intake rose 18.8% over the end of fiscal year 2025, according to the company's earnings release. The second half of the year also saw sharp increases in wind turbine sales from subsidiary Siemens Gamesa, allowing the company's offshore-wind-focused arm to break even for the first time in years, according to Siemens Energy President and CEO Christian Bruch.

“I do strongly believe in offshore ... being a decent-sized market in selected countries of the world, and that is still my underlying assumption — that we can play on the strengths we have in offshore,” Bruch said.

Gas turbine orders moderated somewhat in the fourth and final quarter of the fiscal year, but CFO Maria Ferraro attributed the slightly slower pace of orders to “project phasing.” Bruch said he does not believe that the growing data center backlash that manifest in the U.S. midterm elections has impacted demand for turbines or other electrical equipment. U.S. orders rose 70% year over year, with $6.7 billion in new orders booked during the fourth quarter alone, according to a Siemens Energy press release.

Globally, data center-related customers account for about 15 billion euros ($16.5 billion) of Siemen's backlog, Ferraro said.

“This translates to less than 10% of the total,” she told analysts. “So data centers are an important factor, and we certainly want to participate, but our growth story is much broader and very well diversified.”

Siemens Energy expects data centers to represent about a fifth of the growth in global demand for electricity, which the company expects to grow 60% by 2040. The electrification of transportation, manufacturing growth, and rising consumption of electricity within other buildings will drive the majority of new demand, Bruch said.

On top of this, he said he expects renewable energy capacity to triple by 2040, and he believes the world's most advanced nations need to replace about half of their existing electrical grid. As a result, Bruch said, orders for gas turbines should continue at roughly their current pace through 2030 — and possibly through 2035.

To meet this demand, Siemens Energy plans to invest about 4 billion euros ($4.4 billion) in manufacturing capacity by 2030, Bruch said. That includes a “substantial” expansion of transformer and switchgear capacity in North America, where a manufacturing hub under construction in North Carolina is already sold out for its first two years of operation, Ferraro said.

The growth of the installed gas fleet will also drive long-term maintenance needs: Siemens Energy expects revenue from gas turbine services to more than double by 2035. Twenty-year service contracts signed this year carry margins five percentage points higher than in the past, Ferraro said, thanks to increased market prices and decreased costs.

The company's Gas Services division is focused on a vertical integration strategy that has the company buying upstream suppliers to drive efficiency, Bruch said.

To further strengthen its focus on the utility sector, Siemens Energy plans to spin out its Transformation of Industry division, which serves industrial customers, as an independent company. Siemens is seeking a co-investor to help finance the launch of the new company, which Siemens plans to carve out after the parent company finishes its transition to the new Omterra brand name.

A company press release pitched the spinoff as necessary to avoid limiting what the Transformation of Industry division could achieve because the division competes for business in sectors — including, but not limited to, data centers — that move far faster than the utility sector.

#5704. Accelerating Electrification Can Unlock the Full Benefits of Spain’s Renewables Leadership

A new IEA report finds renewables are already strengthening energy security and competitiveness while cutting fuel import costs—but faster electrification, grid modernization, and greater flexibility can extend these gains across the economy.

Spain’s rapid expansion of renewable power has strengthened its energy security and competitiveness, but faster electrification across transport, buildings, and industry will be essential to meeting the country’s energy and climate goals, according to a new IEA Energy Policy Review published Oct. 5, 2026.

Between 2015 and 2025, renewables’ share of Spain’s electricity generation increased from 35% to 56%, while emissions declined by around one-fifth. These achievements have been underpinned by the country’s strong energy and climate policy framework and its well-established approach to a just transition, which links clean energy investment with jobs, regional development, and social inclusion.

Spain’s growing share of renewable power is already helping to lower wholesale electricity prices and reduce its exposure to imported fuels. Without the vast expansion of renewable capacity, its natural gas imports for power generation would have been nearly 40%, or USD 26 billion, higher in 2024. Nonetheless, imports still provided almost 70% of Spain’s total energy consumption in 2024, underscoring the need to extend the transition beyond electricity supply.

Accelerating electrification of end-uses should also be a priority, the report finds. Today, electricity accounts for just 25% of Spain’s final energy consumption, well below the country’s target of 35% by 2030. Electric vehicle uptake is relatively limited, notably for corporate fleets, while fossil-fuel heating remains widespread, and the industry faces high upfront investment costs.

A combination of taxes, levies, and network charges are keeping end-user electricity prices high, while fossil fuels face comparatively lower charges in Spain. The country is taking steps to address this, including by abolishing the tax on electricity production in 2026. Further rebalancing domestic energy price dynamics, while protecting vulnerable consumers, could strengthen incentives for households and businesses to electrify and help industry capture the competitive advantages of renewable power.

Spain has launched reforms to accelerate storage, demand-side flexibility, and adapting grid rules to a rapidly changing generation mix. Continued progress on power system infrastructure and management, alongside a more balanced mix of wind, solar, and hybrid technologies, can help Spain integrate growing shares of renewable generation while strengthening electricity security and resilience.

The review also highlights the importance of carefully managing the planned nuclear phase-out to maintain security of supply. Following the recent approval to extend the lifetime operation of the Almaraz nuclear power plant, the report recommends implementing a transparent framework for future case-by-case decisions that considers the costs and benefits for consumers and the wider power system.

Strong progress on renewable power in Spain demonstrates that its deployment can advance energy security, competitiveness, and social inclusion. The report finds that the next test will be to turn this progress into a fully integrated, flexible, and resilient energy system. By accelerating electrification, modernizing and optimizing grids, unlocking flexibility, and carefully managing its nuclear phase-out, Spain can offer valuable lessons for other countries pursuing similar energy transformations across the wider economy.

#5700. Grid operators file closely watched large load interconnection reforms at FERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The PJM Interconnection proposed revamping its interconnection process by studying large load and generation interconnection requests in a single, one-year review.

Most major U.S. grid operators have now submitted their highly anticipated proposals for interconnecting data centers and other large loads to the grid in response to directives issued by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.

FERC in June issued “show cause” orders saying that it appeared that regional transmission organizations and others lacked adequate rules for adding large loads to the grid. FERC asked them to propose reforms to address the agency’s concerns or to explain why their existing rules were just and reasonable.

FERC detailed five issues it wanted the grid operators to address in their large load interconnection rules: efficient transmission service application and study processes, including the consideration of alternative transmission technologies; prevention of cost shifts and transparency into transmission costs; co-location and behind-the-meter generation; new transmission services for flexible large loads; and a study process for generating facilities that serve “electrically proximate” large loads and co-located loads.

The move by FERC comes amid a surge in data center development in many U.S. regions and a corresponding pushback on that development, which includes concerns that existing utility customers will pay for some of the data center-related electric infrastructure.

Below is a summary of the proposals at FERC, which were filed on Monday, except for ISO New England’s. The Southwest Power Pool is slated to file its proposal by Friday.

The centerpiece of PJM’s plan is a Fast Energization Process, under which the grid operator would revamp its interconnection study process by studying large load and generation interconnection requests in a single, one-year process — about twice as fast as the current process, according to PJM.

In its filing on Monday, PJM said its proposed Fast Energization Process is designed to address “insufficiently fast interconnection processes for load and generation, parallel and duplicative study processes, speculative requests, limited visibility into the drivers of transmission upgrades, and an ongoing resource adequacy shortfall.”

The proposed process marks “the next evolution of PJM’s interconnection process and further enhancement to the currently effective Cycle process,” Donald Bielak, director of interconnection planning at PJM, told FERC in a separate filing.

PJM’s overall proposal also addresses co-location and behind-the-meter generation rules that were at issue in a separate show cause proceeding, according to the grid operator. PJM filed a separate proposal that requires large computational loads to be able to ride through grid disturbances.

PJM’s transmission owners filed their own proposed tariffs that establish rates, cost allocation, cost recovery mechanisms, financial protections, revenue-crediting requirements and transparency measures for transmission service they provide to co-located loads and large loads.

“The proposed reforms strengthen accountability and protect existing customers,” CAISO told FERC. “They improve transparency, operational visibility, technical standards, cost responsibility, and protection against speculative development.”

The CAISO and transmission owner tariff revisions create streamlined paths for large loads to connect to the electric system, according to the grid operator. “Standardized application information, deposits, milestones, study coordination, and service agreements will make the process more transparent and predictable,” CAISO said.

As part of its proposal, CAISO plans to offer new service options for flexible large loads that can cut their electric use when transmission capability is unavailable.

In its filing on Friday, ISO-NE and the region’s transmission owners said they fully addressed FERC’s show cause order. In response to the agency’s transparency concerns, ISO-NE proposed that it maintain on its website a list of the network upgrades identified for each large load, categorized by upgrade type, and the cost estimates for those upgrades.

To address FERC’s concerns about resource adequacy in New England, ISO-NE said it plans to require that large loads bring their own new capacity and that certain large loads will be excluded from ISO-NE’s capacity market’s installed capacity requirement. The grid operator plans to work with stakeholders to craft the details of its proposed framework, with a goal of filing a proposal at FERC later this year.

MISO said its proposal builds on its other reform plans recently filed at FERC: the Zero Injection Generator Interconnection Agreement, Interconnection Reliability Requirements for Large Loads and Large Load Addition Resource Study.

The just-filed proposal includes a Large Load Project Review process, new transmission service options and tariff revisions and pro forma cost recovery agreements designed to prevent cost shifting onto existing utility customers, MISO said. In a separate filing Monday, MISO proposed Grid Responsive Integration Transmission Service for large loads that can limit their electric use under certain price and reliability conditions.

MISO’s proposals at FERC represent “an important milestone, not an endpoint,” the grid operator said. “MISO remains committed to further enhancing its planning, operational, and market frameworks as experience is gained and system needs grow.”

Among other things, NYISO’s proposal provides new types of transmission services — Firm Withdrawal Service and Non-Firm Withdrawal Service — that reflect the unique characteristics of NYISO’s transmission model and of co-located large loads, large load with behind-the-meter generation, and flexible large loads, the grid operator said.

The proposal includes a compromise approach to resource adequacy requirements, called the State Exempt Load Project exception, which aims to reconcile the NYISO’s reliability responsibilities with the New York Public Service Commission resource adequacy authority, the grid operator said.

The PSC has launched a proceeding to address state-jurisdictional interconnection reforms that will likely influence the evolution of NYISO’s large load rules, the grid operator noted. New York transmission owners also filed a complementary large load proposal at FERC.

#5697. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5695. New York eyes 2030 storage goal with second bulk procurement

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The solicitation seeks up to 1 GW of energy storage, with a preference for longer-duration resources able to alleviate grid congestion and displace peaker-plant emissions in downstate New York.

Projects the state selects in this latest solicitation will need to meet stringent safety codes the New York State Fire Prevention and Building Code Council adopted in 2025, NYSERDA said Thursday.

Those codes took effect in January after a multiyear rulemaking following a spate of grid battery fires in New York in 2023.

To qualify, projects must have a 5-MW minimum power rating, be able to discharge power into the New York state grid even if not physically located in the state and use an energy storage technology that already has been successfully deployed on the grid, according to NYSERDA’s request for proposals.

The procurement also puts some guardrails around project duration and location. Two-hour resources may account for no more than 10% of the total 3 GW of planned bulk storage procurements, for example.

NYSERDA is targeting durations longer than eight hours for at least 20% of the resources in each procurement, though this month’s RFP suggests that is not a binding requirement if viable longer-duration capacity doesn’t bid into the program.

Seven of the eight awardees in the first procurement were 4-hour projects. The eighth was an 8-hour, 140-MW/1,120-MWh lithium battery installation jointly proposed by Grid Connected Infrastructure and Neoen US in far western New York.

In terms of location, NYSERDA aims to have a minimum of 30% of the 3-GW total bulk storage procurement in the New York Independent System Operator’s Zone J, which covers the five boroughs of New York City. A cumulative 5% minimum target covers NYISO Zones G, H, I and K, covering Long Island and most of the Hudson River Valley south of Albany.

In the first procurement, five of the eight awardees brought a total of 610 MW/2,440 MWh to Zones G, H, I, J or K.

Energy storage deployments in these zones have greater potential to reduce emissions from fossil fuel peaker plants, provide benefits to state-designated disadvantaged communities, or both, NYSERDA said.

One of the agency’s selection criteria is a project’s “ability to address system reliability needs and offset reliance on specific, electrically proximate Peaker generation,” according to the RFP. Another is the potential to reduce curtailment of electrically proximate renewable generation.

Eddie Bautista, executive director of the NYC Environmental Justice Alliance, cheered the procurement’s emphasis on reducing emissions from fossil fuel power plants.

“As New York faces the urgent need to keep our grid more reliable, resilient, and affordable, batteries provide the opportunity to retire and replace dirty and expensive fossil fuel peaker plants for environmental justice communities,” Bautista said in a statement.

Selected projects will have access to two state-backed financial tools to mitigate development risk and reduce revenue uncertainty, according to the RFP.

One is an “index storage credit” paid by NYSERDA and modeled on New York’s renewable energy certificate framework. One ISC is equal to one MWh of daily discharge capability, meaning a 100-MWh battery can create 100 ISCs per day if it remains fully available. An ISC’s value is the difference between an eligible project’s strike price — the approximate revenue threshold for commercial viability — and the approximate wholesale and capacity market revenues the project could earn under normal operations, according to the RFP.

The other is NY Green Bank. The New York Department of Public Service earlier this year ordered the green bank to use 25% of its capital for energy storage financing over the next five years, NYSERDA said. That capital is available for development-stage financing, long-lead equipment financing, construction loans, term loans and more, NYSERDA said.

#5693. Batteries included: A practical checklist for storage owners, operators to boost reliability

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Storage operators can utilize defined escalation pathways, telemetry monitoring and situational awareness tools to coordinate when grid conditions require support, writes Xpansiv’s Brian Fellon.

Brian Fellon is senior vice president at Xpansiv, a market platform for energy and environmental commodities.

Winter Storm Fern hit Texas in January 2026 with extreme cold and hazardous precipitation. But unlike Winter Storm Uri in 2021, the lights stayed on. The Electric Reliability Council of Texas’ post-event report captured how the grid remained intact without Energy Emergency Alerts, public conservation calls or systemwide outages.

During Fern, ERCOT’s battery energy storage resources were indispensable for covering peak-demand reserves, while all resource types contributed to meeting broader system demand. The critical lesson is that grid performance is highly dependent on coordinating nascent technologies and real-time operations with legacy generation assets. That lesson is now being tested at scale across the country.

The U.S. electricity sector is undergoing its most abrupt transformation since deregulation. After nearly two decades of flat demand, load growth is accelerating from data centers, transportation and industrial electrification.

The forward demand curve has never been this steep, and ERCOT's long-range planning assumptions contemplate peak load reaching more than four times today’s record levels. Across the PJM Interconnection footprint, forecasted load growth is raising expectations that tens of gigawatts of new supply may be needed over the coming decade to maintain system reliability.

This demand jump is colliding with aging grid infrastructure, constrained transmission, record-long interconnection queues and a shifting policy landscape. These factors are reconfiguring wholesale power markets and revaluing baseload and dispatchable generation, while establishing battery storage as essential grid infrastructure.

Batteries are often described as a foundational solution because they respond instantly, start and stop without thermal plants’ mechanical constraints, and uniquely shift energy from off-peak to on-peak periods. In practice, the grid does not simply benefit from batteries as fixed capacity in the conventional sense. It benefits from dynamic, flexible battery storage systems that are specifically configured to accelerate responsiveness, along with the telemetry, data feeds, market interfaces and 24/7 readiness required to perform under normal and stressed conditions alike.

That distinction matters as storage scales, owners compete for arbitrage opportunities and electricity markets evolve.

In organized wholesale markets, dispatch is not a single daily event. It’s a sequence of day-ahead scheduling, intraday adjustments and real-time dispatch, translated into 5-minute price intervals. For chemically-constrained assets like batteries, timelines are crucial because spot market decisions are inseparable from current and future state-of-charge, known as SoC. Every megawatt discharged at a given time affects what can be delivered later.

Fortunately, policy and market design are adapting to support this reality. FERC’s Order No. 841 requires ISOs/RTOs to remove barriers to storage participation and adopt participation models that recognize storage’s physical and operational characteristics. That mandate expanded the range of products storage can provide, with more options for revenue production.

Although not governed by FERC, ERCOT went even further than Order 841. In December 2025, ERCOT implemented Real-Time Co-optimization Plus Batteries (RTC+B), which co-optimizes energy and reserves in real time while accounting for what battery resources can actually deliver. One month later, during Winter Storm Fern, ERCOT credited RTC+B with helping redistribute ancillary services across the system during a localized transmission emergency. The same change that helped ERCOT lean on batteries during Fern also changed how those batteries earn revenue. As these rules open new ways for storage to participate, the mix of market products that pay off keeps shifting.

Market participants and grid operators need timely, accurate information about what a battery can deliver under variable constraints. Unreliable data or inconsistent operations aren’t theoretical problems, because they show up directly in project economics after commercial operations begin.

Similarly, optimization only creates value if it’s converted into timely, accurate and compliant market submissions. There has never been a greater need for validated bid and offer workflows, automated checks against asset constraints and market rules, reliable ISO/RTO connectivity, clear audit trails, and the ability to rapidly adjust trades as forecasts, prices, outages and SoC conditions change.

The same need for accuracy and coordination extends into real-time operations. Wholesale markets and grid conditions don’t simply pause at 5 p.m. when the workday ends for most electricity consumers. Scarcity pricing, forced outages and weather disruptions often happen during off-hours. Sophisticated storage operators utilize defined escalation pathways, telemetry monitoring and situational awareness tools to coordinate instantly when grid conditions require immediate support. A practical checklist for battery owners and operators includes:

ERCOT has shown what adaptive market design can deliver. Other markets should follow.

Operators can only do so much on their own. Market rules decide how much value flexible resources can actually deliver, and those rules are only as good as a market's culture and willingness to keep improving them.

ERCOT treats market design as ongoing work. When storage grew from a niche resource to a core part of the fleet, the rules were changed to match it. When large-load requests began reshaping the interconnection picture, regulators moved promptly to change how those loads are studied, prioritized and approved. Not every change has been perfect, and some have been contentious. But the gap between identifying a problem and putting a fix in place has been relatively short.

Other grid regulators could benefit by adapting this model to their regions, while avoiding preconceptions that this can only work in Texas. Much of the country runs on a slower cycle, where reforms move through multi-year stakeholder processes, compliance filings and phased implementations.

Implementation has also diverged enough that an operator running assets in three markets is effectively running three separate businesses. Closer alignment on state-of-charge accounting, telemetry requirements and bid formats would lower the cost of entry for smaller operators and reduce operational congestion for everyone.

ERCOT's design doesn't need to be copied everywhere, but its pace of innovation should be. Regions that move from a problem to a fix in months (not years) will attract investment and maintain the reliability that others will eventually fight for.

The era of static demand is over; as the grid is being rewired, success will favor those who are prepared to execute in real time.

#5692. A Radically Different Approach to AI

About forty years ago, I decided I would develop artificial intelligence in my spare time. I knew about the Turing test, and I believed that a computer tricking people into thinking it was human did not indicate intelligence. There was a program called ELIZA that did that already, and it was clearly not intelligent at all.

I wanted to produce real AI, a type of thought that might take some effort for a human being to figure out. Maybe I would have to ask the computer how it reached the conclusion it did. But it would do so using logic a human could follow, examining each step. It would be a process a person could examine.

My goal, I believed, would best be reached by first inventing something I called Artificial Cognition. This would be a native way for computers to deal with data in the form of bit-fields, which were native data for computers, as translations of human words, in the same way that computers use IEEE bitwise representations of real numbers. Once this step was done, it would be easy to string words together in sentences, which could be directly translated into and out of human language. And the sentences would tell computers things they could “understand.”

With such translations, a computer could translate a work, such as Shakespeare’s Henry V, into its own language. Then it could translate the text into whatever language we might want. Some subtleties would be lost, but human translators do the same thing.

My idea was that computers should be able to work on ideas natively. At first, I thought this would require a very wide CPU, with registers that might be 256 bits, or even wider. In time, I came to believe that it would be best to stick to CPUs that were in use. As 64-bit CPUs were clearly coming soon, when I was working on this in 1986, I decided to use that data size. I also thought I might have to design a CPU with an additional instruction set, but I never got to the point of needing one.

So my design goal ended up having a word to translate almost any word (or even phrases) of English, occupying 64 bits of memory. And these could be understood and manipulated in standard registers of a standard computer.

I believe that I did achieve that goal, though the project never went into commercial use. A patent on the final design was issued by the US PTO, but that was long ago, and as I understand it, the whole thing is now in the public domain.

My reason for writing about this is that AI is a very contentious issue today. Anthropic has even warned potential stockholders in its IPO documents that its product could have catastrophic effects if things go wrong, possibly to the point of terminating all human life. I think it even points out that a major part of the value of its stock would be lost if there were no human beings left to buy it.

So I started to work on what I called Artificial Cognition in about 1986. Doing this in my spare time was a long slog. I went through a bunch of design iterations, starting, as I said, with 256-bit words. I won’t go through the work that had to be done determining how many designs there were and why each would not work. Each attempt took about a year or more, but eventually I got to the point of having a design in my fifth try.

I will give a simple explanation of the design in this article. Before I do, I will describe what the design could do at the time the patent was applied for, over twenty years ago. To reiterate, it could do these things:

Eventually, I defined about 10,000 words for this design. Doing this was a full-time project that took two years, during which I also tested the design. At a point where I had about 2,500 words, I built a simple program that took two inputs of English words, translated them into the computer words, compared them to see that they were valid for compares, and held them for the record. Then it went through the entire vocabulary, taking each word (or set of words) in turn and finding any word that compared to it in precisely the same way the first two words compared.

This might sound like it did far more work than it needed to do. Because most words are not comparable, most compares produced faults and were rejected immediately. So the process did not have to do the millions of compares one might imagine. This was even easier than one might suppose, because whole huge sets of words could be rejected together. So if the input words were a pair like “train” and “airplane,” all words for colors or pieces of clothing could be rejected as sets.

One thing about this that is interesting is how it is done in the computer CPU. With a pair of 64-bit registers loaded, an XOR instruction will result in the difference between the two, or it will produce a fault. The XOR requires a single clock cycle. Then, with the word from the dictionary loaded into a register, a single XOR with the difference produces the word that gives the analogy, or it will produce a fault.

If there is a fault, we go on to the next word in the vocabulary. Otherwise, we have an analogy. In practice, for example, I ran this using original inputs of “car” and “small passenger plane.” The results came back, including the examples following, for “as a car is to a small passenger plane” (please note that each set of words in quotes is a single 64-bit word for the computer):

There were many other results, but the combination of lion and eagle stood out so clearly that I looked specifically to see what the comparison entailed. Why “lion” and “eagle,” but not “tiger” and “owl,” or “house cat” and “hawk”? There were about half a dozen predatory land animals and at least four predatory birds in that small dictionary, but “lion” and “eagle” were the only pair returned by the program. What gives?

It turned out that things had gone precisely the way they should have. The reason that “lion” and “eagle” were chosen while others were not is that the definitions of these two had connotative values as symbols of authority. That information was in their 64-bit definitions. You can do a lot with 64 bits, if you know how.

This work was done in a 32-bit machine, simulating the 64-bit machines just about to come to market. With registers loaded (in a 64-bit machine), each compare would take one clock cycle. So the whole thing, with computers pre-loaded, would take two clocks. In a 2-GHz machine, the set of compares took one billionth of a second. One billionth of a second in a 64-bit machine anyone could buy. One billionth of a second in a Raspberry Pi costing $45.

AI companies are bringing products to market that they warn could end the lives of all human beings. Anthropic says its IPO, which comes out on Thursday, will establish the value of the company at $1.5 trillion (NOTE: with a “T”). But Anthropic is warning that a risk for stockholders is that it could kill all humanity, including you, and your entire family, even if you live in a modest home and get no benefit from the company in any way. And everyone else could go with you. That, I read, is what the IPO papers say, but some people believe it could be very worthwhile to do.

I believe this is not artificial intelligence. It might be artificial neglect. Maybe even artificial willful ignorance. Maybe, with artificial rational thought, we could produce artificial due diligence, but AI is not known for rationality.

I wonder how high the value of Anthropic stock will go if only computers could buy it because there were no people left. Of course, without a Fed made up of humans, they might figure out a way to have infinite money to play with.

The iteration of my invention that works depends on 64 bits being divided into categories, each represented by 8 bits. These are organized into eight supersets (3 bits), each with four groups of subsets (2 bits), with each group having eight basic subsets (3 bits). The total is 8 bits per set value, so there are 256 categories.

Of the eight supersets in each category, one is “agent.” This is anything that does anything. It is divided into four groups of subsets, two of which are “living thing” and “mechanism.” These are divided by type into subsets, so “living thing” includes “animal” and “plant,” among others.

The category “location and time” is similar. This is divided into groups of subsets, one of which is “geography.” Within this are eight subsets, among which are “air,” “land,” “water,” “space,” and four others.

We can see the basic way the vocabulary works by taking these for the most significant 8 bits in a word, the field I call “BAS,” and the next most significant field, which I call “ALT,” because I seem to be unable to come up with a better 3-letter name for it. (Sorry, I do get a bit dull sometimes. Often.)

Using just these two fields, we can see that a car, a train, a taxi, a bus, and a bicycle are all in a group, “land vehicle” (for ALT and BAS). An airplane of any type is an “air vehicle.” So are a blimp and a kite carrying prayers to the clouds asking them to make rain.

Similarly, a lion, tiger, bear, or dog would be a “land animal,” and most birds are “air animal.” Please note that I say “most birds.” They are not divided into biological groups, but according to where they function. Some birds only function on land. Similarly, a bat is an “air animal.” And Melville was right, Moby Dick was a “water animal,” just like any fish.

This broadly divides our dictionary into potential word values using 16 bits. This would limit us to just 65,536 potential words, if they were 16 bits long. Fortunately, we are only a quarter of the way through the 64 bits of our potential word definitions.

The next parts of the definition use 8 bits for each of several fields. A SRC field could represent where something comes from, why it has come into existence, or even what fuels it. For a car, this could be a value that decodes as “chemical,” or a value meaning “electricity.” For a sail-powered car, this field might mean “air.” For just “car,” with no motive force specified, this would be left to the value 0, meaning “any” or “whatever.”

An MTD field represents how it is operated. For a car, this could be decoded by a word meaning “engine” or “motor.” That is, unless the car is powered by sail, in which case neither of these values would work, but something other than 0 would be used, because 0 could mean a superset that engines and motors would match to. In the case of a buggy, this field would say “animal.”

A DST field may mean a destination or purpose. For a car, this could be a value meaning “human being,” for what it carries.

In addition to these, there is a CXT field, which can carry context. In the case of a lion or eagle, this is a value meaning “authority,” which is why they matched to each other but nothing else in the test.

So far, I have described six fields. They provide us with far more potential words than any human language has by a huge factor. That should be enough, but there are other uses for bits. For example, I decided some time ago that there should be eight bits assigned to negation bits, each corresponding to a single field in the word.

Clearly, the values that make up the fields in the word are very fuzzy in their meanings. However, in the design, applying how these are to be interpreted has to be done strictly according to the sets of words involved. Implementing such a design means rules have to be developed according what part of the vocabulary is being worked on.

Altogether, I had a bit over 10,000 words defined about twenty years ago. I believed that these could be built up just a bit for software that could read a dictionary. As it happened, no one was willing to look at the invention to evaluate it, so funding of my invention ceased.

Before I finish up, I should mention that this sort of AI does not do images or other types of jobs that require pattern matching. That isn’t what I set out to do. I always assumed that such things would come from people interested in them. But I wasn’t interested.

I have a problem with the AI being produced commercially today. All of it looks to me like it is based on the same sort of pattern matching that is done by a human mind to some extent. And this is a problem because human beings are not naturally rational creatures. So AI companies are basically building irrational products.

As for Anthropic and the other organizations that claim to have products that threaten human life, that looks like artificial insanity requiring artificial restraint. Or artificial constraint. At least until someone can develop artificial ethics and artificial caution. I don’t think Anthropic has that yet. But I think there is a way to do it.

Featured photo: Fuzzy definition, by Luke Jones, via Unsplash

A retired computer engineer, George Harvey researches and writes on energy and climate change, maintains a daily blog (geoharvey.com), and has a weekly hour-long TV show, Energy Week with George Harvey and Tom Finnell. In addition to those found at CleanTechnica, many of his articles can be found at greenenergytimes.org.

#5689. Most states fail ‘community power’ test, advocacy group says

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Institute for Local Self-Reliance issued letter grades based on the presence or absence of policies it sees as supportive of virtual power plants and local control over energy systems.

The scorecards were released shortly before the midterm elections, which saw mixed results for community power advocates and proposals.

In Georgia, Democrats now hold a majority on the Public Service Commission and are widely expected to push back on Southern Company’s plans to add some 10 GW of new generation, mostly natural gas, to meet rising data center demand.

Meanwhile, in Ann Arbor, Michigan, voters rejected a bid to separate from investor-owned DTE Energy and create a public power utility.

On the community power scorecard, the seven highest-scoring states were California, Colorado, Connecticut, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Jersey and Oregon.

The scorecard values policies that aim to make it easier to deploy and monetize distributed resources, including state community solar programs, customer-friendly net metering and interconnection frameworks, streamlined solar permitting, and virtual power plants.

Other criteria relate to what ILSR calls “utility accountability,” such as transmission right-of-first refusal, restructured energy markets, laws or regulations curtailing customer disconnections, and restrictions on utilities charging customers for new infrastructure during construction.

Though state lawmakers or regulators have significant influence over most of ILSR’s metrics, some also have a national nexus.

For example, a Federal Energy Regulatory Commission order in 2011 opened transmission-construction bidding to third parties, eroding vertically integrated utilities’ access to potentially lucrative infrastructure projects.

In April, Entergy, Xcel Energy and seven other utilities asked FERC to halt competitive bidding for transmission projects in the Midcontinent Independent System Operator and Southwest Power Pool regions, which cover parts of more than 20 states. They said the move would accelerate transmission development; competitive bidding advocates countered that it would raise project costs that ratepayers would ultimately bear.

If passed, the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, could shift those rules.

Among BAAJA’s sweeping reforms to energy project permitting and regulation are a wholesale elimination of incumbent utilities’ federal right of first refusal and stricter regional transmission planning requirements.

Even states with relatively high marks on community power have room for improvement, ILSR said.

California, for example, earned B’s for solar permitting and and virtual power plants, but a D on community solar and F grades on disconnection prevention and market structure.

Texas, with an overall F grade on community power, scored a C on net metering and A grades on third-party DER ownership, right of first refusal and municipal franchise authority, which allows cities to negotiate better contracts with electric and gas utilities.

ILSR’s virtual power plant scorecard showed a similar dynamic.

No state met all of the group’s benchmarks for a perfect score, whose requirements include variable compensation based on demand and other factors; third-party aggregator participation; increased access or benefits for low- and moderate-income customers; multiple technology types; and multiple customer classes.

Six states earned a ‘B’ grade for their VPP policies: California, Colorado, Connecticut, Hawai’i, Maryland and New York.

New York and California’s VPP programs are the country’s best, according to ILSR. New York’s only deficiency was not prohibiting “participant clawbacks and fees,” such as early exit fees or penalties for failing to meet minimum performance standards.

California’s only ding was not requiring DER compensation or access parity regardless of whether a resource is owned by the customer, a third-party aggregator or a utility.

#5685. Data center slowdown would raise risks for utility investments

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk after the midterm elections, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, according to a report from Orennia.

Utilities could be left holding the bill for billions in infrastructure spending committed in anticipation of artificial intelligence data centers that are delayed, downsized or canceled, industry observers and experts told Utility Dive.

Opposition to data center construction has crossed traditional party lines, while Democratic gains in the midterms are adding pressure on earlier-stage investments across key markets. Together, these dynamics raise the stakes for project approvals and utility cost recovery, they say.

“From a financing perspective, prolonged uncertainty will increase the cost of capital, reduce available leverage and make investors more hesitant to commit significant development dollars before power is secured,” said Andrejka Bernatova, managing partner of investment firm Dynamix Capital Partners, which invests in energy infrastructure.

Even in regions that theoretically have enough capacity to accommodate new large loads, projects may struggle to secure a credible path to power on the timelines they need.

“Interconnection, permitting, and financing all matter, but they are part of the same execution question of whether the team can get reliable power to the site and actually deliver,” Bernatova said. “A customer commitment or a large announced capacity number does not answer that by itself.”

Ultimately, Bernatova expects investors to favor markets that offer certainty around power availability and timelines, potentially eroding the competitive advantage of markets like Texas, which until recently attracted substantial data center investment.

Over the summer, Gov. Greg Abbott, R, announced a pause on new data center interconnections pending an audit of the queue. In the midterms, he lost his bid for reelection to Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who has pledged to maintain pressure on the industry after taking office next year.

Nearly all of the 46 municipal ballot measures tracked by Bloomberg that addressed data center development resulted in restrictions, moratoriums or bans.

That unfavorability extends to a substantial share of the development pipeline. An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, where incoming Democratic governors campaigned on pausing or restricting construction, according to a report from energy analytics firm Orennia that was shared with Utility Dive.

Texas accounts for nearly half of the pre-construction in development in the U.S., so a statewide approval freeze would have an outsized impact on the national buildout, but the “impact of delayed or unrealized data center build-out on power prices varies widely across technologies and markets,” Orennia said.

If 20% of projected data center demand in the Electric Reliability Council of Texas region fails to materialize, solar and wind generators could see their average wholesale revenue fall 38% and 37%, respectively, according to the report. Implied heat rates, a measure of the economics of gas-fired generation relative to fuel costs, could decline 39%. In the same scenario, two-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads could fall below new-build break-even requirements.

In PJM, delaying half of the planned data center buildout by two years could reduce four-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads by 16% and lower solar and wind capture prices and implied heat rates by about 12% through 2030, the modeling suggests.

The election results are unlikely to produce sweeping federal AI legislation but could lead to congressional hearings and greater transparency requirements, according to D.C.-based research and advisory firm Capstone. Its analysts expect much of the policy debate to take place at state utility commissions and local siting boards.

Despite a proliferation of large-load tariffs over the past year, safeguards may still leave customers exposed in the event of a slowdown, some say.

“Existing tariffs and regulatory frameworks likely won’t fully protect customers because they were designed for a different century,” Louisa Eberle, principal at the Regulatory Assistance Project, warned in a written statement.

Eberle also called for reforms to utility business practices “to turn this data center surge into updated infrastructure, cleaner energy and a better deal for average customers.”

The push for lower electricity bills is prompting policymakers to look more closely at how utilities use existing grid infrastructure.

Richard Caperton, vice president of policy at SPAN, a maker of smart electrical panels, anticipates greater emphasis on data center projects that can be served without major new grid investments.

“I’m really thrilled to see governors like Abigail Spanberger [in Virginia] campaign on affordability and then embrace utilization as the solution,” he said.

In April, Gov. Spanberger signed legislation requiring Dominion Energy and Appalachian Power to report how effectively they use existing grid infrastructure. The State Corporation Commission can use those metrics to establish improvement targets and evaluate utility spending.

“Winning candidates who emphasized lowering electricity prices are already looking for ways to deliver on their promises quickly,” Caperton said. “I expect that will lead to more policies and programs that boost grid utilization being ratified and implemented in early 2027. How those policies affect data center development and utility investments will vary by state.”

Stephen Rose, CEO of infrastructure software provider Render Networks, said he expects to see a “pause and necessary shakeout” in data center development.

“Fully permitted projects with real capital will keep moving,” Rose said. “The speculative end takes the hit: developers land-banking without firm power, and the contractors who staffed up for projects that won't break ground.”

Rose cited AEP Ohio's large-load tariff, approved in July 2025, which requires new data centers above 25 MW to pay for at least 85% of requested capacity for up to 12 years, with exit fees and financial guarantees. The utility’s data-center pipeline subsequently fell by more than half from more than 30 GW to about 13.

Rose expressed concern, however, that a broad slowdown wouldn’t affect hyperscalers as much as it would the businesses and people that build data centers. He recalled delays in Louisiana's Broadband Equity, Access and Deployment program in 2025, where service providers bore the costs of equipment and crews while waiting for approvals.

“When timelines stalled, the hit didn't land on big tech balance sheets. It landed on local drilling, civil and fiber contractors,” Rose said. “A data center slowdown hits the same people first, and Texas risks the same fallout.”

Permitting hurdles could make data center developers more selective about where they build and under what terms.

Andy Roehr, managing director in the tax advisory firm Baker Tilly’s energy and infrastructure practice, sees developers as more likely to redirect capital than abandon projects – though that could lead to a winnowing of queues in some jurisdictions.

“A developer is typically evaluating three, four or five sites at any given moment, so when permitting, regulation or local opposition makes one location difficult to read, the project is far more likely to be delayed or redirected than canceled,” Roehr said.

Roehr said states and municipalities competing for projects that can no longer move forward elsewhere could find themselves in a “bidding war.”

"The most interesting thing I am seeing is that these moratoria are creating a developer’s market since they have the assets, tax revenues and jobs that communities want or need,” he continued.

Some developers may shift to “less optimal sites where projects can get done at an effective price point and time-to-market, but the regulatory and legislative environment is less hostile,” he said.

Changes in expected demand could also lead to repricing across the supply chain, from gas turbines and processor racks to construction costs.

Another uncertainty is the possibility of federal permitting reform, which could make it easier to build certain kinds of energy projects.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, which advanced in the Senate last week, faces a difficult path to passage following the midterm elections, analysts say.

Democrats are split over the proposal, with some expressing concerns over changes to environmental protections in the bill and urging party leaders to wait until next year when the Democrats will control both the House and Senate to pursue their priorities.

Roehr said in his view, the BAAJA takes an “all of the above” approach and would be as meaningful to solar as to nuclear or natural gas. But what the industry needs most, he said, is stability.

“Developers, investors and host communities need certainty to keep committing capital and local resources to these projects,” Roehr said. “These are 20-, 40-, even 60-year investments, and if the environment keeps whipsawing then jobs don’t appear, tax payments don’t appear and capital moves on — there will be no winners.”

#5684. Mobile Energy Hubs: How Chery Auto’s Smart Energy Ecosystem Supports the Development of Smart Cities Worldwide

WUHU, China — Following the 2026 Chery International User Summit, Chery Auto is pressing ahead with its new energy ecosystem strategy. Entering the Globalization 3.0 phase, the company is deploying a mix of energy solutions to meet mobility energy needs around the world. As electric vehicles integrate with power grids, Chery Auto is developing a smart energy ecosystem that transforms new energy vehicles (NEVs) into dispatchable distributed mobile energy nodes, supporting grid management, sustainable urban development, and local energy resource utilization.

Unlike conventional charging, which can increase peak demand, V2G offers a flexible alternative. Vehicle-to-grid (V2G) enables bidirectional flow, allowing vehicles to store off-peak electricity and return power during peak demand. For users, this creates opportunities to participate in energy management and potentially generate value. With participation depending on commercial feasibility and user confidence, Chery Group’s global user base exceeding 21 million provides a foundation for V2G deployment.

Chery Auto is validating its energy ecosystem across homes, communities and industrial parks. Its household Energy Cube integrates solar, storage and bidirectional charging to support electricity supply during exceptional conditions. In Wuhu, a large-scale AC V2G demonstration station enables private vehicles to support grid management through onboard chargers with limited modifications. In Spain, an industrial park microgrid uses green electricity to reduce energy costs, fossil fuel dependence, and carbon emissions.

Chery Auto’s Volt-dragon Ultra-Fast Charging technology targets 500 km of range in five minutes. Its hybrid engine has achieved 48.57% thermal efficiency, while the planned Rhino S solid-state battery targets 400 Wh/kg energy density. Chery’s Rhino Battery prioritizes fast charging, longevity and safety, addressing battery degradation and thermal management concerns associated with V2G charging cycles.

Chery Auto is developing modular solar, storage, charging, and V2G solutions tailored to regional infrastructure. In Europe, electricity markets provide opportunities for distributed energy participation. Mature markets integrate vehicles into grid management, while regions with weaker infrastructure can prioritize microgrids before introducing V2G.

Through applications spanning households, communities, and industrial parks, Chery Auto is extending automotive-grade engineering into energy infrastructure. Its approach supports distributed energy networks using locally generated electricity and managing energy flows, positioning Chery to participate in future smart cities.

#5683. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5682. DOE-funded transmission projects could boost East-West transfer capacity 440%

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The latest funding announcement is for a 1.8-GW, high-voltage, direct current transmission line in Wyoming, an area rich in wind resources and slated for major grid expansion projects.

The U.S. Department of Energy has issued Grid United a $2.8 billion conditional loan commitment to finance a 1.8-GW, high-voltage, direct current transmission line in Wyoming — the latest in a series of funding decisions that could add about 7 GW of transfer capacity between the Western and Eastern interconnections.

In 2021, the DOE said a collection of back-to-back converter stations enabled just 1,320 MW of electricity to flow between the two networks. Increasing transfer capacity could save money and boost grid reliability and resilience, it says.

The Wyoming Intertie project would run about 106 miles between a converter station in Wyoming’s Platte County and an existing substation in Carbon County, linking the Southwest Power Pool and the Western Electricity Coordinating Council. The area around the project is rich in wind resources.

Also, that part of Wyoming “sits at the confluence of major grid expansion projects in WECC and SPP, which makes it a particularly advantageous location for a grid-to-grid connection,” Ashley Kang, a Grid United spokesperson, said in an email.

Grid United expects to complete the permitting for the project in 2029 and begin construction in 2030. The company plans to bring the project online in 2032 or 2033, depending on its procurement of needed HVDC equipment, according to a company fact sheet.

The Houston-based transmission company is developing three other projects that would also add lines between the Eastern and Western interconnections. They include the 3-GW North Plains Connector project between North Dakota and Montana; the 1.8-GW Three Corners Connector between Oklahoma and Colorado; and the 1.5-GW Pecos West project between SPP’s footprint in Texas and WECC in New Mexico.

In separate funding decisions, DOE has recently agreed to help finance the Three Corners and North Plains connector projects. Taken together, the department has agreed to help fund about 6.6 GW of East-West transmission infrastructure by Grid United.

DOE has also agreed to help fund a 490-MW expansion project between Colorado and Texas. The project, planned by Xcel Energy, could be expanded to a total of 2 GW.

The DOE's draft 2026 National Transmission Needs Study found that average price differentials along the seam between SPP's eastern footprint and the Western Interconnection ranged from $18/MWh to $33/MWh from 2012 to 2023.

The Wyoming Intertie project could produce $39 billion in cost savings for ratepayers, help meet growing demand and improve grid resilience during periods of high electric use and extreme weather, DOE said Tuesday. Closing conditions on the loan include Grid United obtaining full project right-of-way, contracts for capacity on the line, and federal, state and local permits.

The conditional, 30-year DOE loan would finance 80% of the project’s construction costs, reducing interest expenses by about $500 million compared with typical financing sources, according to Grid United.

The low-cost, federal loan can be assigned to utilities that may buy capacity on the transmission line, translating into savings for ratepayers, Grid United said.

Increasing transfer capacity between the Eastern and Western interconnections could reduce wholesale power costs, SPP said in August when its board approved a plan to study potential transmission additions between the regions.

#5681. COP31 Leaders’ Summit Spotlights Electrification & Transition Away from Fossil Fuels

Antalya, Türkiye — The COP31 Leaders’ Summit concluded today, with several new announcements and speeches from heads of state and the UN Secretary-General. Developments included country endorsements of the COP Presidency’s 35×35 electrification pledge, growing support for the transition away from fossil fuels, and new pledges from philanthropies to the Tropical Forest Forever Facility, among others.

Following is a statement from Ani Dasgupta, President and CEO, World Resources Institute:

“One message came across loud and clear from world leaders: climate action has shifted from an environmental movement to a core economic and security imperative. The conversation is moving beyond high-level commitments to the practical challenges of how to transition away from fossil fuels while supporting workers and communities and how to put entire economies on a cleaner, more resilient path.

“Electrification and the transition away from fossil fuels rightly took center stage. These are two vital, intertwined goals, and progress on one must reinforce the other. Early support for the 35×35 electrification pledge is encouraging, but many more countries need to endorse it and turn their commitments into credible national plans backed by concrete policies and investment.

“President Lula deserves credit for keeping the transition away from fossil fuels at the top of the international agenda and delivering a global roadmap that countries can adapt to their own circumstances. Australia and Türkiye now have an opportunity to lead by example, putting forward their own transition plans and encouraging others to follow.

“Vulnerable countries delivered an equally clear message: access to affordable finance is critical to them implementing their climate ambition. High borrowing costs and unsustainable debt are holding back investment in the very countries that need it most. With the $1.3 trillion finance goal established, the focus is shifting to how all sources of finance can work together to deliver investment at scale and how we ensure affordable capital reaches those who need it most. There are encouraging signs, including an influx of pledges from philanthropies for the Brazil-initiated Tropical Forests Forever Facility.

“Adaptation and early warning systems received much-needed attention after a year of crushing heatwaves, fires, and floods. But that attention now needs to turn to the investment in systemic resilience as the best way to make economies and societies stronger and improve people’s lives. Building more resilient cities was a major focus and will take deep collaboration between national governments, cities, and states. Cities are on the front lines of delivering the climate solutions and essential services that make people’s lives better, and demonstrating these benefits is critical to accelerating the transition. Local leaders are on the front lines of climate impacts and the delivery of essential services that make communities safer, healthier, and more resilient, yet too often lack the resources to respond. Cities and local communities especially need greater access to adaptation finance.

“It was disappointing to see no dedicated ocean event at the Leaders’ Summit, despite over 90% of countries including ocean action in national climate plans. The ocean is on the front lines of the climate crisis, but it could also be one of our greatest allies in tackling it. Ocean-based solutions can provide up to one-third of the emissions cuts needed by 2050 to keep 1.5°C within reach. This ambition for ocean action must be reflected in any COP31 cover decision and translate into concrete progress, especially for vulnerable coastal and island nations.

“Some leaders also delivered a resounding defense of multilateralism, even amid deep geopolitical tensions. International cooperation on climate may be changing shape, but it is still alive and needed now more than ever. Even in a fractured world, leaders recognize that no country can secure its economic future, build resilience, or prosper without working together on shared climate and environmental challenges. That spirit of cooperation must now carry through the negotiations and deliver a strong, ambitious outcome at COP31.”

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org
