#5680. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5678. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5676. Minister O’Brien Announces Completion of Over 300,000 Home Energy Upgrades with Government Support Since 2019

Minister for Climate, Energy and the Environment Darragh O’Brien has last week announced that more than 300,000 home energy upgrades have been completed with government support since 2019. The more than 300,000 energy upgrades have benefitted well over a quarter of a million individual houses (271,000). Over 42,000 of these were vulnerable, energy-poor households that benefitted from fully-funded (free) energy upgrades. All of these homes are now warmer and healthier, with lower energy bills. On this journey, more than 115,000 of these homes achieved a B2 energy rating or better. Over 22,000 heat pumps were installed.

Since 2019, SEAI (Sustainable Energy Authority of Ireland) schemes have provided over €2 billion for communities and households – including the aforementioned home energy improvements. Looking across all schemes (if expenditure on Local Authority upgrades by the Department of Housing is included) the spend (in monetary terms) on vulnerable households and those at risk of energy poverty each year now accounts for more than 60% of the overall spend.

Momentum is growing, following the introduction of new measures, higher grants, and enhanced eligibility announced in January and again in October. The changes included the introduction of grants for windows and doors, increased grant for heat pumps, higher grants for certain Department of Social Protection payment recipients, as well as new supports for certain landlords.

So far this year, SEAI has received more than 120,000 applications, more than double the volume at the same point last year.

Commenting on this milestone, Minister O’Brien said:

“I am delighted that we have achieved this really significant milestone, whereby we have now completed more than 300,000 home energy upgrades. The recently enhanced measures to the home energy upgrade schemes are helping to deliver meaningful and long-lasting reductions in energy costs for households and businesses right across the country. Depending on the measure installed and the characteristics of the property, households could benefit from savings of up to nearly €900 per year. These grants are on top of the existing supports for energy upgrades, including insulation, window and door replacements and heat pumps. When installed as part of whole home upgrade, homeowners can save over €1,100 per year on their energy bills.

“Importantly, more than 42,000 households have benefited from fully funded upgrades through the warmer homes scheme, ensuring that those most in need of energy supports are not left behind in the transition to a cleaner more sustainable energy future. There are a number of recently enhanced measures to support households at risk of energy poverty, medically vulnerable customers, lower-income homeowners and renters. These will help to ensure that those who need support most can access the benefits of lower energy bills and warmer healthier homes.

“These enhanced measures, developed through the work of the National Energy Affordability Taskforce which I chair, form part of the Government’s broader efforts to improve energy affordability, strengthen energy security and support the transition to a cleaner energy future. Budget 2027 allocated a record capital allocation of over €650 million for SEAI home and community energy upgrades, including more solar PV, new battery grants and a boiler scrappage scheme. The forthcoming Energy Affordability Action Plan, which I will bring to Government in the coming weeks, will set out a roadmap for improving energy affordability for households and businesses.”

CEO of the SEAI (Sustainable Energy Authority of Ireland), William Walsh, said:

“Behind these figures are people and families, in warmer, more comfortable homes, with more security over their energy bills. There is real momentum behind this work now. Every year we help more and more people, and with energy costs on the minds of many, especially at this time of year, I’d really encourage anyone thinking of upgrading to get in touch with us at SEAI, or check out our website www.seai.ie to see how to get started.

“Thanks to record Government support, the grants available have never been more generous – there really is an option for everyone now, regardless of where you’re starting out from or what your budget is.”

The recently enhanced measures include: solar PV and battery are now offered as standard under fully-funded Warmer Homes Scheme; more generous grants for solar, attic and cavity insulation for certain Department of Social Protection recipients and homes in HAP, RAS, and RASP tenancies; more favourable eligibility criteria for solar PV and solar thermal; a fossil fuel boiler scrappage support scheme; a home battery energy storage grant; and enhanced supports for businesses that will save thousands in energy costs.

For more information on home and business energy supports see the SEAI website: https://www.seai.ie.

#5675. How National Grid, Eversource are using VPPs to solve local-level constraints

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Northeast’s virtual power plant foundations are far stronger today than a decade ago, but utilities still need more visibility into distribution-system loads and energy resources, executives said.

National Grid and Eversource have both built robust, multi-device DER portfolios spanning multiple customer classes, Caldwell and Eversource vice president Tilak Subrahmanian said.

Eversource’s portfolio includes about 100,000 thermostats and 25,000 commercial and industrial customers across a territory that includes Greater Boston, ISO-New England’s biggest load zone, Subrahmanian said.

National Grid has about 100 MW of DER load in New England and more than 350 MW in upstate New York, respectively representing about 2% and 5% of regional system peak, Caldwell said. National Grid’s residential portfolio is mostly thermostats, but stationary storage and managed electric vehicle charging are increasingly meaningful, he added.

Eversource feels fortunate to operate in a region where state regulators provide time, funding and encouragement for utilities to “test hypotheses” about alternatives to traditional poles-and-wires investments, Subrahmanian said. That allows Eversource to explore use cases where distributed resources offer economic and system benefits over more capital-intensive infrastructure projects — which isn’t always the case, he added.

“It’s a learning process … [with] behind-the-meter devices, we have to be very specific about what problem we’re trying to solve,” he said. “We are cautiously optimistic, but a lot of it needs to play out still.”

National Grid and Eversource both participate in ConnectedSolutions+, a souped-up version of the long-running New England VPP program that offers higher incentives for distributed resource hosts on constrained distribution feeders.

Eversource announced two CS+ pilots earlier this year: one focused on areas served by three substations in Greater Boston and another focused on a single substation in southeastern Massachusetts. The Boston-area program aims to shift load away from demand peaks, while the southeastern Massachusetts program is intended in part to soak up excess distributed solar generation during the middle of the day.

At least one provider of low-cost home batteries, Haven Energy, is targeting customers of both utilities with deeply discounted energy storage subscriptions in areas where batteries deliver clear grid or resilience benefits. Earlier this month, National Grid said it would partner with Haven to provide up to 125 income-qualified households with no-cost battery systems as part of a pilot program it calls BackupBattery+.

“The value of a battery to the grid can be fundamentally different even one mile apart,” Haven Energy CEO Vinnie Campo told Utility Dive in August.

With increased DER adoption and regulation allowing for flexible cost recovery, the Northeast’s VPP foundations are far stronger today than a decade ago, when Caldwell was new to National Grid, he said.

Back then, “nothing ever really penciled, [it] was really hard to do … now, nearly a decade later, everything seems to be clicking. [There is] a sea change in how utilities see these things as practical tools for solving challenges,” Caldwell said.

More capable DER orchestration software is also unlocking potential, Diamond said. Traditional demand response events typically begin “firm” and then lose effectiveness as customers drop out, she explained. But this summer, across 70 dynamic load-shaping events for 12 different utilities, EnergyHub was “able to deliver consistent load shape” with up to 90% consistency for the entire duration, she said.

“[That] is huge for utilities, especially if you are trying to hit a peak hour — it’s really hard to do that if you don't have that consistency,” Diamond said.

Eversource is eager to gain more visibility into the position, sizing and behavior of loads and distributed energy resources on its system so that it can dial in location-specific solutions, Subrahmanian said. It only takes a few unmanaged EVs to overload the small, poletop transformers that remain common in parts of Eversource’s territory — infrastructure whose inevitable replacement can perhaps be deferred by a targeted managed charging program, he said.

Right now, Eversource can’t even confidently say which of its customers have air conditioning, but its ongoing advanced metering infrastructure rollout should improve the situation soon, he added.

“We will be in a much better position in a year,” Subrahmanian said.

#5674. NAED Energy Holding Inc. Details the Rating of Its PV-1 Tandem Cell Line: 16,000 Wafers per Hour per Line, 4.6 GW from Six Lines

NAED Energy Holding Inc. (NAED) just published the rating of PV-1, its production line for T2X perovskite/silicon heterojunction tandem solar cells. Each PV-1 line is rated at 16,000 G12 half-cut wafers per hour: 400 wafers every 90-second index, a rate set by the line’s inline PVD tool (ITO-1). The line’s four PECVD modules are built for a faster 76-second index, about 18,950 wafers per hour.

At NAED’s planning basis—85% availability, 97.5% yield, and the T2X programme target of 30% cell efficiency—one PV-1 line produces about 116 million cells and 0.77 GW of tandem cells a year. NAED’s six-line site, at 96,000 wafers per hour, has a rated capacity of about 4.6 GW a year.

Throughput and capacity in this release are rated values, and cell efficiency is modelled. NAED will publish measured throughput, yield, and efficiency, with their test basis, as results are logged. For all technical information on PV-1 and the T2X cell, contact cto.fusion@naedcorp.com.

The 34.4% figure is NAED’s modelled T2X cell (Voc 1.969 V, Jsc 20.18 mA/cm², fill factor 0.867, 217.2 cm² half-cell). For context, the highest published efficiencies for perovskite/silicon tandem cells of this size are in the 32–33% range, including Maxwell’s certified 32.38% on a G12 half-cell reported in January 2026. NAED’s planning figures use the 30% programme target.

The comparisons below are rate-to-rate. A PV-1 figure describes a complete integrated line, while some benchmarks describe a single tool; supplier figures are the suppliers’ own published ratings.

A single PV-1 line runs at or above the rate of leading single deposition tools while carrying the full tandem process — wet clean, PECVD, PVD, top-cell deposition, printing and test — in one line. Six lines carry the output of about three large HJT lines or seven to eight commercial tandem lines.

One year of output from the six-line site is enough cells for about 7.7 million 600-watt panels, which would generate roughly enough electricity for about 650,000 U.S. homes for a year (17% capacity factor, 10,500 kWh per home; typical U.S. values, not site data). Panels keep generating for 25–30 years, so each year of production adds to that total.

#5673. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5671. NextEra-Dominion merger hearings kick off with focus on scope

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The case is “simply not the one to litigate the cost of service [or] who’s paying their fair share ... including the data centers,” said Joe Reid, an attorney representing NextEra and Dominion.

The proposed merger between Dominion and NextEra, announced in May, would create the largest regulated electric utility in the world, with a 130-GW large load pipeline. Clean energy groups, local Virginia officials and leaders of the state’s executive branch have raised concerns about the potential impact of the deal on rates and data center development.

McGuire Woods partner Joe Reid, representing the joint petitioners NextEra and Dominion, said the case “is simply not the one to litigate the cost of service, and nor is it the case to litigate who's paying their fair share and ensuring everybody pays their fair share, including the data centers.”

Reid noted that the proposed acquisition is a “consequential combination, but its size does not change the requirements of the Transfers Act or the analysis required to determine whether the statute's requirements have been met.”

SCC Chair Kelsey Bagot asked Reid if the commission “should not consider longer-term benefits in evaluating this proposed transaction,” and Reid replied that the commission “should consider all the evidence that is in front of it.”

Nate Benforado, a senior attorney with the Southern Environmental Law Center, argued that the Transfers Act is less narrow than the joint petitioners claimed.

“The analysis that the Utility Transfers Act requires is broad,” Benforado said. “Petitioners must prove that this transaction will not impair or jeopardize adequate service at just and reasonable rates … This is not simply a ‘do no harm standard’. This looks at virtually everything a utility does.”

Will Reisinger, representing the Piedmont Environmental Council, pointed out that the statute provides that “before approving this merger, the commission ‘shall be satisfied’ that adequate service to the public at just and reasonable rates will not be impaired or jeopardized.”

“That standard, satisfied, is subjective,” he said. “This is not a matter of calling a ball or a strike. This decision is 100% within your judgment, within your discretion.”

Will Cleveland, the attorney representing Google, said that the company plans to present testimony from energy economist Carolyn Berry, “who will testify that the minimum demand charge approved as part of Dominion's 2025 biennial rate case discourages large loads like data centers from taking advantage of their statutory right to retail choice.” Dominion has said “they don't think this testimony is relevant to the case,” Cleveland said, but Google disagrees.

Amazon’s attorney, Brian Greene, said that that company would want to see Dominion address two issues if the commission were to approve the transaction: “One is reforming the large load interconnection queue, and second is addressing the need for new capacity through bring-your-own options,” he said.

Greene said that Amazon proposes that “if the transaction is approved, Dominion be required to reform its study process through a cluster study approach that evaluates projects together and looks for coordinated system-wide solutions … Approving the merger without reforming the interconnection queue would be like putting more power behind the same undersized wire.”

#5670. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5669. The grid will fall short of AI’s power needs. These 6 resources can close the gap.

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own, writes Simon Irish, CEO of Terrestrial Energy.

Simon Irish is CEO of Terrestrial Energy, a developer of small modular nuclear reactors.

Most planning for artificial intelligence’s electricity demand starts with a forecast and asks whether it is right.

I would start somewhere else: Assume the grid will fall short, then ask how to close the gap — and how fast.

The evidence supports that starting point. McKinsey & Company estimates the U.S. will need 30 GW to 55 GW of additional capacity by 2030 as data center demand grows and aging plants retire. PJM Interconnection, the largest U.S. grid operator, has already seen the impacts of this trend. Its latest capacity auction fell about 6.6 GW short of its reliability requirement and cleared at the price cap.

Forecasts can be wrong, and requested load can be speculative or duplicative. But the PJM result is a market outcome, not a forecast. The useful question is which tools can close the gap, by when and at what cost.

The first four options below address serving data centers through the grid. The final two consider generation at or near the data center, with an optional grid connection. These categories overlap; on-site generation is a delivery strategy, not a generation technology, and can use multiple technologies.

Flexible demand. Duke University’s Nicholas Institute estimates the grid could absorb 76 GW of new load if that load accepted curtailment averaging 0.25% of annual consumption, in events of about 1.7 hours. It is the fastest option because it needs no new generation. It makes better use of capacity already on the grid.

But that capacity is finite, and beyond it, further load growth requires new supply. There is also a practical hurdle; to date, many data centers have shown little willingness to curtail AI workloads, because the value of computing far exceeds the cost of power.

Existing nuclear plants. Increasing output at operating plants through uprates and restarting closed plants can add reliable electricity supply, while extending operating licenses keeps existing plants running. A U.S. Department of Energy initiative targets 5 GW of added capacity at existing nuclear plants by 2029, and DOE has closed a $1.9 billion loan to restart Iowa’s Duane Arnold plant, returning 615 MW to service.

The limit is opportunity: Few operating plants remain candidates for significant uprates, and restarts are underway at nearly every plant where one is practical.

Natural gas. Gas offers firm power and a familiar path, but gas turbine supply has become the constraint. GE Vernova’s gas turbine backlog and slot reservations reached 116 GW at midyear 2026, and the company has said a heavy-duty turbine ordered today will not arrive until 2031. Buyers also take on fuel-price exposure and decades of emissions.

Wind, solar and storage. These resources can be deployed quickly and at scale. Their limit is that data centers require dependable power around the clock, so matching that requirement takes substantial overbuilding, energy storage and transmission infrastructure to deliver the same capacity as a firm resource.

On-site generation. Building a power plant at a data center can bring electricity online sooner by avoiding some grid upgrades and connection delays, where permitted.

However, it does not speed up equipment deliveries. An on-site gas turbine plant faces the same turbine shortages as one supplying the grid. Reliable operation also requires sufficient on-site backup capability or access to grid power during outages and maintenance. If a project relies on the grid, developers need clear terms defining that access and its cost.

Advanced nuclear energy. Advanced reactors offer firm, clean power and can be built as a fleet. They can serve data centers directly, with an optional grid connection. They will not close the gap this decade, and their real test is cost at fleet scale.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own. Three principles follow.

Flexible demand, uprates at existing nuclear plants, and new wind, solar and storage can help meet near-term electricity needs.

Natural gas and on-site generation can help fill the gap while longer-term resources are developed, but only if equipment deliveries, permits and grid interconnection align with the data center’s schedule.

Advanced nuclear energy can contribute in the 2030s and beyond.

Utilities must plan now for both natural gas and advanced nuclear energy. DOE’s long-term modeling indicates 550 to 770 GW of additional firm, clean generating capacity could be needed by 2050, with advanced nuclear energy providing about 200 GW.

Infrastructure built to meet an immediate shortfall should also have a useful role later. A gas plant could provide firming capacity or operate during periods of peak demand as longer-term supply becomes available.

Make flexibility the price of speed. Utilities can offer earlier interconnection to customers accepting curtailment under defined, metered and enforceable terms. For data center operators, that is a choice between some power sooner and firm power later. Customers that can curtail load may get power sooner; those that cannot must wait for firm supply. This converts flexibility from an assumption into a contract and rewards developers whose flexibility is real.

Secure the long-lead items for firm, clean power now. Buyers who reserved gas turbine production slots early secured earlier deliveries; those ordering now may have to wait until 2031.

Firm, clean power has its own long-lead items, including fuel supply and equipment manufacturing capacity. Utilities and data center operators can sign reservation or offtake agreements that give suppliers the demand signal to build capacity before it is needed.

These principles also address the forecast debate. Some investments — flexibility, uprates and transmission — remain valuable across demand scenarios. Others are options worth buying, such as reservations for firm supply in the 2030s and beyond.

And some are bets to avoid: irreversible commitments sized to a demand number that may not arrive. If forecasts prove too high, the cost is the price paid to preserve an option. If they prove right and no one bought the option, the cost is the kind of shortfall PJM has already seen.

Planning for a shortfall is not pessimism. Of all the inputs AI needs, reliable power may prove the scarcest. The race to scale AI is a race to deliver power. Utilities, regulators and developers need tools that arrive on time, hold their value if forecasts change, and keep electricity reliable and affordable for existing customers.

Voltus CEO Dana Guernsey compared new “bring your own new capacity” contracts to the power purchase agreements that “revolutionized” utility-scale solar and wind development.

#5668. As Hearings Begin, Sierra Club Virginia Calls for the SCC to Stop the Dominion NextEra Merger

Richmond, Virginia — Tomorrow, November 17, the Virginia State Corporation Commission (SCC) will begin the evidentiary hearing for NextEra Energy and Dominion Energy’s merger proposal, which would create the nation’s largest ever utility. Sierra Club filed expert testimony last month urging the SCC to reject the merger given the way it threatens the Virginia’s Clean Economy Act (VCEA) and violates the Utility Transfers Act, posing a significant risk to energy affordability for Virginia families.

Dominion and NextEra have failed to show that customer benefits, not shareholders profits, were a motivating factor for the merger. The utilities have offered a temporary $10 per month bill credit to Virginia customers for four years to help cover any additional costs. However, the credit does not so much as cover the $30 per month Virginia families expect to pay in anticipated bill increases to maintain fossil fuel plants and meet AI data centers demand in 2027 alone.

If approved, the new utility would service 10 million customers across the Southeastern United States. Allied groups will rally outside the SCC tomorrow for the Stop the NextEra Takeover: Final Rally at the SCC.

Ahead of tomorrow’s hearing, Connor Kish, Director of the Sierra Club Virginia Chapter, issued the following statement:

“Throughout November, Virginians have shown up in droves at public hearings to oppose Dominion and NextEra’s proposed merger. Utility customers, independent policy experts, and local leaders have cited sky-high energy bills, AI data center demand, and abandoned climate goals as reasons to be wary of Dominion and NextEra’s bid to become the country’s largest ever utility.

“Meanwhile, the utilities have yet to prove the merger will result in any tangible benefits for customers. Their offer of a temporary $10 monthly bill credit for customers is nothing in comparison to the $10 billion in profits shareholders are anticipated to receive should the SCC approve the merger. Virginia deserves utilities that prioritize families, not shareholders out for bonuses. To keep the lights on and make bills affordable, it’s critical that SCC fulfill its duty to the public and reject the merger.”

#5666. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

Campbell based on reported utility cost recovery filings for portions of the facility’s extension orders.”

#5664. AI Writes Better Discharge Summaries Than Doctors, Study Suggests

— Physician reviewers gave AI summaries higher marks for accuracy, completeness, clarity, and tone

Artificial intelligence (AI) outperformed clinicians in drafting hospital discharge summaries, a U.K.-based retrospective study showed.

A higher proportion of large language model (LLM)-generated discharge summaries were signed off by a clinician reviewer with no or minor changes compared with clinician-authored summaries (92.2% versus 70.0%), meeting prespecified noninferiority criteria, reported Kathleen Goldsmith, MSc, senior data scientist at a partnership between Imperial College London and Imperial College Healthcare NHS Trust in the U.K., and colleagues.

Moreover, clinician reviewers rated LLM-generated summaries higher on accuracy, completeness, tone, and clarity compared to clinician-written summaries, they wrote in JAMA Network Open.

Finally, a smaller proportion of AI-generated discharge summaries contained inaccurate or incomplete information compared with ones written by clinicians (26.2% vs 45.1%), they reported.

"Together, these findings suggest that AI-assisted documentation could strengthen the quality of information transferred at discharge, an important component of safe and effective continuity of care," Goldsmith told MedPage Today.

While this research focused on discharge summaries, Goldsmith said it could be applied to other administrative tasks, but "patient safety and clinical value have to be central to how we adopt technology like this."

In an accompanying editorial, Melanie Molina, MD, of the University of California San Francisco, wrote that this research raises the question not of whether AI can produce acceptable drafts, but whether physicians can verify the information in real-time.

"Lengthy, complex, and fragmented clinical records create a verification paradox: the cases in which automated summarization is most valuable are often the cases in which verification is most difficult," Molina wrote, adding that emergency department care poses particular challenges.

Plus, AI tools have already evolved far beyond the model tested in this study, with newer, more sophisticated models less likely to hallucinate.

Molina argued that while this research shows that "a thoughtfully designed LLM pipeline can produce discharge summaries that clinicians prefer to routine documentation," this does not yet equate to a safer transition of care.

"The enduring clinical task will be ensuring that what it writes is true, complete, and actionable," she wrote.

Goldsmith and team sought to evaluate how AI tools could "free up clinician time for patient-facing care while maintaining clinical safety and accuracy" and to develop a framework for evaluating the use of LLMs in clinical settings.

This retrospective noninferiority study used Imperial Clinical Analytics, Research and Evaluation (iCARE) research data from a network of 5 hospitals in London from Sept. 1, 2022, to Sept. 30, 2023.

Discharge summaries written by clinicians and by GPT-4o mini were blindly reviewed by 12 resident physicians from the Imperial College Healthcare Trust. The primary outcome was clinician sign-off on the discharge summary with little to no edits. Goldsmith noted this endpoint reflects how the technology would be used in real-world settings.

"Having clinician input in both the co-design, use, and evaluation of healthcare AI technologies is crucial to ensure these systems are trusted, and truly deliver the benefits to clinical work," Goldsmith said.

Ultimately, there were 1,294 discharge summaries included, of which 649 were clinician-authored and 645 were LLM-generated.

Patients' median age was 46 and 56.6% were female. The median length of stay was 3 days. On average, each case required the review of 30 clinical documents, or about 4,000 words.

The researchers noted some limitations, including that de-identification may have removed clinically relevant information, that only one LLM was evaluated, and the retrospective study design; prospective assessments at the point of care are still needed.

#5663. Mercedes Is Auctioning Off A C111 For The First Time Ever, And It'll Be Your Last Chance To Buy One For At Least 25 Years

Back in 2022, Mercedes-Benz brought a chosen group of collectors to its Museum for a secret auction, at which one of the two 1955 300 SLR Uhlenhaut Coupés was sold for a record-breaking €135 million, about $143 million according to exchange rates at the time. Most people, myself including, thought there was no way Mercedes would have ever sold one of the Uhlenhauts, not to mention many of the other slightly less rare and iconic cars in its collection. Now that it had auctioned off its most valuable car, it opened up so much speculation: What could be next?

Turns out the next thing is another completely unexpected sale. On Tuesday night, at an event kicking off the week's Rétromobile New York festivities, Mercedes-Benz announced that in just a few months it will be selling one of its groundbreaking C111 prototypes. This will be the first time Mercedes has ever sold a C111, and unlike the Uhlenhaut, it will be a public auction held at RM Sotheby's Rétromobile Paris sale in February, with all proceeds going towards funding the future of the Mercedes-Benz Museum.

While I don't think the C111 will touch the Uhlenhaut's record hammer price, you really should never say never. There's no precedent for how much this car could go for, especially given that Mercedes-Benz has promised it won't sell or auction off another C111 for at least 25 years. If you want one, now might be your only chance, and the C111 in question is perhaps the best one of the bunch.

As a bit of a memory refresher, the first C111 was introduced to the public at the 1969 International Motor Show in Frankfurt, though a few were already built by then, and an updated version was shown the following year in Geneva. It was strictly an experimental vehicle, a testbed for future technology, design and powertrain ideas. Prospective customers handed Mercedes blank checks to get a C111 of their own, and Mercedes says there was "enormous public interest," but the C111 was never put into production. The company cites the 1973 oil crisis, new emissions standards and high production costs as the reasons why, but that didn't stop Mercedes from continuing to build more new versions.

Between 1969 and 1978, Mercedes built 17 different C111s across three iterations of the model: the C111-I, the C111-II, and the C111-III. The C111 was originally conceived with a rotary powertrain, first a three-rotor Wankel with later versions getting a four-rotor engine. One was built with a V8 and another has since been converted to have a V8, and the last few C111-IIIs had five-cylinder turbodiesels with which they set a bunch of records at Nardò. Three of the C111s were dismantled after testing, and one was destroyed in crash testing; the rest have remained in Mercedes' collection ever since, with only a couple of them being still functional.

As Mercedes continued building new iterations of the C111, the overall design and various details evolved, but every C111 was spectacular and clearly part of the same family. The company had never made a mid-engined sports car, not to mention anything such a wild wedge shape, so it's no surprise they were show-stoppers everywhere. Some C111s had their glass-fiber-reinforced plastic bodywork originally painted white, and the last few were metallic silver, but the cars are best known for the Weißherbst paint worn by the vast majority of them — "white autumn" in German, named after a traditional copper-tinted rosé.

The C111 being sold is a C111-II, car number 33, which Mercedes says is "only of internal significance." Production began in March 1970 with the car being completed in August, when it became the second C111-II to go into the test fleet. It was fitted with a 2.4-liter four-rotor Wankel engine that produced a massive 345 horsepower and 289 pound-feet of torque, sending the 2,734-pound car from 0 to 62 mph in 4.8 seconds and on to a top speed of 186 mph (300 km/h), making it the fastest car in the world at the time. In fact, chief test engineer Hans Liebold once hit 188 mph on the Autobahn heading towards Baden-Baden.

Mercedes says Car 33 "was used to test a fluidic component designed to ensure even emptying of the two side fuel tanks, which have a combined capacity of 30.6 gallons, to carry out temperature measurements in the rear trunk, and to conduct test drives at the Hockenheimring." It was featured on the first German issue of Playboy in 1972, and since 1975 it has been retired, part of Mercedes-Benz's collection. When the museum opened in 2006, the car has been on display in the Fascination Design area, floating on a plinth surrounding a curved staircase.

In 2024, the Mercedes-Benz Classic team in Fellbach gave Car 33 a full restoration, making it one of just a couple C111s that actually still run, and the only running car with a rotary engine. The mechanical restoration was completed without messing up the car's lovely patina, the only addition to the car being a fire extinguisher. It has the original paint and houndstooth cloth seats, and a Becker Europa radio that actualy works.

There are 24,766 kilometers on the odometer (about 15,389 miles), and Mercedes says that while Car 33 was used to test anti-lock braking systems, the ABS currently doesn't work. The car comes with its original magnesium racing wheels, sized 10x15-inch in front and 13x15-inch in the rear, but they are "not only suitable for display purposes," so Mercedes created a new set of 8x15 light alloy wheels that the car can actually be driven on. The rear trunk tub, which is designed to hold luggage, is fitted and able to be removed.

I'm particularly excited to see how much this car goes for because I've had a few very special experiences with C111s, including this specific one. Mere days before the world shut down in 2020, I was given a tour of Mercedes' secret "holy halls" warehouses in Germany, where I saw half of all the C111s all lined up together. (The photo you see below, which Mercedes graciously let me take on a film camera.)

At Monterey Car Week in 2022 I got to ride shotgun the V8-powered C111 on the Tour d'Elegance, which was incredible even when we were stuck in traffic and slightly overheating. In 2024, I got to ride up and down Big Sur in this particular car, number 33, fresh off its restoration with a fully functioning rotary engine. The V8 C111 already felt extraordinary, but the four-rotor motor's totally unique buzzy sound and character really takes it to the stratosphere. There's no other car in existence quite like it.

Apart from other C111s, of course. Yes, it's pretty nuts that Mercedes is selling one of these things, but it will still have 12 others in the collection. I'm sure now that the Classic team got this one working, it will be able to restore one of the other rotary-powered C111s in the collection. Don't think this is the last the world will see of this C111, either. Part of the buyer contract for the Uhlenhaut was that its new owner must bring the car to events and shows, and that Mercedes gets to use it for things when it wants, and I'm sure the same will be the case for this sale.

Whoever buys it really should use it, too. For all of its fantastical styling and engineering, it's a proper car. Sure, the rotary doesn't really like sitting around at low speeds, instead egging you on to keep reaching the red mark at 7,000 rpm on the tachometer, and there's no air conditioning so your only respite from in-cabin heat is to open the small vent windows (or just open the gullwing doors). It's pretty comfortable, though, and pretty easy to get in and out of. The engine isn't so loud that you can't hold a conversation with your passenger, and the interior just feels like any other Mercedes from the '70s.

The C111 be will be sold on February 3, 2027, at the Salles du Carrousel underneath the Louvre, where RM Sotheby's typically has its Rétromobile auction. RM Sotheby's says bids can be placed by phone or in person at the auction, after reigstering ahead of time of course, and if you're interested you can go to the Mercedes Classic Center website to learn about "the terms and conditions of participation, bidder registration and opportunities for pre-inspection." Given the C111's existence as a forward-thinking experiment, I think it's fitting that the money goes toward making the automaker's museum even better for future generations. Says Mercedes:

The aim of "Mercedes-Benz Museum 2.0" is to bring the brand's unique heritage to life for future generations and make it accessible worldwide. To this end, Mercedes-Benz is continuously developing both the Museum in Stuttgart and its virtual offerings. A key component is the Museum's digital twin. This makes the Museum experience accessible regardless of physical location, thereby extending its reach far beyond Stuttgart. It will give people all over the world greater access to the Mercedes-Benz brand heritage. The virtual twin is being developed in collaboration with The Classic Car Trust (TCCT) and its digital platform, Roarington.

"With 'Mercedes-Benz Museum 2.0' , we are consistently taking our Museum to the next level. We are continuing to write our company and brand history right up to the present day, creating new exhibition experiences and providing space for topics that are of particular interest to our visitors – from more recent classic cars to the latest innovations. With a digital twin, we are also opening up the Museum to people all over the world. Visitors can also experience the exhibits interactively here. The first results will be visible as early as 2027." -Bettina Haussmann, Director of the Mercedes-Benz Museum

So, how much could it go for? Who the hell knows. The collector car market is so absurd these days — McLaren F1s are $35-million cars now — and there's no prior sales of cars that you can really compare the C111 to. Last year the Indianapolis Motor Speedway auctioned off a Mercedes-Benz W196 R race car that become the second-most-expensive sale ever, also in parnership with the automaker and RM Sotheby's. The most expensive public sale of a Ferrari 250 GTO has been just under $52 million, while private sales have been rumored to exceed $70 million, and I think the C111 could easily clear that mark.

For its part, Mercedes says that "for at least 25 years, this will remain the only opportunity: Mercedes-Benz has contractually committed not to sell any further C111s during this period." That's definitely an incentive for people to keep raising their paddles. I'm also going to take this sale as confirmation that Mercedes is definitely working on a C111-inspired supercar in the vein of the Vision One-Eleven concept from a few years ago.

#5662. XPENG Forecasts 45% Sales Growth in 4th Quarter

XPENG just reported 3rd quarter financial details today, while also forecasting what will happen in the 4th quarter. Everything is looking quite rosy at the moment.

As far as 3rd quarter financials go, on the back of strong sales, the company logged 24.28 billion yuan ($3.643 billion) of revenue. That’s a new quarterly record for the company. It’s 19% more than in Q3 2025 and 23% more than Q2 2026.

That said, despite strong revenue, the company still scored a net loss. It had a significant 870 million yuan ($130.5 million) net loss, in fact, which was up 128% from 380 million yuan in Q3 2025 and down 35% from 1.34 billion from Q2 2026. XPENG made a small profit in Q4 2025, and since then has again been trying to get its finances into the green again instead of in the red.

Good news going into the 4th quarter is the company expects even better. XPENG is forecasting 169,000 to 174,000 vehicle deliveries, which would mean year-over-year growth of 45% to 50%. Expected revenue is 30.4 billion yuan to 31.8 billion yuan, which would mean growth of 37% to 43%.

We’ll see. The company is still not expected to get another net profit quarter, but perhaps the quarter could surprise and XPENG could log its second quarterly profit. One thing making that unlikely, though, is that XPENG’s R&D costs were up significantly, 17.5%. Also, general and administrative expenses were up 11.4% year over year. We’ll see what the 4th quarter brings.

#5661. Cancer History Tied to Severe Maternal Morbidity During Childbirth

— Deliveries among cancer survivors have increased in recent years

More cancer survivors are giving birth, and severe maternal morbidity was increased at delivery for both past survivors and current cancer patients, a cross-sectional study in the U.S. showed.

From 2016 to 2023, hospital deliveries increased from 30.1 to 45.6 per 10,000 for past cancer survivors (average annual percentage change [AAPC] 6.3%), while hospital deliveries for current cancer patients were relatively unchanged (7.0 to 7.4 per 10,000 deliveries, AAPC 0.2%), reported Koji Matsuo, MD, PhD, of the University of Southern California in Los Angeles, and colleagues in JAMA Network Open.

Compared with a severe maternal morbidity rate of 6.8 per 1,000 hospital deliveries for people without cancer, this rate was 11.9 per 1,000 deliveries for past cancer survivors (adjusted incidence rate ratio [IRR] 1.45, 95% CI 1.37-1.53) and 48.2 per 1,000 deliveries for current cancer patients (adjusted IRR 3.80, 95% CI 3.58-4.04).

Of note, the inpatient maternal mortality rate during the admission for hospital delivery was 14-fold higher for current cancer patients compared with those without cancer (273.0 vs 7.0 per 100,000 hospital deliveries; adjusted IRR 14.24, 95% CI 10.87-18.66).

"As cancer care and outcomes have improved and fertility preservation and assisted reproductive technology have become more widely available, pregnancy after cancer is an important issue in survivorship care for reproductive-aged individuals, including adolescent and young adult cancer survivors," wrote Matsuo and colleagues. "The increase in delivery hospitalizations among past cancer survivors in the U.S. may partly reflect these changes."

"These findings support the importance of preconception evaluation for cancer survivors and early referral to tertiary care centers with multidisciplinary teams, as currently recommended by multiple organizations," they added.

This study used national hospital discharge data from the Healthcare Cost and Utilization Project's National Inpatient Sample, which approximates a sample of 20% of discharges from participating hospitals across 48 states and the District of Columbia.

Among 28,360,443 hospital deliveries, 0.4% were among past cancer survivors, less than 0.1% were among current cancer patients, and 99.5% were among women without a cancer diagnosis. Median age at delivery was 33, 32, and 29 years across the three groups, respectively.

Among past cancer survivors, thyroid cancer (22.3%) was the most frequent type of cancer diagnosis, followed by cutaneous melanoma (15.7%), breast cancer (11.7%), lymphoma (10.8%), uterine cervical cancer (6.6%), leukemia (4.2%), and ovarian cancer (3.6%).

The study's primary outcome was severe maternal morbidity at the time of delivery, with morbidity indicators restricted to those with a case-fatality rate of 1% or higher after an event. The most common were cardiac arrest or ventricular fibrillation, conversion of cardiac rhythm, heart failure or arrest during surgery or a procedure, and amniotic fluid embolism.

Rates of severe maternal morbidity at delivery varied by cancer type, with greater associations seen among survivors of leukemia and lymphoma, and more modest associations with breast, cervical, and thyroid cancers.

Most hospital deliveries (57.6%) for past cancer survivors were full term, while only 39.6% were full term for current cancer patients.

Matsuo and colleagues acknowledged that the study had several limitations. For instance, they pointed out that unmeasured confounding factors were possible since they did not have access to information on cancer stage, histologic type, familial cancer syndrome, treatment history, duration of remission, residual organ dysfunction, or detailed disease status.
