#5660. 7 Engine Swaps Where The Exhaust Note Will Make You Do A Double Take

A lot of really cool cars come straight out of the factory, but for those who want something truly unique, or just love a bizarre challenge, an obscure engine swap is often the best direction in which to head. Now, here at Jalopnik we've covered no end of awesome conversions in the past, including documenting your dream engine swaps, but this list focuses on something a little more specific.

The list is full of engine swaps which we love, purely because they wind up giving the base car an exhaust note that nobody would ever expect. Included among the 7 is a VR6-swapped Porsche 911 with more than 1,000 horsepower, and a Korean city car with a GM-sourced V6 wedged under the smallest hood imaginable. And to prove our point, each feature engine swap is accompanied with a video to demonstrate just how absurd these swaps are.

Before you race to the comments to point out all the cool swaps we have missed, it's also worth mentioning that we paid particularly close attention to projects which, from the outside at least, looked largely stock. Or, if not stock, at least they weren't giving the secret away. So, no superchargers protruding through hoods here, or turbos masquerading as headlights. In no particular order, these are 7 of the most double-take-worthy engine swaps we found, kicking things off with a Ferrari-powered DeLorean that sure would have given Doc Brown a far easier time in cracking that 88 mph threshold in the "Back To The Future" films.

Almost everything about the factory-original DeLorean is awe-inspiring. The brushed stainless steel bodywork, the rakish lines, and, of course, the silver-screen connection that immortalized the model forever as Doc Brown's time machine. However, its 130-hp 2.8-liter V6 has always been a source of disappointment in the eyes of many. Put simply, the DeLorean always deserved better.

A firm by the name of FuelTech took it upon themselves to address this concern. This particular vehicle did not have an engine when they found it, but it would have been powered by a PRV (Peugeot-Renault-Volvo) engine.  Instead of settling for the original engine, they put in a far more exotic 4.3-liter V8. The mill itself was sourced from a Ferrari California, and delivers 453 horsepower, alongside 358 pound-feet of torque. The soundtrack that now echoes from the DeLorean's rear-end is nothing like what bystanders might expect.

Helping to distribute that power is a six-speed Tiptronic transmission, pinched from a Porsche 911. A KW kit addressed any and all suspension-related concerns, while a larger brake kit was also understandably called upon. The DeLorean isn't a car which usually sees such in-depth modification, with most owners choosing to keep them entirely stock, or to turn them into time machine replicas. However, this particular example was purchased by FuelTech in a sorry state, with no engine and needing much work, so it's perhaps not the purist's nightmare many might first assume.

One of the wildest Cummins swaps out there is this 4BT-swapped Toyota Prius. The Toyota would have started life with a 1.5-liter inline-four hybrid system, but that — along with the entire interior and a whole heap of metalwork — has been entirely stripped out. In its place, owner Ben Schulz dropped in a turbocharged 3.9-liter inline-four diesel Cummins engine, which dynoed at 360 hhp and 550 lb-ft of torque.

Schulz says a TDI engine and front-wheel drive was at least lightly considered at one point, but instead, the Cummins and rear-wheel drive swap idea was too good to ignore. Hearing a high-capacity turbo diesel 4-cylinder thundering down the road, which is traditionally more at home in construction vehicles, but then seeing a Prius emerging through a thick cloud of black smoke must be one of the most double-take-worthy engine swaps of all time — it's a wild swap, that's for sure.

Remarkably, the car still looks fairly standard from the outside; even the standard rear wheels were widened rather than replaced, in order to retain that factory look. Future plans include refitting as much of the original interior as possible, which should further boost the sleeper appeal of this coal-rolling Toyota.

The Chevrolet Corvette hasn't always sported a V8 engine. In fact, the first Corvettes arrived with the Blue Flame inline-six engine under their hoods, but for certain, there has never been a factory four-cylinder Corvette. However, some engineers from Unruly Motorsports decided to build exactly that, swapping a C6 Corvette's 6.0-liter LS2 out for a turbocharged four-cylinder Honda K24.

In its stock guise, the C6 Corvette produces a healthy 400 hp, but the minds behind this K24 build had their sights set an awful lot higher. Before dropping this particular K24 into the Corvette, it managed to make an impressive 828 horsepower on 20 pounds of boost. It boasts forged internals, a closed deck, and a 67mm turbocharger with ceramic ball bearings, and interestingly enough, it's hooked up to a built 2-speed Powerglide transmission. Once setup in the C6, Unruly Motorsports managed to see a peak of 917 hp on the dyno, alongside 563 lb-ft of torque.

This swap wasn't carried out just to annoy the purists, although no doubt it probably has upset a few of them. Instead, it was built to perform, and with it churning out quarter-mile passes in the very low 11-second range, it certainly does exactly that. All while turning more heads than most silver drop-top C6 Corvettes, thanks mostly to that most curious and un-GM-like four-pot soundtrack.

People have been swapping all sorts of engines into Miatas for a while now. We've seen 800-horsepower and 10,000 rpm K24-powered Miatas, and even motorcycle engine-swapped Miatas, and while those will both sound distinctly different from standard models, it's this Volvo-motivated Miata which has our interests piqued today.

Apart from a few curious graphics, this first-generation Miata looks pretty stock. It's passable for just another cheap project car, the type which are spotted fairly commonly. However, beneath the factory hood lies a B2544T engine, which belongs to the T5 family. In short, 2.4 liters, 5 cylinders, one turbocharger, a goal of between 300 and 400 horsepower, and a Miata.

Plans were originally made for a T6 engine to slot under the hood, but the T5 would fit into the available space easier. For our purposes, it suits too, as while a boosted inline-six does sound cool, the T5 boasts a particularly distinctive sound.

The engine is hooked up to a manual 6-speed transmission plucked from a BMW 1 Series, which the owners notes was chosen due to its ability to handle an awful lot of torque. The real joy of a swap like this is that it demonstrates how creativity trumps funds. Mating a cheap Miata to a junkyard Volvo motor and coupling that with an old BMW transmission is just as cool as any other swap on this list.

We've looked at this 6.2-liter LS3-powered Tesla in the past, but it certainly deserves a spot on this list too. Popular automotive YouTuber Rich Rebuilds took what was a flood-damaged Tesla Model S and mated its body with the LS3 out of a Chevrolet Camaro, while at the same time making every effort to keep it looking relatively stock from the outside. A vinyl wrap was chosen for the finished product, though, in a rather unflattering brownish color, until you realize it plays on the car's "ICE-T" nickname, which cleverly combines ICE (internal combustion engine) with T, for Tesla.

That's why, when the key is turned and that thunderous V8 soundtrack booms out of the Tesla's side-exit exhaust pipe, it's absolutely one of those engine swaps that will give bystanders cause to stop what they are doing and give this former-EV a double take.

The team dropped the LS3 into the Tesla's frunk and mated it with a sequential 6-speed manual transmission, which itself was routed through a custom-made transmission tunnel. It wound up making around 440 horsepower, and bizarrely garnered the YouTuber a lot of hate online. Remember, this was a flood-damaged car; he essentially saved it from the junkyard. After the build's completion, the V8 Tesla was passed around various YouTubers before ending up back with Rich some 5 years later. The car returned to him with numerous updates, most notably a Magnuson supercharger, in addition to a heap of issues, so it will be interesting to see how the V8-swapped Tesla's life continues from this point forward.

The Porsche 911 996 Turbo is already a powerful car, producing 415 hp and 413 lb-ft of torque as standard from its Mezger 3.6-liter flat-six engine. That engine is also really quite susceptible to tuning, with kits available from Speed Density Motorsports that can send that standard output soaring close to and even north of 1,000 horsepower.

However, Speed Density Motorsports found a way of delivering up to 1,500 horsepower from the 996 Turbo platform, but its method of securing such a figure is a little unconventional. For this, the flat-six was removed entirely, and a 3.2-liter VR6 engine – the same engine which has been doing the rounds in Volkswagen cars for over 30 years now — was dropped back into the space instead, albeit now with a wild state of tune. The engine, 3.3 liters, with billet aluminum and forged internals was installed, and a Precision 8565 ball-bearing turbocharger was fitted. Everything is new and high-end; this is a 1,500-horsepower VR6 running on methanol fuel, after all.

Footage of the outfit's Stage 4-equipped shop car, with Stage 4 being detailed as a 3.2-liter VR6-swapped single turbo build capable of cracking 1,300 horsepower on E85, shows off the unusual and aggressive exhaust note thrown out the back of this once flat-six-motivated 996. This particular generation of 911 has been the subject of other curious engine swaps in the past, but the iconic warble of a VR6 has to be the best candidate yet from a soundtrack perspective.

There aren't that many reasons to celebrate the achievements of Daewoo, certainly not from an enthusiastic perspective. The Matiz was a diminutive little four-door hatchback produced by the Korean firm from the late '90s onward, until GM purchased the automaker and production carried on as the Chevrolet Matiz. Eventually, the Spark would replace the nameplate.

As standard, a 52-horsepower 0.8-liter inline-three powered the little Matiz. It was never a performer, instead prioritizing cheapness, as most Daewoo cars did. However, one man — bored of engine-swapping Vauxhalls — decided to make a humble one-owner Matiz the subject of a seriously cool engine swap. The brief was simply to place the biggest engine possible into the smallest car available.

The engine in question was a naturally aspirated 3.2-liter V6 from a Vauxhall Vectra, which kicks out 208 horsepower and 221 lb-ft of torque, in addition to a sensationally unusual engine note for this dinky little city-dweller. Nothing to shout about, but considering a standard Matiz weighs only 1,711 pounds, the power-to-weight ratio is really quite impressive. It was understandably a tight fit under the Matiz' tiny hood, but with custom engine mounts and a modified intake manifold, the builder squeezed it in while still retaining the factory airbox.

Ample stopping power comes from a custom big brake kit on the front consisting of Focus RS rotors and Lancer Evolution VI calipers, which sit behind 18-inch Porsche alloys. A lightened flywheel and limited-slip differential make the most of the V6's power, and — aside from a Vauxhall gear-shifter — the interior has been kept entirely stock.

#5658. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5657. Waymo's Latest Play To Win Over Humans To Driverless Taxis Is Women's Safety

Waymo has been burning piles of money as a Google spin-off company for a decade now with no end in sight. Technical challenges aside, what really makes or breaks a new technology is the behavior of the people using it. The company's latest reach-out to the average American arrived in ad-form last week, and it bets on women and their constantly compromised safety.

"Built for women. Designed for peace of mind," is a one-minute video following women doing all of the things we are taught to do when simply trying to move through the world — looking over the shoulder constantly, engaging in fake, one-sided conversations over the phone, getting out keys, etc. Layered in is a voiceover montage of women's voices saying all-too-common phrases uttered when a friend leaves the pack. Things like "maybe keep your keys out before you leave the bar," and "have someone walk you out to your car." The ad ends with a woman (separately) getting into a car. She shuts the the door and gives a big exhale. The Waymo is her safe space.

A Waymo robotaxi could possibly prove safer for any rider, regardless of gender, considering how faulty human drivers can be. Meanwhile, Uber's handling of its sexual assault cases would give any reasonable woman pause. Lyft and Uber even offer women-only drivers to customers because of women's understandable hesitance to get into a car with a man they don't know. So, why not expand that logic by removing the driver entirely. Waymo further backed its advantages for women's safety in its a blog saying that the company's data showed over half of female riders said that "Waymo improves their sense of personal safety. Having a space where riders are in control of their environment makes a real difference."

As a woman who has said or heard (or practiced) much of the advice in Waymo's video, I'll admit there is some comfort in not having to worry about being alone in a car with a driver. Advertising on the bet that other women will too makes me feel a cynical little queasiness in my gut, however. It's a drop in the bucket for what women have to worry about when they leave their homes. For instance, in Waymo's own video, a women walked from venue to robotaxi for some distance alone on a dark street. Should something happen in that time, a human driver might be able to spot it and intervene. A woman already has to shoulder the responsibility of her attacker's actions against her, will that scrutiny now extend to her choice in transportation?

I wanted to know more about what Waymo offers for riders in terms of safety not only in the car, but outside of it. Waymo, in response, wrote that the company takes "any situation involving threats, harassment or violence toward a rider extremely seriously."  It does, however, "... recognize that personal safety encompasses more than avoiding traffic collisions. It also involves how people experience transportation, the support available to them and their ability to make informed choices throughout a trip."

That being said, I asked Waymo how it can help riders in a variety of safety situations whether outside and inside the vehicle, taking into consideration that it's not just women who are concerned, parents might send their kids in Waymos, among many other individuals.

For instance, if a customer were attacked outside of a vehicle, there's not a ton a Waymo car can do. The rider can reach emergency services like 9-1-1 through the app or from their phones and, in some instances, Waymo says the vehicle may remain stationary until help arrives and communicate with people outside of the vehicle via its external speakers.

If a vehicle were to make a stop due to a technical issue someplace, the doors remain locked and it's recommended the rider stay in the vehicle until the issue is addressed or Waymo's Roadside Assistance arrives (if the situation requires it). In some cases a Waymo driver will arrive and get behind the wheel to help the vehicle autonomously navigate to the rider's destination.

In most any case, if something happens, riders are directed to call for assistance in the app, on their own by phone, or through the in-car screen. It's not a perfect system, as no one can control every element in every environment. The company earnestly takes it seriously though, and says it is always open to hearing more feedback.

What Waymo can't do unfortunately is fix behaviors that still remain rampant in American society in the 21st century, but hey, at least it can use the fears of those behaviors to advertise.

#5656. The BLUETTI Apex 200 Expandable Portable Power Station — CleanTechnica Tested

BLUETTI continues to innovate, finding new ways to move its portable power station technology into meaningful new formats that provide owners with additional capability, portability, and utility.

They recently reached out about a new stackable portable power station format based around the Apex 200 power station. It packs in a meaningful 2.5 kilowatt-hours (kWh) of storage capacity by itself, but the real magic is that it was built to be modular and can be linked up with additional storage modules to be stacked underneath, connecting directly to the main unit.

Disclaimer: BLUETTI provided the Apex 200, B300K, and a pair of solar panels to the author for the purposes of this review.

This modular format is a game changer because it means you don’t need an installer to bolt 300 pounds of batteries to the wall of your house. Instead, you can order them in manageable formats and add more as you need them. Among other things, this capability allows the owner to assemble and disassemble the battery pack that meets their needs when they need it.

If you’re heading out to camp for a week in your RV, you can grab an Apex 200 and one of BLUETTI’s B300K expansion batteries and bring 5.5 kWh of capacity with you on the road. When you get home from the trip, you can put them back into the garage and add that back into the battery capacity for your home.

The BLUETTI Apex 200 serves as a head unit or master controller for the battery stack, while the other battery modules are effectively servants to it. For example, when you’re setting the unit up to charge from a wall outlet or from a set of BLUETTI’s solar panels, which can wire directly into the Apex 200 with MC4 connectors, you simply plug one set of connectors or one AC charging cable into the Apex 200 and it automatically balances the energy storage across however many additional battery packs are plugged into the unit.

That really simplifies the charging structure and allows you to use the battery exactly how you want to. If you want to just head to the park with two solar panels that are portable, you can do that and have a much more manageable power system, effectively bringing a mini-grid with you on the go.

We tested the BLUETTI Apex 200 out on a wall charger and, much like most of the other portable power stations on the market, it effectively maxes out the capacity you can actually draw from a wall outlet, which is around 1,600 watts continuous. Even with the best charging cables, you’re going to notice some heat building up in the cable, and BLUETTI has made some concessions for this. The Apex 200 has a switch that allows you to select from different charging modes:

When it comes to solar, the BLUETTI Apex 200 can plug in to solar arrays through either an XT60 connector or the MC4 connectors that wire directly into the unit on the side. Between the MC4 connectors and the XT60 solar input, the unit can pull down 3,000 watts of solar, though you will need to make sure the voltage on your panels lines up with what each of the ports on the unit can handle. When we initially wired our two Bluetti 100-watt solar panels into the unit, we had them wired in parallel, which resulted in just 40 volts being pushed into the unit.

Stacking them in series gave us 80 volts, which pushed it over the minimum 60 volts for the MC4 connectors and allowed the BLUETTI Apex 200 to start charging from the panels. Pushing 2,000 watts maximum of solar power into a 5.5 kWh battery stack is going to take some time to get it charged up, but it’s easy to manage scaling the input power system up to an impressive 2,000 watts to really deliver some meaningful power.

The flexibility is pretty impressive, because it means you can dial the system in to exactly what you need, whether that’s bolting an array to the top of your RV or installing solar panels on the side of your house and then wiring them in a way that’s the most convenient for you.

BLUETTI also makes a larger B500K battery, so you can really pick and choose exactly the right level of storage that you need for your house and the right size battery packs that you want to be able to move around.

The MC4 connectors for solar panels are a little bit troublesome on the BLUETTI Apex 200 because they require a special tool to release the locks that connect the male and female ends of MC4 connectors together. That means if you have your solar panels wired up and plugged into the unit, you’ll need an MC4 connector release tool to disconnect the solar panels from the Apex 200 before you move on. Alternately, you can wire the solar panels up through an XT60 connector, which is a simple plug-and-play adapter for lower voltage solar setups.

When it came time to use the stored power from the BLUETTI Apex 200 and the B300K battery pack, we had to pick up some more high-draw appliances to really put them the the test. We bought a 1,500-watt electric fireplace and 1,500-watt portable heater for a total draw of around 3,000 watts by. We also grabbed an an Instant Pot from the kitchen to pull a bit more juice from the battery stack.

The BLUETTI Apex 200 handled both of the heaters running full blast without a problem. It shared a continuous power output capacity of 3,200 watts. This gets bumped to 3,600 watts when you attach a B300K extension battery. Beyond that, you can even pull a little bit more power, pulling peak usage up to 4,800 watts for a few seconds, because of its peak power handling capacity.

Keeping the larger heater unit attached, we added an Instapot to the the mix, and again the unit had no issues no matter what power levels we used. It’s a solid stack with impressive stats that actually deliver when you need them to the most.

BLUETTI has really built an impressive system here with the Apex 200 and its different expansion battery options. A single BLUETTI Apex 200 can be expanded up to 33.3 kWh, and they can even go bigger than that if you need something with more storage.

BLUETTI allows you to link three Apex 200 battery stacks together through their Hub 1 accessory for a total of just under 100 kWh of storage capacity. That can be accomplished by stacking three Apex 200s with a bunch of BLUETTI’s 5kWh B500Ks and then linking them all together through the Hub 1.

It’s a massive system that shows BLUETTI isn’t just building products for a quick sale, but building a much more comprehensive, flexible storage system that you can build at home into the right configuration for your needs with components that act more like LEGO building blocks.

The ability to wire in solar via XT60 connectors and MC4 connectors is exciting, giving homeowners even more options to produce power from the sun for them to run their lives on. The BLUETTI Apex 200 retails for $1,399 USD.

For more information about the BLUETTI Apex 200, the B300K, B500K expansion packs, or their solar panels, head over to their official website.

Live intentionally, make conscious decisions, love more, act responsibly, play. The more you know, the less you need. As an activist investor, Kyle owns long term stock holdings in Tesla and Rivian.

#5655. 314 Action Celebrates Its Victories in Getting Docs, Other Clinicians Elected

— "Things went even better than expected," said Shaughnessy Naughton, the group's founder

The midterm results earlier this month included the election of several physicians and other healthcare professionals to federal and statewide seats, especially on the Democratic side. MedPage Today Washington Editor Joyce Frieden spoke with Shaughnessy Naughton, founder and president of 314 Action, about the organization's role in helping to get some of these clinicians -- as well as scientists -- elected to office. The conversation, which was conducted over the phone with a public relations person present, has been edited for length and clarity.

How did your organization do in terms of seeing its chosen candidates elected?

Things went even better than expected. We are the only organization to elect Democratic scientists and doctors to all levels of government -- 14 members to the House, including 10 who took seats from Republicans -- two new doctors to the Senate, which hasn't had Democratic doctors serving in close to 60 years; and a Democratic doctor as governor in Ohio, the first woman doctor-governor in the country.

There's a couple of arms to that answer. First, we recruited and invested early in quality candidates. Additionally, this was a healthcare election and our candidates leaned into their backgrounds as doctors and as problem-solvers to present a clear contrast with their opponents that have overseen, particularly in the federal races, the slashing of Medicaid, the elimination of the [Affordable Care Act Enhanced Premium Tax Credit], the assault on rural health, and on and on.

Then you couple that with [HHS Secretary] Robert F. Kennedy Jr. -- the vaccine delusionist -- overseeing HHS during one health crisis after another, leading to preventable deaths from measles and preventable diseases. So I think all of that compounded to really create a lane that voters saw as a viable and important alternative to what they currently had.

Annie Andrews, MD, [D-S.C. senator-elect] is at the top of that list. South Carolina hasn't elected a Democrat statewide, I think, in over 20 years. It is very hard to change people's voting patterns, but it does speak to the strength of her message and her candidacy that she was able to not only get elected, but she brought along a congressional race in South Carolina -- one that flipped to Democrat -- and 10 statehouse seats. Wow!

We are really looking forward to what she can accomplish in the Senate. I was having coffee this morning with a senator who had just met her at orientation this week, and he said he told her to "Lean in and make sure your voice is being heard because we really need you right now." I think that that is true coming from the senator's mouth, but I think it's also true coming from the voters' mouth.

You mentioned the vaccine issue and the Affordable Care Act tax credits. What other healthcare issues are you hoping to see acted on in the new Congress?

We were promised [by the current administration and Congress] to see a reduction in healthcare costs, and we've seen the exact opposite. Premiums continue to skyrocket. Out-of-pocket costs and deductibles just keep going up, and there doesn't seem to be much relief on prescription drug prices either.

This is at a time when we're having record numbers of foodborne illness outbreaks and measles deaths and spread. The threshold for herd immunity is 95% for measles, and most kindergarteners' classes are below that at this point. So it really does call to have strong health leaders leading with facts and evidence and their values to protect Americans' health and make healthcare more affordable.

We are looking for action on healthcare affordability and accessibility, and holding the administration accountable for what, to say the least, have been questionable choices such as the approval of candy-flavored vapes, so we do expect that Congress should be able to deliver on both.

Even though they don't have enough votes to override a presidential veto?

What happens remains to be seen. The moves from the administration do not [suggest] that they want to work with Democrats, but the remaining Republicans in the House still have elections in 2 years, and the American people spoke loud and clear that they want relief from high prices across the board.

Can you explain more about your organization's role? I understand you spent more than $30 million this cycle. Do you recruit as well as contribute financially?

Yes, we are involved in recruiting. One of the things we're seeing bearing fruit is an investment at the state and local level. One of the people we endorsed in the 2018 cycle was [microbiologist] Jasmine Clark, PhD. She ran in a Republican district in the Georgia statehouse and flipped the seat. This year she stepped up to run for a congressional seat, and won.

We work with campaigns, helping them put together their campaign strategy, a financing plan, and consulting teams. We find staff and work at the grassroots level with them. Often our campaign staff will be in the district with them when they launch, and our communications staff works with them on media training.

We have individual donors; this cycle we had 150,000 individual contributors. Their first donation average is $10; the lifetime average is $17. People usually donate three times a year and many have been donating to us for 3, 4, or 5 years. We do a lot of grassroots fundraising, and we also have been the beneficiary of [former New York City Mayor] Michael Bloomberg's generosity this cycle, which makes a lot of sense because he is someone invested in public health and public health education.

We have candidates fill out a questionnaire, which is on our website. And we are already thinking about 2028 -- even 2027 has some local elections [of interest]. We need to play everywhere. When you run credible candidates, they can resonate and make a difference and get elected.

What is your background and how did you end up starting 314 Action?

I was a chemist and worked in breast cancer research and drug discovery, and I worked on creating an antibiotic to combat superbugs. In 2014, I became concerned by the antiscience rhetoric coming out of so many politicians, so I decided to run for Congress in Bucks County, Pennsylvania, where I'm from. But I didn't have 314 Action and was locked out of the [standard] donor network, so I started cold-calling chemists and making my case and building out a network from there.

Ultimately, I didn't win, but I learned a lot in the process and part of what I learned is that it's very hard to break into politics when you don't have that traditional political background. And so I founded 314 Action to break down those barriers, to recruit, train, and elect people with scientific backgrounds, with the thinking that there isn't an issue that doesn't benefit by having data-driven analytical thinkers actually at the governing table.

#5654. At $13,000, Would You Add This 1997 Pontiac Trans-Am WS6 To Your Wish List?

With its WS6 RPO-optioned ram-air induction, today's Nice Price or No Dice Trans-Am has over 300 horsepower to play with. Let's see if the seller's set price means they're playing with us.

Europa, the celestial body, is the smallest and least massive of Jupiter's Galilean moons. Notably, it also has the closest orbit to its host planet, and tidal forces from the gas giant have molded the moon's surface into an almost smooth landscape, devoid of the craters and topographical deviations common on other otherworldly bodies.

Small, light, and smooth also describes the 1973 Lotus Europa John Player Special we looked at on Tuesday, proving Colin Chapman's marketing savvy was on point. Our car was number 90 out of 100 Lotus built in the iconic black-and-gold livery of the company's Formula One race team's sponsor, John Player & Sons, celebrating the team's 1972 World Championship win.

At $27,000, few of you wanted our Europa in your orbit, with many of the comments focusing negatively on the car's odd, semi-El Camino looks. In the end, voters overlooked that personal taste, paving the way for the Europa's narrow 54% 'Nice Price' win.

Unlike Lotus, which expanded its racing efforts into many series, including Formula 1, Indy cars, and the GT class, Pontiac largely limited its factory-supported on-track heroics to the drag strip, the NASCAR circuit, and the namesake series of today's 1997 Pontiac Trans-Am.

Boutique cars are all the rage today, with companies like Mini and Porsche allowing buyers to choose from a plethora of add-on options to personalize their cars to the nth degree. That wasn't always the case, though; back in the day, an automaker's option sheet let a buyer craft a car to fit their needs, but within limits. At General Motors, that option list has long been denoted by three letters: RPO. They stand for "Regular Production Option," and have given rise to many package codes denoting now-venerated performance options like the WS6 group on this Trans-Am.

First introduced in 1978, the WS6 Special Performance Package was originally positioned as a handling upgrade. The package included snowflake alloy wheels fitted with fatter-than-stock tires and a rear anti-roll bar. Later editions added four-wheel disc brakes. For our car, WS6 still means bigger wheels and tires and more aggressive suspension tuning, but it notably adds a ram-air induction intake fed by two functional nostrils fronting the car's blistered hood.

That ram-air induction feeds an LT1 V8, kindly provided by the capable engine builders over at Chevrolet. This was the last year for the LT1, as Pontiac introduced the LS1 the following year. That engine had previously been exclusive to the Corvette. With the big gulp air intake, the 350 CID LT1 still puts out a very healthy 305 hp and 335 pound-feet of torque, significant increases over the standard engine's 285 hp and 325 lb-ft.

Here, that mill is mated to a 4L60E four-speed automatic, something the seller touts in the ad, claiming this to be one of only 347 auto-equipped WS6 cars produced this year. The ad also boasts that "Everything works like it came off the showroom floor."

It's not all stock, though. The ad claims a replacement quad-tip exhaust with Flowmaster mufflers, new poly suspension bushings, and KYB shocks as notable updates. There are 79,778 miles on the clock, a modest number, but still called out in the ad, with the seller explaining, "While it has changed hands among enthusiasts, the low 79k mileage and remarkable preservation proves it has spent its life as a garage-kept weekend toy rather than a daily driver."

Back in 1997, a corral of 300 horses was a lot. Today, even a four-cylinder Mustang offers more than that and will easily outdo this Trans-Am in any dimension of performance. That doesn't mean this cool red-over-white convertible isn't worth a look. First off, when did you last see a classic Trans-Am like this? I bet it was at a Cars & Coffee or other enthusiast enclave. That makes it interesting simply because of its uniqueness. Then there are the pop-up headlamps. We love those guys! This is also, as the ad touts, an incredibly well-maintained example of the breed, needing nothing but the next owner to agree not to mess it up.

Yes, the interior is gawd-awful, with miserable hard plastic surfaces, wide gaps between panels, and buttons that look like they came straight from the bargain bin at Temu, but that's just how Pontiac (and pretty much all of GM) rolled in this era. At least the seats look comfortable as all get out, and the car has all the niceties one could want, including manual climate, cruise, power windows, locks and top, and a CD stereo. As an added incentive, the title is as clean as the car.

Okay, so if anyone is in the market for a last-of-the-Mohicans Trans-Am, this is a strong candidate. Yes, later cars with the LS do perform better and have more aggressive styling, if that's your thing. On the other hand, they also tend to command substantially higher prices. This car's $13,000 asking price might make up for that performance gap.

We'll now need to debate that question, as that's what we do here. With that in mind, what's your take on this Trans-Am and that $13,000 asking price? Does the WS6 package sway your opinion of the car's value? Or is that immaterial, and is spending more on a later, more capable car really the way to go?

Atlanta, Georgia, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5653. Groups Decry Trump EPA’s Decision to Grant Louisiana Oversight of Toxic Coal Ash

Testing at all Louisiana coal plants found unsafe groundwater contamination.

BATON ROUGE, La. — The Sierra Club, Earthjustice, and the Alliance for Affordable Energy Louisiana decried the Trump administration’s final decision today to put the State of Louisiana in charge of oversight of toxic coal ash waste. The Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality (LDEQ) has repeatedly allowed coal plants to violate federal coal ash protections.

Coal ash is generated when coal is burned, and massive quantities are stored at coal-burning power plants near Baton Rouge, Alexandria, and Shreveport.

At all three active Louisiana coal plants (Big Cajun 2, Brame Energy Center, R S Nelson), and the state’s one retired plant (Dolet Hills), monitoring data shows that coal ash is contaminating groundwater at levels above federal safe drinking water standards. These toxic chemicals include arsenic, boron, cobalt, lead, lithium, radium, sulfate, and thallium. More than 61% of Louisiana’s population relies on groundwater for all or part of their drinking water supply.

“Louisianans know all too well that we can’t trust our state regulators to hold polluters accountable, so it’s nonsensical that the Trump Administration just gave LDEQ even more authority to handle something as dangerous as coal ash,” said Darryl Malek-Wiley, a Sierra Club senior field representative in Louisiana. “It’s clear this decision is meant to favor wealthy coal-owning utilities at the expense of our health.”

“The Trump administration chose polluters over the people of Louisiana,” said Earthjustice senior attorney Mychal Ozaeta. “An alphabet soup of toxic chemicals from burning coal is contaminating groundwater in locations across the state. The right thing to do is make the polluters clean up their toxic mess, and protect sources of drinking water. Instead, the Trump administration is handing over authority to Louisiana’s state government which won’t lift a finger to clamp down on the concerning levels of contaminants poisoning the state’s groundwater.”

“Our state regulators are dangerously inadequate in their oversight and enforcement of polluting industries, which has already cost Louisiana our safe air and water. We simply don’t think LDEQ can handle even more responsibilities they won’t take seriously,” said Logan Burke, Executive Director of the Alliance for Affordable Energy.

Public interest groups submitted formal comments to EPA urging the administration to reject Louisiana’s request to handle coal ash oversight in the state.

Coal ash contains arsenic, mercury, chromium, lead, lithium, radium, and other heavy metals, which have been linked to numerous types of cancer, heart and thyroid disease, respiratory illness, reproductive failure, and neurological harm.

As an example of the state’s abysmal track record on coal ash, LDEQ has allowed power plants to close coal ash dumps with ash in groundwater, ensuring that the dumps will contaminate groundwater with toxic chemicals indefinitely. LDEQ should not be in charge of issuing permits that let coal plants continue to violate the law. These permits will shield power plants from federal enforcement and prevent Louisiana residents from stopping coal ash pollution.

LDEQ is a severely understaffed and extremely lenient environmental regulator. In addition to its coal ash failures, its air monitors are far away from Louisiana’s heaviest air-polluting facilities, and the agency has for years been criticized for approving permits for wealthy polluters, including LNG terminals and a massive Formosa plastics factory. Louisianans know all too well that LDEQ does not protect low-income communities of color, and creates a lax regulatory environment that has enabled disasters like the recent Smitty’s Supply explosion and spill.

Please see the Earthjustice Louisiana coal ash page.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5652. 2027 Subaru Forester Wilderness Hybrid Is The Happy Wanderer

Subaru has a knack for making its vehicles incredibly capable right out of the box, but it also knows how to add extra garnishes to take things to the next level, like when it Wilderness-ified the standard Forester by bumping up the ride height and giving it all-terrain tires, more underbody protection, tougher exterior body panels and a slew of copper accents. Now Subaru is taking that notch up another notch by hybrid-ifying the Wilderness-ified Forester, resulting in — you guessed it — the 2027 Forester Wilderness Hybrid.

The hybrid powertrain offers more power, better fuel economy and a more refined drivetrain experience than the standard Wilderness' 2.5-liter boxer four-cylinder, while also affording the Forester a bit more off-road capability thanks to the added torque of an electric motor. Making things even sweeter is the fact that choosing the hybrid only adds $1,000 to the Forester Wilderness' MSRP, a delta that Subaru says will be overcome in just 2 years, or about 30,000 miles.

It makes it a bit hard to understand why the regular Forester Wilderness exists at all. The added fuel efficiency and comfort mean the Forester Wilderness Hybrid can do a seemingly endless amount of driving, exploring and wandering around, and it's absolutely happy to do so.

Full Disclosure: Subaru flew me out to central California, put me up in a very nice hotel and fed me, all so I could drive the 2027 Forester Wilderness Hybrid. They sourced me a lovely 2026 Ascent Touring to drive all the way from Chicago to New York when every flight in the U.S. was grounded, and I faced the prospect of spending a few days sleeping on the floor at O'Hare. For that reason, I am eternally grateful to both Aaron Cole and Miranda Jimenez.

There isn't much of a difference between the Forester Wilderness and Forester Wilderness Hybrid outside of the hybrid powertrain, but it does a hell of a lot to transform the character of Subie's compact crossover. Like in the non-Wilderness Forester Hybrid, Subaru teamed up an Atkinson-Miller-cycle 2.5-liter boxer four-cylinder with two motor-generator units inside the continuously variable transmission. One is used to stop and start the gas engine and charge the battery, while the other handles regenerative braking and sends actual drive power to all four wheels. They're fed by a 1.1-kWh battery that lives where the spare tire once was, which may be disappointing to some off-road enthusiasts who also love busting tires. However, that positioning means the hybrid has the same exact amount of rear cargo room as a regular Forester, which normal people are going to appreciate.

They're also going to appreciate the extra power. The Forester Wilderness Hybrid pumps out 194 horsepower, a 14-hp improvement over the normal Forester Wilderness. Subaru gives no combined torque figures, but the gas motor puts out 154 pound-feet, and the electric motor produces its own 199 lb-ft. Either way you slice it, the numbers are stronger than the 178 lb-ft of torque from the normal Forester Wilderness. In typical Subaru fashion, the symmetrical all-wheel-drive system sends internal-combustion power to all four wheels — unlike the RAV4, which has no mechanical connection between the front and rear wheels — and that makes a huge difference off-road.

Gas mileage has never been a Subie strong suit, but the hybrid's fuel economy is also much improved to go along with its added performance. According to the EPA, the Forester Wilderness Hybrid will get 33 mpg city, 29 mpg highway and 31 mpg combined, improvements of 9 mpg, 1 mpg and 5 mpg, respectively. The EPA-expected driving range is also bumped up from 432 miles to 515 miles. Happy wanderer, indeed.

The Forester Wilderness' lovably ugly looks are still present, and the hybrid maintains the same 9.3 inches of ground clearance as its gas-only counterpart. It also gets the same 235/60R17 Yokohama Geolander All-Terrain tires, raised roof rails with 800 pounds of capacity, bronze interior and exterior design elements, and redesigned front and rear bumpers with increased approach, departure and breakover angles.

Hill descent control and Subaru's X-Mode system with Snow/Dirt and Deep Snow/Mud settings mean it's pretty much impossible to get this thing stuck; those two modes can basically be summed up as stuck and absolutely biffed. They allow the Forester Wilderness Hybrid to do pretty much anything off-road; at the very least, it can do things far beyond what anyone would expect from a car without a locking rear differential.

The majority of the time I drove the Forester Wilderness Hybrid was without any pavement below me on the seemingly endless acreage of Rancho Quien Sabe, and I've got to say, it was nearly boring in the way the SUV saw any obstacle in front of it and just traversed it without fuss or hesitation. The Forester happily trudged along, unbothered by what sort of terrain it was forced to deal with. In the rare moments where I found myself a bit more stuck than I thought I should be, all that was needed was a little bit of extra power, and the added torque of the electric motor pulled me out with ease. Aiding in that was the 180-degree front view monitor that helped me see where I was going, but while it was undoubtedly helpful, I can't help but think the resolution left a bit to be desired.

I'm no fool, and I know that for all of its off-road prowess, the Forester Wilderness Hybrid will spend the majority of its life on road. Luckily, it's quite good there. Subaru did a good job of insulating the cabin so not too much road noise or rattly boxer sounds enter the cabin, and the more powerful drivetrain means it's even better at getting up to speed than the slightly anemic normal Forester Wilderness. It also comes as standard with all sorts of safety features, courtesy of Subaru's EyeSight suite of tech like emergency lane-keeping assist, emergency steering assist, blind spot detection, rear cross-traffic alert, reverse automated braking, and full-speed adaptive cruise control. (I was able to truly experience the highs of EyeSight during my 820-mile drive home in the Ascent).

Changes to the Forester Wilderness Hybrid are decidedly minimal on the inside, too. Drivers are still treated to a 10-way power seat, and in front of the steering wheel is a fairly customizable 12.3-inch gauge cluster screen that can display a full-screen map. Beside that in the middle is an 11.6-inch vertical infotainment screen, which is getting harder and harder to justify as the years pass — especially now that the new Outback is out, and its system is so good. Luckily, you can hook up wireless Apple CarPlay or Android Auto, but even they are a bit laggy, and glare is still prevalent when it's super sunny.

Screen woes aside, the animal-free interior is fairly well appointed for this type of vehicle. Dual-zone climate controls, a panoramic-ish sunroof, TomTom navigation, a heated steering wheel, four heated seats, a power tailgate, all-weather floor mats and an 11-speaker Harman/Kardon stereo are all standard on the Forester Wilderness Hybrid.

Space is also very solid for the class. I had more than enough room to fit comfortably behind myself at 6-foot-1, and there's 33.1 cubic feet of room behind the second row for all of your Outdoor Activity Crap. If you somehow need more room for your Outdoor Activity Crap, drop the second row, and that space increases to 64.1 cubic feet. If that somehow still isn't enough for your Outdoor Activity Crap, you can strap 800 pounds to the roof (statically). There are enough interesting materials choices and quality-feeling items that you won't feel too silly about the cost.

Subaru says the 2027 Forester Wilderness Hybrid starts at $41,970, including destination, and that's pretty much where pricing ends, as well. Outside of a few optional paint colors, there aren't any packages you'll be able to add to the Wilderness Hybrid. What you see is what you get. Nice and easy. And as I mentioned before, it's just $1,000 more than the standard Forester Wilderness. There's a hard deal to beat.

The Forester Hybrid Wilderness has a real can-do attitude to it. It's a real billy goat of a car — one that takes a licking and keeps on kicking, happily coming back for more. When driving, it was as if the Forester was whistling a little tune to itself as I mashed the throttle, forcing it to traverse up a hill a compact crossover has no business going up.

No, it doesn't have the best infotainment in the world and it's not a classically "beautiful" vehicle, but it has something most cars don't: character. The Forester Wilderness Hybrid wants to get lost with you, and all you need to do is make sure you've got the time to get lost with it, because with over 500 miles of driving range, you can get lost for a long, long time.

#5651. NEW: Sierra Club Report Shows Data Centers’ Potential to Erase 15 Years of Climate Progress

Despite Americans’ Disapproval, Data Centers Rush to Build Climate-Threatening Projects

Washington, D.C. — Sierra Club has released a new report that shows that without swift, critical guardrails, artificial intelligence data centers in the United States could undo 15 years of climate progress.

The power demand brought on by data centers combined with utility companies’ rush to fossil fuels has severe and threatening climate impacts. The Artificial Intelligence, Real Pollution report shows that between data centers connected to the electrical grid and those that are building their own on-site power supply, these projects could add 680 million tons of greenhouse gas emissions into the atmosphere.

Sierra Club’s Dirty Truth Report, released in September 2026, reflected the alarming rate at which utility companies are building new methane gas power plants to power artificial intelligence data centers. Today’s report shows that additionally, data center projects are building massive amounts of on-site power sources that are subject to more lenient guardrails and reporting standards.

This report comes on the heels of the 2026 midterm elections, in which voters across the country overwhelmingly supported candidates who promised more guardrails and accountability on data center projects. Voters also passed nearly every ballot measure that would slow the breakneck speed at which data centers are developing. Artificial Intelligence, Real Pollution does not just outline the real climate risk that the industry’s current growth trajectory poses, it shares substantive and impactful strategies that states can implement right now to prevent harmful emissions from gas plants while protecting ratepayers from rising costs caused by data center demand.

In response, Sierra Club Chief Program Officer Holly Bender issued the following response:

“For twenty years, the Sierra Club has led the environmental movement’s most successful campaign—the Beyond Coal Campaign—with a clear-eyed vision that a clean power grid is the backbone of any climate strategy. Millions of people have contributed to this work, advocating for clean air, clean water, and investments in clean energy across the country. We face an unprecedented risk to our hard-won progress, as the picture of the climate impact of artificial intelligence data centers and their power demand becomes clear. We now know that as currently planned, this industry could put all of our progress at risk.

“Artificial Intelligence, Real Pollution shows the staggering amount of greenhouse gases that would be emitted into our air if we allow data centers and utility companies to continue their rush to fossil fuels. More pollution will worsen the extreme weather families are facing, cause illnesses, and drive up the cost of healthcare and disaster recovery. Just two weeks ago, Americans unequivocally demanded more accountability, more transparency, and more guardrails on AI data centers. With this report, the Sierra Club has not only clarified what’s at stake, but shared a menu of strategies that can be implemented right now to protect consumers, communities, and the climate.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5650. FDA Adds Another Player in ALK-Positive Lung Cancer

— Among the indications, neladalkib approved in second-line after alectinib

The FDA granted accelerated approval to the third-generation ALK inhibitor neladalkib (Nelkarmi) for previously treated advanced ALK-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), making it the seventh approved agent in a crowded therapeutic field.

The approval limits use to patients with unresectable locally advanced or metastatic ALK-positive NSCLC previously treated with alectinib (Alecensa) or at least two prior ALK tyrosine kinase inhibitors and platinum-based chemotherapy.

Support for the conditional approval came from 222 patients in the phase I/II single-arm ALKOVE-1 trial.

In the cohort of 67 patients who had only received alectinib in the metastatic setting, neladalkib induced responses in 49%, with a duration of response (DOR) of at least 6 months in 76% of the responding patients. For the cohort of 155 patients previously treated with two or more ALK inhibitors and platinum-based chemotherapy, 32% responded, with a DOR of at least 6 months for 69% of the responders. The proportion of responders with DORs of at least 12 months was 52% and 51% for the two cohorts, respectively.

"Patients with ALK-positive lung cancer are often treated with multiple targeted therapies over many years," said investigator Alice Shaw, MD, PhD, of Dana-Farber Cancer Institute in Boston, in a press release from drugmaker GSK. "Advances in treatment have brought us closer to the goal of managing ALK-positive lung cancer as a chronic condition, but balancing disease control and long-term tolerability remains a significant challenge. The approval of neladalkib provides an important new treatment option for patients."

An analysis reported at the 2026 American Society of Clinical Oncology (ASCO) meeting showed a response rate of 26% among patients with exposure to lorlatinib (Lorbrena), the only other approved third-generation ALK inhibitor, versus 46% in patients not previously treated with lorlatinib. DORs of at least 6 or 12 months in the lorlatinib-treated subgroup occurred in 68% and 54% of responders.

In the full cohort of patients presented at the meeting (median three prior lines of therapy), landmark analyses of progression-free survival (PFS) showed a 6-month PFS of 50%, 12-month PFS of 33%, and 18-month PFS of 28% for patients treated with neladalkib. Median PFS was 5.7 months, increasing to 14.5 months in patients not previously treated with lorlatinib. Additionally, a third of patients with brain metastases had objective responses with neladalkib.

At the ASCO meeting, invited discussant Angel Qin, MD, of the University of Michigan in Ann Arbor, said the ALKOVE-1 results suggest neladalkib has potential as an option in the post-lorlatinib setting, albeit with relatively modest activity, and a manageable side-effect profile.

The most common adverse events (AEs) were elevated liver enzymes, which occurred in almost half of patients and was grade ≥3 severity in 15-20% of patients. Other AEs occurring in at least 20% of patients were musculoskeletal pain, constipation, dysgeusia, peripheral neuropathy, edema, and fatigue. Prescribing information for neladalkib includes warnings and precautions for hepatotoxicity, renal toxicity, ocular toxicity, creatine phosphokinase elevation, and embryo-fetal toxicity.

The ongoing phase III ALKAZAR trial of neladalkib in the first-line setting may provide more information about the drug's potential as upfront therapy in advanced ALK-positive NSCLC. A preliminary analysis from ALKOVE-1 at ASCO showed an objective response rate of 86% for patients with no prior ALK-targeted therapy.

#5649. When Less Is More: Rewilding as a Strategy for Nature Recovery

Can we bring nature back from the brink of collapse? That may sound melodramatic, but the evidence is sobering: the Planetary Health Check 2026 shows that we have crossed 7 of the 9 science-based planetary boundaries;[1] the Living Planet Report 2026 finds that wildlife populations have declined 73% on average since 1970;[2] and around 1 million animal and plant species are threatened with extinction, with 75% of the land surface having been significantly altered by human activity, according to the IPBES, the leading intergovernmental body for assessing the state of biodiversity.[3]

In fact, with vertebrate extinction rates up to 100 times the natural background rate, some scientists believe we are at the onset of a sixth mass extinction.[4] But perhaps we should not be surprised at this precipitous decline in the health of our living planet. After all, around $7.3 trillion is spent every year on activities that harm nature ($4.9 trillion by companies and $2.4 trillion in government subsidies), while only $220 billion goes towards protecting or restoring ecosystems (2023 figures).[5] In other words, for every $1 we spend on taking care of nature, $33 is spent on activities that degrade the environment.

The drivers of nature loss are many, including climate change, overexploitation of resources, pollution and invasive alien species, but the largest is land-use change, primarily for agriculture.[6] Research by UNEP and Chatham House goes so far as to identify agriculture as a threat to 86% of species at risk of extinction.[7] So, is regenerative agriculture the answer? That is certainly one approach that would help, as I explored in a previous CleanTechnica article. But there is another, more direct—and some would say more radical—solution that is gaining popularity: rewilding.

As with regenerative agriculture, rewilding is more a collection of ideas and practices than a well-defined science. Nevertheless, experts convened by the IUCN define rewilding as the process of rebuilding a natural ecosystem, following major human disturbance, by restoring natural processes and food webs.[8] The Global Rewilding Alliance puts it even more simply, as “helping nature heal”.[9] In practice, this can mean anything from leaving areas of nature undisturbed to actively introducing keystone species such as beavers, bison and wild boar.

Perhaps the most cited rewilding experiment is the reintroduction of wolves into Yellowstone National Park in 1995–96, after they had been eradicated there by 1926. This resulted in a so-called trophic cascade, whereby elk numbers fell, scavengers such as eagles, ravens and bears gained a year-round food supply, and willow and aspen began to recover in places.[10] The strength of that recovery, and the extent to which the wolves were the primary driver, has been contested,[11] but few dispute the overall positive effect on biodiversity and the ecosystem.

The Yellowstone case is rewilding on a large scale, but it is still dwarfed by the rewilding work of Tompkins Conservation, created by the late Doug Tompkins, co-founder of The North Face and Esprit, and his wife Kristine McDivitt Tompkins, former CEO of Patagonia. Working primarily in Chile and Argentina, they have helped create or expand 16 parks protecting over 45 million acres of land and sea, and reintroduced 14 species, including around 50 wild jaguars.[12] Besides the ecological benefits, ecotourism is boosting local economies.[13] In Chilean Patagonia, every dollar the state invested in the national parks along Chile’s Route of Parks in 2024 returned US$6 to local communities,[14] while visitors to Argentina’s Iberá Park, partly degraded by cattle ranching in the past, have doubled in a decade to 60,000 a year.[15]

At a more local scale, the Knepp Estate in the UK has become another well documented case of rewilding. In her book Wilding: The Return of Nature to a British Farm, Isabella Tree tells the story of how she and her husband, Charlie Burrell, converted their 1,400-hectare farm from intensive dairy and crop agriculture, which was losing money, into a thriving “wild farm,” with free-roaming herbivores such as cattle, ponies, pigs, and deer.[16] A 20-year review shows an incredible rejuvenation of biodiversity, including a 916% increase in breeding bird abundance in the southern block, a 132% rise in bird species richness, a near-ninefold increase in dragonflies and damselflies, and a doubling of butterfly species in some areas.[17] There are climate benefits too, with 5.2 tCO2e/ha/yr sequestration, comparable to new native woodland.[18]

Critics of rewilding often frame it as a trade-off: more nature means less food; more wildlife means fewer jobs; more biodiversity means lower economic growth. These arguments can (and should) be tested, for in many (but not all) cases they will prove to be a false choice. My view is that it should be about balance. I saw this pragmatic approach at a rewilding site in Norfolk, England, called Wild Ken Hill, where they have designated separate areas for regenerative farming, coastal conservation, and rewilding—including 30 or so wild Exmoor ponies, 6 very inquisitive and vocal Tamworth pigs, and a small herd of Red Poll cattle.

My conclusion on rewilding is that it can certainly help us to reach the global goal of reversing biodiversity loss and becoming nature positive. It is by no means the only strategy, but it is one of the most direct and impactful. As I noted in the chapter on nature restoration in my book Thriving: “We know that nature is on the ropes, bruised and battered by dumb agriculture and dumber economics. But another thing we know is that, given half a chance, nature bounces back. Ecosystems are resilient and designed to flourish. The most powerful and effective action we can take is to step back and give nature space and time to recover.”[19]

This article is adapted and updated from content in Wayne Visser’s book “Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

[1] Planetary Boundaries Science (PBScience) (2026). Planetary Health Check 2026. Potsdam Institute for Climate Impact Research, Potsdam. planetaryhealthcheck.org

#5648. What's The Story Of The First Time You Ever Drove A Car?

We know you're an automotive enthusiast if you're a regular Jalopnik reader, which means you probably have a pretty good recollection of the very first time you ever drove a car. I'm not certain what the statute of limitations is for driving a car without a license, so hopefully you don't perjure yourself by sharing your story, because we want to hear all about it! Share the story of the first time you ever drove a car in the comments section below.

RuPaul actually inspired this question. On social media he recently told the story of the first time he ever drove a car at 11-years-old, when he stole the keys to his dad's 1966 Toyota Corona and drove it around Cerritos, California. RuPaul describes himself as someone who's obsessed with cars, and I know most of us Jalops would describe ourselves in a similar fashion. That's why we want to know the story of the first time you ever drove a car

I'm not sure that I've even shared this story with my friends or family, but sharing it here seems like a good idea. I don't remember my exact age at the time, but I know I was in middle school, so it was definitely before I should have legally been behind the wheel. Still, I'd been practicing my driving in my conscious and unconscious mind for at least a decade before this, so I felt ready.

One fateful summer day at Model Boat Shop (don't laugh, it was really fun), a friend's older sister came to pick him up, but her boyfriend was the one who drove. Along with my brother, our conniving, car-obsessed minds hatched a brilliant plan to strongarm this poor high school kid into letting us drive his green second-generation Nissan Altima. We convinced him it would earn him brownie points with his girlfriend if he'd let her little brother and his friends drive his car, and he bought it, hook line and sinker.

He took us to a nearby parking lot and let us loose, and I have the most distinct memory of doing incredibly safe and not-at-all dangerous acceleration runs and panic brake evaluations in that parking lot while the owner of the car looked on in horror. Thankfully nobody got hurt and nobody got sued, but that joyous memory was the first time I drove a car. What's your story?

#5647. China’s Electric Heavy Trucks Are Taking Sales from Diesel and LNG

In September 2026, battery-electric trucks captured 49.8% of China’s new domestic heavy-truck registrations. Of 65,800 trucks registered, 32,800 ran on batteries. Another 36 used hydrogen fuel cells. Battery electric accounted for more than 99% of the broader new-energy category. China’s freight operators are buying batteries at a scale that puts diesel and LNG suppliers under pressure.

The shift has gathered speed. Battery electric accounted for about 13% of domestic heavy-truck registrations in 2024, 28% in 2025 and nearly 38% in the first nine months of 2026. The figures come from compulsory-insurance registrations for trucks sold into the domestic market, excluding exports. China’s total domestic truck market remains below its pre-pandemic levels. Battery trucks are gaining share while the market for new heavy trucks remains constrained.

From May through September, new-energy heavy trucks exceeded 40% of new registrations for five straight months. The category reached approximately 226,400 vehicles in the first nine months, up 64% from the same period in 2025, and nearly 99% were battery electric. Tractor units that pull semi-trailers are leading the change. In September, 64.48% of new domestic tractor registrations belonged to the new-energy category. Electric trucks are moving freight on ordinary commercial routes, including work beyond ports and industrial yards.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing traces China’s heavy-truck sales from 2015 through 2026, examines real freight costs and charging choices, and assesses the consequences for manufacturers, logistics companies and fuel demand.

The operating economics explain much of the appeal. Trucks earn money by carrying cargo, so annual kilometres, payload, electricity prices, charging stops and vehicle availability matter as much as the price on the invoice. High-mileage routes make electricity and maintenance savings more valuable. A costly grid connection or charging queue can eat into those savings. Fleet operators need to evaluate the truck and its route together.

Recent ICCT case studies document how those economics play out. A Guangdong warehouse fleet of 28 electric trucks had estimated five-year ownership costs 16% below comparable diesel trucks. In Yangtze River Delta express delivery, 50 electric tractor-trailers working routes of 300–500 km achieved a narrower 3% advantage over six years without purchase incentives. Another operation covering 900 km a day with strategically placed high-power charging showed estimated ownership costs 19% below diesel. The differences reflect route, utilization and charging arrangements.

Those three operations offer a more useful procurement lesson than a single quoted range figure. The operator starts with cargo, schedules and energy access, then selects equipment. The narrow 3% margin in express delivery also shows why financing and charging costs can determine whether a viable electric route makes money.

Battery swapping has carved out a sizable part of this market. In September, 20.09% of newly registered battery-electric heavy trucks were classified as swap-capable. That compares with 22.92% across January–September 2026 and 30.39% for all of 2025. The number of swap-classified trucks continued to rise, while conventional charging gained a larger share of the expanding electric market. China’s registration classification also includes some trucks with leased batteries that are recharged rather than exchanged.

Swapping fits dense, repeatable freight operations such as steelworks, ports and bulk-material haulage. Fleet operators and station owners can standardize vehicles and use stations often enough to pay for batteries held in inventory. Conventional charging serves a wider range of routes and can fit scheduled stops, overnight parking and depot operations. Both approaches depend on predictable truck traffic and reliable electricity connections.

China is building infrastructure around those freight patterns. Yunnan commissioned 3,350 km of heavy-truck charging corridors in 2025, with 113 stations and 869 connectors. Fujian opened a 649 km coastal corridor in October 2026, including 15 charging stations and eight swapping stations. The IEA’s 2026 charging review describes the concentration of heavy-vehicle charging around freight corridors and industrial centres. Long-haul coverage still has gaps, but the network is expanding where trucks already have work to do.

Hydrogen has found little traction alongside this expansion. China registered 689 fuel-cell heavy trucks in the first nine months of 2026, compared with more than 223,000 battery-electric trucks. LNG has a much larger installed customer base, although its advantage over diesel has narrowed as gas prices change. Battery trucks offer a different cost structure built around drivetrain efficiency and lower maintenance needs. That makes the competition increasingly difficult for equipment and fuel suppliers dependent on combustion engines.

Sales changes take years to work through the operating fleet. Kpler projects that new-energy trucks, predominantly battery electric, will account for about 8% of Chinese heavy trucks in service at the end of 2026. It also estimates they will displace about 230,000 barrels of diesel per day compared with moving the same freight using diesel trucks. Freight vehicles with high daily utilization can have a large effect on fuel demand before they become a majority of vehicles on the road.

China’s June 2026 heavy-truck programme joins purchasing incentives, grid access, charging corridors, financing and logistics planning. Its 2030 targets include a 40% share of new-energy heavy-truck sales, a 20% share of the operating fleet and 30,000 km of zero-carbon freight corridors. Monthly new sales have already surpassed the sales-share target. Building and replacing enough vehicles to reach the fleet target will take longer.

Other countries have different electricity tariffs, road networks and freight routes, but the procurement method travels. Start with the actual jobs trucks perform. Identify routes with strong electric economics, plan the charging and grid connections those routes require, and finance vehicles and infrastructure as one programme. Other drivetrains can compete where their operating costs or performance are better. The evidence from China’s largest commercial freight applications increasingly favours batteries.

The complete TFIE Strategy Briefing includes the historical registration series, the ICCT ownership-cost cases, charging and swapping corridors, and fleet-turnover analysis. TFIE Strategy works with fleets, utilities and policymakers on freight-electrification scenarios and investment decisions.
