#5646. Erectile Dysfunction Drug Shows Mixed Results in Alzheimer's

— AR1001 missed the primary endpoint in one population, but slowed decline in another

Mirodenafil (known as AR1001), an oral phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitor approved in South Korea to treat erectile dysfunction, showed mixed results in people with early Alzheimer's disease in the phase III POLARIS-AD trial.

The treatment missed the primary endpoint in one prespecified group but showed positive results in another, reported Sharon Sha, MD, MS, of Stanford University in Palo Alto, California, at the Clinical Trials on Alzheimer's Disease conference in Boston.

In the overall study population, AR1001 showed a nonsignificant difference of -0.11 points versus placebo in Clinical Dementia Rating-Sum of Boxes (CDR-SB) scores at 52 weeks (P=0.4015). CDR-SB scores measure cognitive and functional impairment and can range from 0 to 18.

In a population of monotherapy patients -- those not on an acetylcholinesterase inhibitor or memantine at baseline -- AR1001 demonstrated a significant 52-week treatment difference of -0.44 points versus placebo (P=0.0266), representing a 31% slowing of decline.

Secondary endpoints of cognitive and functional measures trended in favor of AR1001 in both populations, with a nominally significant difference in Mini-Mental State Examination scores emerging in the monotherapy population.

The most frequent adverse events were diarrhea, falls, and dizziness. Adverse events led to discontinuation in 5.2% of patients in the AR1001 group and 1.7% in the placebo group.

Serious adverse events were similar in the AR1001 group (10.0%) and the placebo group (9.8%). Four deaths occurred in the AR1001 group (acute myeloid leukemia, cerebrovascular accident, suicide, and ruptured cerebral aneurysm) and three deaths occurred in the placebo group.

The CDR-SB data and safety profile are encouraging, Sha noted. "POLARIS-AD made its primary efficacy endpoint in the prespecified monotherapy population," she said. "The secondary endpoints had trends in favor of AR1001."

AR1001 is a brain-penetrant phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitor that enhances cyclic guanosine monophosphate (GMP) signaling. In preclinical Alzheimer's models, the treatment improved cognitive performance and reduced amyloid and tau burdens.

"AR1001 takes a novel approach, relaxing blood vessels to increase blood flow to the brain," noted Richard Oakley, PhD, of the Alzheimer's Society in England, who wasn't involved in the study.

"AR1001 is from the same family of medicines as Viagra [sildenafil], but it is being developed specifically for Alzheimer's disease. People should not take Viagra to try to treat Alzheimer's," Oakley said in a statement. "We look forward to seeing the full results from this trial and how this drug progresses."

In 2024, a study of 270,000 men in the U.K. linked erectile dysfunction drugs with a reduced risk of Alzheimer's disease. Those findings conflicted with data from the NIH DREAM initiative, but supported an earlier study of Medicare beneficiaries that suggested that sildenafil users were less likely to develop Alzheimer's than non-users.

The POLARIS-AD trial included 1,535 amyloid-positive Alzheimer's patients with mild cognitive impairment or mild dementia in the overall population; the monotherapy subgroup had 604 patients. The average patient age in the study was 73, and just over half were women. Participants were randomized to receive AR1001 30 mg or placebo once daily for 52 weeks.

The primary endpoint was the 52-week change in CDR-SB score in the overall and monotherapy populations. The primary efficacy objective required a significant treatment difference compared with placebo at week 52 in at least one population under the prespecified multiplicity procedure. The prespecified plan was suggested by AR1001's phase II findings, Sha pointed out.

Biomarker tests showed that AR1001 increased cyclic GMP levels in cerebrospinal fluid compared with placebo at week 52 in both the overall (P<0.0001) and monotherapy populations (P=0.0058), supporting target engagement, Sha said.

The presentation included no data about sex differences in treatment effects and Sha said the company has not yet run those analyses. "These results are hot off the press as of Friday," she noted, adding that "continuing analysis is happening, and the open-label extension is ongoing."

#5645. Waymo Abandons Highways Entirely After California Ban

SAN FRANCISCO, Nov 17 (Reuters) – Alphabet's Waymo has halted operations of its robotaxis on US freeways, it said on ​Tuesday, after a California regulator barred its vehicles from ‌freeways in the state.

The California ban, which took ​effect last week, is a result of a series of ​incidents where Waymo robotaxis entered active construction zones, the California Department of Motor Vehicles said in a statement on Tuesday.

"The Department determined that ​Waymo's driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public ​safety," it said.

According to parent company Eicher Motors' latest disclosures, Royal Enfield sold 842,538 motorcycles between April and October 2026, an 18% increase over the same period last year. That's an additional 125,684 motorcycles in just seven months, or roughly 18,000 extra bikes every month.

Note that this doesn't cover all the Sprinter vans you see on a daily basis, but just vehicles sold to individual buyers. If you live in your Sprinter, though, it counts.

#5643. $60 Million U.S. Military Plane No Match For Axe-Wielding Irishman

Some ledes in this business practically write themselves. An axe-wielding Irishman was charged Tuesday with destruction of government property after he allegedly broke into an airport and smashed the absolute stuffing out of a U.S. Air National Guard C-130 Hercules, destroying the plane outright in the process. The peace activist is alleged to have destroyed the plane over his anger over the U.S. support of Israel and the ongoing war with Iran.

The Department of Justice says Daithi O'Corrain spent hours smashing dials, snipping wire bundles, and cutting crucial hoses on the behemoth transport plane after breaking into Shannon Airport, located in southwest Ireland, back in April. O'Corrain supposedly really did a number on the plane. The unsealed indictment says O'Corrain poured ball bearings into the fuel tank, destroyed every single one of the delicate dials in the cockpit, and cut the plane's hydraulic lines. The damage was so extensive that the $60 million plane can not be repaired and will be scrapped.

"As alleged, Daithi O'Corrain carried out a deliberate, planned attack against a U.S. military aircraft, causing tens of millions of dollars in damage and jeopardizing critical military operations," said FBI Assistant Director in Charge James C. Barnacle, Jr. "This was not an act of spontaneous vandalism. The defendant allegedly spent months researching the aircraft, acquiring tools and materials, and preparing to maximize the destruction he could inflict. The FBI and our law enforcement partners will continue to pursue anyone who targets U.S. military assets, regardless of where those attacks occur."

O'Corrain is alleged to have snuck into the plane around 4:40 a.m. on April 11, 2026 after he bashed in the locking mechanism on the rear crew door. He then barricaded himself inside the aircraft, covering the windows with crew member hammocks. After disabling the interior doors with epoxy and ball-bearings, the axe-smashing began. O'Corrain put his whole heart into taking out this plane, to the point that he needed an offsite break before he was back at it the next morning, which is when he was caught.

Several hours after O'CORRAIN first entered the airport, the Aircraft's flight crew (the "Crew") returned to the Aircraft to depart for their next destination, observed O'CORRAIN through a cockpit window, and notified Shannon Airport security. Members of the Crew then observed O'CORRAIN escape out of a hatch in the Aircraft's roof and inflict substantial additional damage to the Aircraft's exterior, including by striking the exterior of the Aircraft with an axe, and pouring unknown substances and placing objects, including ball bearings, into the Aircraft's fuel tanks.

O'Corrain is currently being held without bail in a Limerick prison where he faces three charges: trespassing at Shannon airport, criminal damage to a fence, and criminal damage to a U.S. military plane. If convicted, he faces up to 10 years in prison.

O'Corrain is a long-time peace activist who opposes the U.S. war with Iran and the U.S.' support of Israel. He decided to take matters into his own hands, according to friends who set up a legal support fund for O'Corrain. According to the feds, O'Corrain spent weeks planning his assault on the U.S. Air National Guard C-130 Hercules, researching just how to hit it where it would hurt the most.

Ireland was the first European country to recognize Palestine as a state, and the last to grant Israel an embassy. In fact, it is one of the most pro-Palestine countries in the world, NPR reported in 2024:

A little over a century ago, Ireland and Palestine were both under British control. The latter was called British Mandate Palestine, or Mandatory Palestine. Many of the same British colonial officials served in both places — and left a deep impression on locals.

For example, former British Prime Minister Arthur Balfour was most famous for something he wrote after he left that office: The 1917 Balfour Declaration, which announced Britain's support for the establishment of a "national home for the Jewish people" in Palestine, a region previously ruled by the Ottomans.

But before his famous declaration, Balfour served in the 1880s as a British Cabinet secretary for Ireland. In 1885, he opposed home rule for Ireland, and his disagreement with colleagues over the issue ultimately helped to topple a previous British prime minister's government.

According to the indictment, O'Corrain's attack follows three other — less serious — incidents at Shannon airport in 2025.

#5642. Macrotroponins One Reason Not to Take cTnI Readings at Face Value

— High cTnI less of a red flag when macro-cTnI is also in the picture

Beware attempts to interpret cardiac troponin (cTn) as a cardiovascular risk marker without accounting for the presence of macrotroponins, researchers warned.

In a cohort study of community dwellers in Norway, high cardiac troponin I (cTnI; >10 ng/L) without any macro-cTnI was associated with a whopping 50.0% rate of incident cardiovascular events over a median follow-up of 8.3 years, according to a group led by Torbjørn Omland, MD, MPH, of University of Oslo and Akershus University Hospital in Lørenskog, Norway.

This was a significant jump in cardiovascular risk compared to those with high cTnI and prevalent macro-cTnI (6.8%; adjusted HR 8.5, 95% CI 3.1-23.6) or those whose cTnI readings were deemed too low to merit additional macro-cTnI testing (7.2% in the group with cTnI <5 ng/L; adjusted HR 6.1, 95% CI 3.2-11.6).

"The salient novel findings of the current population-based study are that macro-cTnI is highly prevalent in individuals in the general population with cTnI >10 ng/L by the widely used Abbott high-sensitivity assay and that macrotroponin presence may obscure the true association between cTnI and the incidence of cardiovascular events," Omland and colleagues reported in European Heart Journal.

Macrotroponins are high-molecular-weight complexes formed by cTn autoantibodies and circulating cTn molecules. These large complexes are slower to clear than cTn alone, which can cause assays to read higher.

With macrotroponin I apparently more prevalent than macrotroponin T, study authors suggested implications for interpreting cTnI results in real-world practice.

"Despite the consistent high prognostic value of cTnI demonstrated in numerous studies and individual participant meta-analyses, misclassification of risk based on high concentrations due to the presence of cTnI autoantibodies has been recognized as a potential problem. The current findings suggest that accounting for the presence of macro-cTnI may enhance the prognostic value of cTnI by reducing the extent of risk misclassification," they wrote.

Omland's team based the study on 3,703 community dwellers (median age 64 years, 49% women) participating in the Akershus Cardiac Examination 1950 Study.

Serum cTnI was initially analyzed using the ARCHITECT STAT high-sensitivity (hs) assay; the same samples were used to measure cTnT on a Cobas e801 using the STAT hs-troponin T assay. Investigators then sought samples where cTnI exceeded 10 ng/L (approximately corresponding to the study-specific 95th percentile) for subsequent detection of macro-cTnI.

Out of 108 people with such high cTnI, 81% met criteria for having macro-cTnI. Participants with original cTnI concentration <5 ng/L (n=3,256) or 5-10 ng/L (n=267) were the control groups.

The investigators found that people with high cTnI and prevalent macro-cTnI tended to have lower cTnT concentrations (9.0 vs 19.0 ng/L without macro-cTnI, P<0.001).

Beyond that, there were no significant between-group differences for age, sex, kidney function, C-reactive protein concentrations, use of cardioactive medications, prior cardiovascular disease, or risk factors at baseline.

The study's primary endpoint covered a composite of myocardial infarction, ischemic stroke, heart failure, coronary revascularization, and cardiovascular mortality.

Compared with people with cTnI levels under 5 ng/L, those with a high cTnI and macro-cTnI turned out not to be at significantly greater cardiovascular risk (7.2% vs 6.8%; adjusted HR 0.7, 95% CI 0.3-1.6).

Study authors cautioned that the study was limited by the complexity of the macrotroponin detection method used and the moderate number of participants with cTnI >10 ng/L, among other caveats.

"It remains unknown whether assay interference or delayed clearance of macrotroponin causes higher cTnI levels in those classified as macro-cTnI positive," Omland and colleagues added. "As such, these observations should be regarded as preliminary and hypothesis-generating, requiring validation in larger, more diverse, and higher-risk study populations."

#5641. These Are The First Changes Our Readers Would Make If Stellantis Made Them Its CEO

Talk to anyone who works for an automaker, and they'll tell you journalists make the worst product planners. Let's take a look at some of the most popular answers. What do you think? Will any of it work?

Reliability is the #1 priority. Buy some 90's Lexus/Toyota and Acura/Honda and ask "why is this 30+ year old car with 100/200/300+ thousand miles more reliable and better built then the vehicles we have coming off the assembly line today?" Follow that up with more fuel-efficient vehicles for the next gen. Fuel isn't here forever, don't be caught with your pants down, stop ignoring it. STOP putting so much R&D into d**k measuring with constant dumb horsepower figures and put some effort into delivering a quality long lasting product that doesn't scream "I financed this car for 96 months with a 580 credit score and I'm on the verge of first payment default. And most of all, bring back a halo car, they need a new Viper.

You want high horsepower and performance? Let's do it right with a little civilized engineering. Bring back a proper Lancia Integrale. Let's go rallying again and show the world who still has the unbeaten records in titles.

Let Abarth breathe its magic on other Fiats. And with all of those EV offerings in Europe, bring them here.

OK, as much as Hemi fans want not want to hear it, after the Trump admin leaves through election or prison, fuel economy and emissions standards are back on the table. But Dodge still needs to be performance based. Now, looking it up, here is the vastly bloated list of Stellantis brands:

Abarth, Alfa Romeo, Chrysler, Citroen, Dodge, DS Automobiles, Fiat, Jeep, Lancia, Maserati, Opel, Peugeot, Ram Trucks, Vauxhall

In Europe, there is a TON of overlap in those brands. The "mass produced/non-performance" brands must be pared down. Citroen, Fiat, Lancia, Opel, Peugeot, and Vauxhall – they need to be combined to 2-3 brands at the most. So, let's keep one French (Peugeot), one Italian (Fiat), and one British (Vauxhall). And before the comments fly, Fiat still sells well in Europe, and they have a mix of EVs and fuel efficient small cars that help with emissions requirements.

In America, good bye Chrysler. It's been a long run, but the ONLY Chrysler minivans I see on a regular basis have FLEET license plates and bar codes on windows. Merge Ram Truck back into Dodge. It's amazing how many people still call these trucks as Dodge Rams. Correct the mistake and save the money trying to keep an unneeded brand alive. With Dodge, keep the Hemi going for one more generation, but start working on hybrid versions right now as that turbo-6 is going to be the standard engine very soon.

And from what I saw, bring some Peugeots over to the US as Dodges. Some are very attractive.

Go spy on Toyota and figure out how to make a vehicle with decent build quality.

electric PT Cruiser! call it a Pronto EV or whatever, but give it 5 seats, a low and flat load floor, upright van-like styling and big windows (not necessarily retro but PTs don't look bad these days anyway). think chevy Bolt, but a bit more voluminous and easy-to-load like a Fit. customization would serve it well, particularly if it's aimed at Slate or Maverick/Fathom. It should not look fast, because we are done with that.

I always had a vision for Chrysler. I think they need to serve as Stellantis's Cadillac:

Bring back the Chrysler 300 using the Charger EV, start it at $50K, less focus on power, more comfort and agility.

Bring back the 300 SRT with a hybrid V8, power power power, start it at $80K.

Bring back the Chrysler 200 as an EV coupe/cabriolet, $35K to start

If you want people to take on EVs, you need to make them accessible as possible. As for styling, I'm thinking Italian Mob Boss, some Kings of New York type shiiiet at least for the 300, the 200 can be a more sleeker design. Also I'm de-Kiafying the Pacifica, and I guess I'll add some mid sized SUV because of course we would need one 🙄.

Build a better Corolla and strip all tech out of the base model. Even offer something with manual locks, mirrors, and windows with a 5 speed manual. No CVT, no direct injection, no turbo, etc. Physical buttons for all controls and engineered to go 150K miles with abuse and neglect.

Also build another Chrysler 300 midsize RWD car that is reasonable in pricing like the original.

Do that with success and you can build fund stuff like in the 90s with Prowler, Viper, and Japanese made Stealth. It is amazing what they did in the 90s by having such a surge in popularity after the dark ages of the 80s. They sold cars like the Neon, could cars, and LH platform like crazy.

Fix the charger. Divide it into four properly engineered cars:

Dodge Challenger: engineered from the ground up to be a two door ICE muscle car. Shorten the wheel base, take about 400 to 600 pounds out, lower the belting, do something about that caboose, give it more aggressive (or at least less boring styling).

Dodge Charger: this is your four door ICE car for Challenger drivers to grow into when they start a family. Keep the longer wheel base, keep the big trunk, take out 400 to 600 pounds, lower the beltline.

Dodge GTO: this is your two door electric muscle car. You're the only two door EV on the market. Fix everything that's wrong with tbe current EV Chsrger and make it irresistible.

Dodge Coronet: this is your four door EV muscle car for Dodge GTO drivers to grow into when they start a family.

#5640. BYD E6Y Odyssey

A couple of friends embark on a road trip to pick their EV purchase, and it turns into an Odyssey.

The BYD E6 is a BEV classic. Globe-trotting friends have ridden in them as taxis in Singapore. Majella and I even took one for a test drive (it reminded me of a ’70s Chrysler Valiant). It was the best selling pure electric car in China in 2016, with about 600,000 sold globally, and it is viewed by many as ahead of its time. So, when one showed up at our coffee and cake morning tea, I had to interview the owner, Joanne, and her road trip friend, Meredith. What they shared with me was truly an odyssey of determination to get Jo’s car home. Please note: this story took place 6 years ago. Charging availability has improved and so has Joanne’s knowledge of living with an electric vehicle.

Joanne told me that she had been looking for an EV for two years when a search on carsales.com found an ad for an E6Y (the taxi version of the BYD E6). It was a pre-COVID import and had been mothballed at Sydney airport after the company importing the vehicles had decided not to go ahead with taxis. Photos of all the BYDs at the airport were widely circulated as evidence that no one wanted to buy an EV. The cars were being “sold by a mate out of the back of the factory.” At the low cost of AU$32,000, claiming a 400 km range from its 83-kWh battery and only 100 km on the odometer, Joanne saw it as a bargain.

There was some consideration of putting the car on a flatbed and having it shipped, but in the end, the two friends decided on a road trip.

Jo informs me that: “They only brought 120 of the BYD E6Y taxi versions into the country. BYD sold some GLX versions, which had more bells and whistles.”

She was soon to find out that all is not as it seems. Her lack of knowledge of EVs meant that her trip in the car from Sydney back to Brisbane would be an odyssey of Greek tragedy proportions. Jo reminisces: “Meredith reminded me our original plan had been to spend just two days driving back to Brisbane with the one overnight stay. I thought I could ‘fast charge’ the car and make it back to Brisvegas on the second day. Hahahaha. Lesson learned the hard way.” They expected it to be just a twelve-hour trip. Meredith adds, “I told her we would laugh about it one day.”

Joanne contacted the seller and planned her flight to Sydney. Meredith got excited and joined the “road trip” in Newcastle. Jo paid the money and took ownership of the car. “It took two hours to get out of Sydney.” To be fair, that wasn’t the car’s fault — just Sydney’s congested traffic. However, the fact that the previous owner had only charged the car to 50% didn’t help. She was able to charge the car to 80% on the outskirts of Sydney. “But I had no idea that I wouldn’t get the expected range at highway speeds.”

Another learning curve was the state of charging points — sure, there were plenty listed on PlugShare, but many were unavailable or broken. Again, dear reader, I must emphasize this was 6 years ago.

Her next planned stop was accommodation she had booked at Newcastle. She got to within 2 km when the battery readout said “zero.” She was unable to find a place to charge — the car had not come with a granny charger. Jo clarified her Newcastle experience by email: “the Uni in Newcastle was the first I tried to charge. My friend Meredith and I tried three other places to charge all with no luck. I found out later that the Newcastle chargers were notorious for not working.” She confided to me that she now knows that the car can go a little further even when it says zero battery. She also had to learn the difference between AC and DC charging. The BYD she had bought could only handle AC charging.

With the battery reading zero, she felt she had no choice but to leave the car at a nearby garage. The owner of the accommodation she had booked kindly came and picked her and her friend up. The next day, the BNB owner dropped them back to the garage and a tow truck was ordered. The tow truck took them and their car to a local winery where they trickle charged next to the golf carts. The winery also had a Tesla level 2 charger for customer use. It was charging when they arrived. “We left a note on the windscreen of the Tesla that was using the level 2. During breakfast the Tesla owner rang and said the charger was available. We left the winery fully charged and off up the highway.” Not a bad place to get stuck, methinks.

280 km up the road to Kempsie, there was access to free charging in the council carpark, but no available accommodation! That’s what happens when you travel on the Queen’s Birthday long weekend. “We were checking out the back seat of the car as a place to sleep when a friend of a friend told them he had a farm in area. ‘Have I got a place for you!’ he told them. After we finished charging, we had to drive down this dark farm track. We couldn’t plug the car in because we still didn’t have a granny charger!” They made themselves comfortable in the available beds in the barn. The next morning in the light of day they found they had driven alongside of a lagoon! “We could have easily driven into that!”

Meredith adds: “I didn’t think I’d be sleeping in a shed with a tractor and then I couldn’t sleep because of the smell of diesel.” The trip was not just a challenge, but torture.

Their next adventure was just a few kilometers up the road at Gladstone NSW. Low on juice, they contacted people through PlugShare and got a message from a local café owner. You can check out the café here. He already owned a Hyundai Ioniq, and so had a granny charger to lend. The café owner knew a fellow who was visiting his mum in town. This friend had ordered an Atto 3 from BYD and was waiting on this first shipment of BYDs to arrive. Jo took him for a ride around town in her car. “This was his first ride in a BYD.”

He arranged for them to leave their car at his mother’s home in the next town (Smithtown). Jo and her friend were now in a hurry, as both had to get back to Brisbane to go to work the next day. Leaving the car charging, “We belted back to Brisbane in this horrible little roller skate hire car.” Home in Brisbane, Jo had to plan how to retrieve her BYD purchase. Meredith did not want another “road trip.”

This time, Jo travelled south by train. Even this trip was not without drama. She missed her stop. Thankfully, she was able to get off at the next stop and change to the northbound train to make it back to where her car waited faithfully for her. She took a taxi to her car, returned the borrowed granny charger, and began her journey home. She was so keen that she ended up with a speeding ticket.

Joanne arrived at her motel in Balina with 10 km of range showing on the dashboard. But the available ocular charger soon filled up the car. By 9:00 pm, with a full battery, she couldn’t wait to go home. The fireworks display in town was a fitting sendoff for the final leg of her journey.

Jo would like to acknowledge all the good advice and offers of help she received from fellow users of PlugShare, as well as all those referred to above. She is now a confident and competent driver of her BYD E6Y and has made many trips back and forth to Sydney from Brisbane with less drama. The BYD E6Y Facebook group has been a great help. She says she would not part with her green E6Y Singapore taxi — “a comfortable and solid basic car.” Rumour has it that there are about 250 on Australian roads, with the record distance travelled over 1 million km.

Another owner commented: “This unstoppable tank will outlive me I’m sure, best of all its super basic … no internet, no lane assist, no self-driving and plenty of new and used cheap parts available. This is why it seems like there are so few … they are so good NO ONE who has one is selling them.”

Jo has even taken her car camping. “The camping trip was to Yandina up the coast. I did sleep in the back of the car on that trip very comfortably. But that time I had a mattress and bedding. I did take out the bottom half of the back seat from the car so I could get a completely flat rear area for the mattress. When I returned from the camping trip, I popped the rear seat bottom back in the car. It’s just two bolts and a couple of plastic clips that hold it in.”

And in the future, I asked? “It will be hard to part with my E6. I am waiting on the first solid state 1000km BEVs before I would consider parting with it. I just love the two-tone paint job.”

Jo is another happy battery electric vehicle owner, sharing her learning experience “odyssey” and having fun. “Thanks for the opportunity to immortalise the odyssey,” she told me in her latest email. The future is bright and electric as we learn to live with our new vehicles.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

David Waterworth has 1012 posts and counting. See all posts by David Waterworth

#5639. White House Yanks Pick for FDA Chief

— Overton faced little chance of winning a full Senate vote on chamber floor

The White House said Tuesday it has pulled the nomination of Heidi Overton, MD, PhD, to lead the FDA amid growing indications she didn't have the votes needed to win Senate confirmation.

Overton, a physician and White House domestic policy advisor, has spent most of her professional career working directly for Trump or conservative groups aligned with his agenda. Her nomination in August was seen as a move to bring a stabilizing Trump loyalist to the FDA after a tumultuous year under Trump's first FDA commissioner, Marty Makary, MD, MPH.

But Overton faced bipartisan questions in the Senate about her willingness to break from Trump, particularly on his unsupported claims about the dangers of childhood vaccines.

Sen. Bill Cassidy, MD (R-La.), who chairs the Senate health committee, said earlier in the day he remained undecided about Overton ahead of a critical vote on her nomination scheduled for Wednesday.

Cassidy had previously called Overton's support for Trump's plan to break up combination vaccines -- including the measles, mumps, and rubella shot -- "almost disqualifying." And he pressed her during a September hearing about her scientific independence and willingness to push back against Trump and Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., a longtime vaccine critic.

But late Tuesday night, Cassidy suggested he had been prepared to back Overton and suggested her nomination was derailed by disagreements over abortion, not vaccines.

"I believe she would have had the votes to pass out of committee and pass the full Senate," Cassidy stated in a posting on X.

He went on to say that anti-abortion Republicans had sought commitments on "life issues" from the White House, but "the Administration withdrew Dr. Overton rather than make such commitments."

Currently, the FDA is conducting a safety review of the abortion pill mifepristone -- at the request of Trump -- that could reinstate restrictions on the drug's use, including a requirement for in-person prescribing. FDA has promised a report on its safety findings by March.

To win confirmation, Overton would have needed the votes of all 12 Republicans on the Senate Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions Committee. Democrats hold 11 seats. Without support of the committee, Overton faced little chance of winning a full Senate vote on the chamber floor.

Republicans are racing to try to confirm all of Trump's current nominees before they hand over power to Senate Democrats in January.

Cassidy Has Previously Threatened to Oppose Health Nominees

Cassidy has repeatedly tried to win commitments on upholding vaccine standards, including from Kennedy, by threatening to withhold his vote. Ultimately, though, he voted in favor of each nominee that came before his committee. With only a matter of weeks left in his term, Cassidy faced little downside in opposing the latest FDA nominee. He lost a primary race in June to run for a third term.

Opposition to Overton's nomination also came from several influential figures in the Make America Healthy Again movement, who blamed her and other White House officials for sidelining some of Kennedy's top health goals.

Earlier this month, Calley Means told Joe Rogan that White House aides, including Overton, worked within the administration to advance a Supreme Court decision blocking thousands of lawsuits over the weedkiller glyphosate. Means said that policy and other key decisions were carried out at the behest of industry lobbyists.

"There is a very concentrated group in the Trump administration that are antagonistic to what people voted for," Means said.

Trump Has Pulled Back Several Other Health Nominations

The White House has previously pulled several other health nominations. Trump's first nominee for the CDC, David Weldon, MD, was replaced because it was unclear he had enough Republican support to be confirmed. Two of Trump's surgeon general nominees, including Casey Means, MD -- Calley Means' sister -- were also pulled after facing pushback from Senate Republicans.

The FDA is currently being led on acting basis by Kyle Diamantas, an attorney and friend of Donald Trump Jr. He was elevated to the post by the president after previously serving as the agency's top food official.

The FDA under Makary was marked by a string of scientific disputes, leadership changes, and media stories detailing low morale and internal dysfunction among staffers. The FDA has lost about 20% of its employees to layoffs, buyouts, retirements, or resignations since Trump took office.

#5638. Lawmakers Pressing Mercedes-Benz On China Ties Ahead Of Proposed Vehicle Ban

Republicans and Democrats on Capitol Hill don't agree on much, but for one reason or another, they're all pretty much aligned on the idea that we cannot allow Chinese-made vehicles to enter the United States, and that's causing quite the headache for Mercedes-Benz. Top lawmakers on both sides of the aisle on the House Select Committee on China have asked the mostly German automaker to answer detailed questions about its ties to China and some of its technology partnerships as Congress looks to put a stop to China-owned automakers from selling vehicles here — not that they really were in the first place.

Representative John Moolenaar, the Republican chair and Representative Ro Khanna, the top Democrat on the panel (see what I mean about bipartisan), said that the fact that roughly 20% of Mercedes-Benz was owned by various Chinese investors presents significant risks, according to Reuters. In a letter reviewed by the outlet, the two said that the ownership concern is compounded both "by the specific nature of the shareholders" and the "operational influence they appear to exert" over Mercedes-Benz. To be honest, I think if Mercedes was actually being influenced by China, its sales would probably be better there.

Mercedes, for its part, said it will cooperate. In a statement, the automaker added that it operates independently under the direction of its board of management. Here's what it told Reuters:

"Our shareholders have no management board seats and no management authority," the automaker said, adding ​the company "has long supported measures designed to protect U.S. national security, and any suggestion that the company opposes those objectives is incorrect."

The committee wants answers to its two dozen questions from Mercedes by December 1. It's also asking for the company to provide supporting documents and to meet with Mercedes-Benz North American CEO Jason Hoff.

This latest tidbit comes as Congress sets the stage to enshrine an even tougher ban on Chinese vehicles already virtually banned. Lawmakers say that Mercedes' answers would help Congress as it considers legislation "to mitigate the profound threat" posed by Chinese vehicles in the U.S. market. Of course, they don't seem bothered by all of the other Chinese tech products we've already got over here. Nope. Not at all.

To me, a much more profound threat to the U.S. would be a company like Mercedes-Benz, which employs about 10,000 folks in the U.S. directly and supports a further 107,000 between suppliers and service providers, suddenly not being able to do business here anymore because of ill-conceived legislation. That legislation, which as we previously covered back in July when it was approved by the Senate Commerce Committee, would ban companies with more than 15% ownership by Chinese entities from selling vehicles in the U.S. Unfortunately for Mercedes-Benz, that's the situation it finds itself in, as state-owned Chinese automaker BAIC owns about 10%, as does Geely chair Li Shufu.

Some seem to understand the implications of nuking a company like Mercedes from the U.S. market, and perhaps that's why Republican Senator Bernie Moreno (one of the bill's architects) said that the final legislation will ensure that Mercedes isn't banned from the U.S., according to Reuters. He added that there were similar discussions about how Volvo, which is majority-owned by Geely, could continue to sell vehicles in the U.S. Unfortunately, its sister company, Polestar, will not get the same grace period for whatever reason.

At the end of the day, this is all rather silly. Regulation imposed by the Biden administration in early 2025 effectively banned all Chinese automakers from selling passenger vehicles in the U.S., arguing that they could send sensitive drive data to China (as if home-grown automakers aren't taking our data already), and there is a 100% tariff on Chinese EVs that enter the U.S.

Congress and the lobbyists that automakers pay handsomely can take it easy; Chinese cars aren't coming stateside anytime soon.

#5637. Senate Republicans Seek to Again Raid States’ Coffers & Threaten EV Charging

FY 2027 Transportation Proposal Puts EV Charging Investments at Risk

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Monday, Senate Republicans released an Appropriations package that proposes to take back nearly $2 billion in federal funding already allocated to states to build out electric vehicle (EV) charging infrastructure under the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act and repurpose it for general transportation uses, putting critical clean transportation investments at risk.

Republicans already clawed back roughly $500 million from the National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) Formula Program in the FY 2026 appropriations bill. Now, they are proposing to further divert these funds, even as all 50 states, Washington, D.C., and Puerto Rico have approved plans for their use and are working to deliver critical charging infrastructure to their communities. More than half of available NEVI funding has already been committed to projects, and the proposal threatens to upend additional awards that states continue to make to charging developers.

For example, Texas recently made an announcement of $226 million in awards that will support 321 stations. The Senate proposal would threaten Texas’s remaining NEVI funding, along with funding for states like Ohio, which has one of the largest numbers of operational NEVI-funded stations in the country.

In response, Clean Transportation for All Director Katherine García released the following statement:

“It is unacceptable for Senate Republicans to once again seek to claw back critical EV charging investments. Trump’s day-one freeze of NEVI set states back, and they are only now back on track after we successfully challenged that freeze in court. This latest proposal threatens affordable transportation for American families, clean air for communities, and states’ efforts to build the infrastructure of the future. We have successfully defended NEVI against the Trump administration’s attacks, and we will continue fighting to protect the program and ensure these investments reach communities across the country.”

The NEVI program, established through the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law in 2021, provides funding to states to build a nationwide network of EV fast-charging stations. Protecting that investment is essential to expanding charging access, supporting EV adoption, reducing dependence on volatile gasoline prices, and cutting transportation pollution.

Since the Trump administration’s unlawful freeze of the NEVI program was lifted following successful litigation by Sierra Club, its partners, and a coalition of states, hundreds of millions of dollars in NEVI funding have been committed to charging projects.

Congress must preserve funding for EV charging infrastructure while providing dedicated resources for passenger rail, public transit, and other clean transportation priorities.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5636. Zoox Partnering with Uber to Bring Autonomous Mobility to More Riders

Now live in Las Vegas, with Los Angeles to follow

In March 2026, we announced a multi-year partnership between Zoox and Uber to bring our robotaxi experience to Uber’s growing autonomy platform in select cities.

Now, beginning today, Uber riders in Las Vegas will have the opportunity to be matched with a Zoox robotaxi on eligible trips. Zoox will also continue to offer rides through the Zoox app. In addition, Zoox and Uber are the official robotaxi and rideshare partners of MGM Resorts’ Las Vegas properties. Together, they’re working with MGM Resorts to make pickups and drop-offs easier and more intuitive, with improved on-property wayfinding, in-app guidance, and infrastructure improvements designed to support smoother traffic flow.

The Zoox purpose-built robotaxi gives riders the agency to create a space all their own, with design elements for enhanced socialization and safety not possible in a traditional car. Bringing this experience to Uber will help introduce the benefits of purpose-built autonomy to new, curious riders.

Within the Zoox and Uber apps, riders can take a Zoox to popular resorts and venues on and around the Strip, as well as rides in the Downtown & Arts District. More destinations will be added soon.

We will continue to offer our service through the Zoox app, in addition to rides being available through Uber in those cities.

Zoox was founded in 2014 on a bold belief: if you’re going to reinvent mobility, you have to start from scratch. Instead of retrofitting cars with autonomous technology, we designed the world’s first purpose-built robotaxi: fully autonomous, all-electric, bidirectional, and intentionally engineered around the rider experience. Since the very beginning, we’ve been focused on our vision of delivering safe and enjoyable mobility.

Like Zoox, Uber was founded in San Francisco with a vision to improve urban transportation. Uber started in 2010 as a ridehail app connecting riders with cars, but quickly evolved. Over the past decade, it expanded into shared rides, food delivery, freight logistics, micromobility, and is now building an autonomous vehicle network, consistently redefining how people and goods move through the world. By leveraging smartphone technology, dynamic pricing, and real-time data, Uber helped catalyze the on-demand economy and reshape how people get from A to B on a global scale. This partnership is a testament to Uber’s dedication to advancing transportation within the ridehail space.

Zoox has always taken a measured, step-by-step approach by starting small, learning quickly, and scaling responsibly. This launch with Uber mirrors that approach. We’re beginning with a small fleet with the potential to expand as we refine our operations, technology, and customer experience.

Uber and Zoox will launch in Los Angeles next year, after Zoox opens service on the Zoox app.

#5635. Why Doesn't GTA 6 Have Licensed Vehicles Despite Rockstar's Immense Wealth?

Grand Theft Auto, as you might imagine, has plenty of extremely cool cars. Grand Theft Auto VI brings this focus on its titular Autos even further, with a genuine race track and all the associated automotive accoutrements, but one series trend remains stalwart: No matter how close GTA's cars come to their real-life inspirations, Rockstar Games still won't place real logos or brand names on them. Why not?

Rockstar cofounder Jamie King has two reasons: Cost and creativity. For the former, GTA VI isn't even out yet and per GTABase, fans have already catalogued 317 unique vehicles — Rockstar has plenty of money, sure, but individually licensing each of those makes and models would still take serious cash that could be better used on other aspects of development. For the latter, not slavishly matching existing vehicles allows Rockstar freedom in designing and engineering the cars to better fit a game world. Cars don't need to match their real-world suspension design, drivetrains, or numbers of doors, and can instead tweak those to be better suited for what players need in-game.

There's another reason, though, that King didn't mention: Automakers are picky about how their cars are used in games. Grand Theft Auto is as much about the Grand Theft as the Autos, and carmakers often limit how much their cars can be damaged in-game — "GTA" players would be disappointed if they shot up a car and found it immune to bullet holes, explosions, and fire. By avoiding real-world branded vehicles, and the associated rules automakers place on their intellectual property, Rockstar can build more verisimilitude within its games.

Rockstar owns up to cost and creative control as reasons it doesn't license cars for Grand Theft Auto, but freedom to do what it wants with the vehicles in its games is likely just as big a reason. So when you spawn into Leonida tomorrow, and start to wonder why Jason and Lucia's obvious Ford Ranchero is branded as a Vapid Ganado, ask yourself how much worse you'd be willing for GTA VI to be in order to have a Ford badge on that grille.
